->*Introduction regarding the compilers of the books of the Old Testament.*<-

During this period, the book of the Law was lost until the eighteenth year of the reign of his grandson, Josiah. <margin>2 Chronicles 34.</margin> At that time, Hilkiah the High Priest found the book in the Temple while it was being repaired. After it was found, the King lamented that their fathers had not acted according to the words of the book,

# Chap. 1. Prophecies of Daniel.

...a new covenant to seek the Lord God of their fathers, under penalty of death for anyone who worshipped other gods; and his son Jehoshaphat <note>King of Judah, son of Asa.</note> removed the high places <note>Local shrines used for sacrifices, often associated with unauthorized or pagan worship.</note> and, in the third year of his reign, sent some of his princes, priests, and Levites to teach in the cities of Judah. They carried the Book of the Law with them and traveled throughout all the cities of Judah teaching the people. This is the same Book of the Law that was later lost during the reign of Manasseh <note>A king of Judah known for his idolatry, during whose reign the temple was neglected.</note> and found again during the reign of Josiah; therefore, it must have been written before the third year of Jehoshaphat.

The same Book of the Law was preserved and passed down to posterity by the Samaritans, and therefore it must have been received by the ten tribes <note>The Northern Kingdom of Israel, distinct from the Southern Kingdom of Judah.</note> before their captivity. <margin>2 Kings 17:27, 28, 32, 33.</margin> For when the ten tribes were taken captive, a priest from the exile was sent back to Bethel by order of the King of Assyria to instruct the new inhabitants of Samaria in the customs <note>original: "manner"</note> of the "God of the land." The Samaritans received the Pentateuch <note>The first five books of the Bible, also known as the Torah.</note> from this priest, as it contained the law or requirements of the God of the land which he was to teach them. <margin>2 Kings 17:34, 41</margin> They persisted in the religion he taught them while also continuing to worship their own gods; and by adhering to what they had been taught, they preserved this Book of the Law in the original Hebrew script <note>original: "character of the Hebrews"; referring to the Paleo-Hebrew alphabet used before the Babylonian exile.</note>, while the two

<meta>The text continues on the next page with the history of the remaining two tribes of the Southern Kingdom.</meta>

<summary>Newton argues that the Pentateuch must predate King Jehoshaphat's reign and explains how the Samaritans preserved the Book of the Law in its original Hebrew script after the Northern Kingdom's exile.</summary>
<keywords>Jehoshaphat, Book of the Law, Samaritans, Pentateuch, King of Assyria, Manasseh, Josiah, Hebrew script, 2 Kings</keywords>

# Part I. Observations upon the Prophecies of Daniel.

The tribes, after their return from Babylon, changed their script <note>original: "character"</note> to that of the Chaldeans <note>The Aramaic script learned by the Jews during their captivity in Babylon.</note>, which they had learned while in Babylon.

And since the Pentateuch <note>The first five books of the Bible: Genesis, Exodus, Leviticus, Numbers, and Deuteronomy.</note> was accepted as the Book of the Law by both the two tribes of Judah and the ten tribes of Israel, it follows that they received it before they were divided into two kingdoms. After the division, they did not accept laws from one another but remained in a state of conflict. Judah could not reclaim Israel from the sin of Jeroboam <note>Jeroboam I, who led the northern tribes to worship golden calves at Dan and Bethel.</note>, and Israel could not persuade Judah to join them in it. Therefore, the Pentateuch was already the Book of the Law in the days of David and Solomon. David and Solomon organized the affairs of the Tabernacle and the Temple according to the law found in this book; and when David, in the 78th Psalm, urges the people to listen to the Law of God, he is referring to the Law of this specific book. In describing how their ancestors failed to keep it, he quotes many historical events from the books of Exodus and Numbers.

<margin>Genesis 36:31.</margin>
The lineage of the kings of Edom who ruled "before any king reigned over the children of Israel" is recorded in the book of Genesis. Therefore, that book was not written entirely in its current form before the reign of Saul <note>The first king of Israel; the author suggests the text must have been finalized when Israel finally had its own kings.</note>. The author recorded the line of those kings up until his own time, and therefore wrote before David conquered Edom. The Pentateuch is composed of the Law and the history—

## Chap. 1. Prophecies of Daniel.

The history of God's people has been collected from several books, such as the history of the Creation composed by **Moses** (*Genesis* 2:4), the book of the generations of **Adam** (*Genesis* 5:1), and the *Book of the Wars of the Lord* (*Numbers* 21:14). This book of wars contained an account of what was done at the **Red Sea** and during the journeying of **Israel** through the wilderness, and therefore it was begun by **Moses**. **Joshua** might have continued it up to the conquest of **Canaan**, for **Joshua** wrote certain things in the *Book of the Law of God* (*Joshua* 24:26) and therefore might have recorded his own wars in the *Book of Wars*, as those were the principal wars of God. These were public books, and therefore were not written without the authority of **Moses** and **Joshua**.

**Samuel** had the leisure during the reign of **Saul** to organize these records into the form of the books of **Moses** and **Joshua** that are now extant <note>original: "extant"; meaning currently existing or surviving.</note>, inserting into the book of **Genesis** the lineage of the Kings of **Edom** "until there reigned a king in **Israel**" <note>This phrase indicates that the text was finalized after the Israelite monarchy had begun.</note>. The book of **Judges** is a continued history of the judges down to the death of **Samson** <note>original: "Sampson"</note>, and therefore it was compiled after his death from the *Acts of the Judges*. Several things in this book are said to be done *when there was no king in Israel* (*Judges* 17:6, 18:1, 19:1, 21:25), and therefore this book was written after the beginning of the reign of **Saul**. The books of **Moses**, **Joshua**, and **Judges** contain one continuous history from the Creation down to the death of **Samson** <note>original: "Sampson"</note>. Where the **Pentateuch** <note>The first five books of the Bible: Genesis, Exodus, Leviticus, Numbers, and Deuteronomy.</note> ends, the book of **Joshua** begins; and where the book of **Joshua** ends, the book of **Judges** begins. Therefore, all these books were composed from the writings of **Moses**, **Joshua**, and other records by a single person <note>original: "one and the same hand"</note> after the beginning of the reign of **Saul** and before the eighth year of **David**.

**Samuel** was a sacred writer (*1 Samuel* 10:25) who was acquainted with the history of **Moses** and the **Judges** (*1 Samuel* 12:8–12). He had the leisure during the reign of **Saul** and sufficient authority to compose these books. He was a prophet and judged **Israel** all the days of his life, and he was held in the highest esteem by the people. The Law by which he was to judge the people would not have been published by anyone with less authority than his own, as the lawmaker is not inferior to the judge. Furthermore, the **Book of Jasher** <note>A lost non-canonical book mentioned in the Hebrew Bible.</note>, which is quoted in the book of **Joshua** (*Joshua* 10:13), was in existence <note>original: "in being"</note> at the time of **Saul**'s death (*2 Samuel* 1:18).

This and other losses during the desolation of **Israel** by the conquering **Philistines** might have given **Samuel** the opportunity—after some relief from those enemies—to collect the scattered writings of **Moses** and **Joshua**, and the records of the **Patriarchs** and **Judges**, and arrange them into the form we have today <note>original: "now extant"</note>.

The book of **Ruth** is a history of events that occurred in the days of the **Judges**, and it may be viewed as an addition to the book of **Judges**, written by the same author at the same time. For it was written after the birth of **David**, <margin>Ruth 4:17, 22</margin> and not long after, because the history of **Boaz** and **Ruth**—the great-grandfather and great-grandmother of **David**—and their contemporaries could not have been remembered clearly for more than two or three generations. And since this book traces the genealogy of **David** from **Boaz** and **Ruth**, but omits **David**’s elder brothers and his sons, it must have been written in honor of **David** after he was anointed King by **Samuel**, but before he had children in **Hebron**. Consequently, it was written during the reign of **Saul**. It does not proceed to the history of **David** himself, and therefore seems to have been written shortly <note>original: "presently"</note> after he was anointed.

# Observations upon the Part I.

Those who attribute the books of **Joshua**, **Judges**, and **Ruth** to **Samuel** therefore judge correctly.

**Samuel** is also considered the author of the first book of **Samuel** up until the time of his death. The two books of **Samuel** do not cite other authors, and therefore they appear to be original works. They begin with his genealogy, birth, and education, and may have been written partly during his lifetime by himself or his disciples—the Prophets at **Naioth** in **Ramah** (1 Samuel 19:18–20)—and partly after his death by those same disciples.

The books of the **Kings** cite other authors, such as the *Book of the Acts of Solomon*, the *Book of the Chronicles of the Kings of Israel*, and the *Book of the Chronicles of the Kings of Judah*. The books of the **Chronicles** cite the *Book of Samuel the Seer*, the *Book of Nathan the Prophet*, and the *Book of Gad the Seer* for the deeds <note>original: "Acts"</note> of **David**; the *Book of Nathan the Prophet*, the *Prophecy of Ahijah the Shilonite*, and the *Visions of Iddo the Seer* for the deeds of **Solomon**; the *Book of Shemaiah the Prophet* and the *Book of Iddo the Seer* concerning genealogies for the deeds of **Rehoboam** and **Abijah**; the *Book of the Kings of Judah and Israel* for the deeds of **Asa**, **Joash**, **Amaziah**, **Jotham**, **Ahaz**, **Hezekiah**, **Manasseh**, and **Josiah**; the *Book of Hanani the Seer* for the deeds of **Jehoshaphat**; and the *Visions of Isaiah* for the deeds of **Uzziah** and **Hezekiah**.

->Observations upon the Part I.<-

Those who attribute the books of *Joshua*, *Judges*, and *Ruth* to *Samuel* judge correctly.

Samuel is also considered the author of the first book of *Samuel* up until the time of his death. Because the two books of *Samuel* do not cite other authors, they appear to be original works. They begin with his genealogy, birth, and education, and might have been written partly during his lifetime by himself or by his disciples (the Prophets at *Naioth* in *Ramah*; see 1 *Samuel* 19:18–20), and partly after his death by those same disciples.

The books of *Kings* cite other sources, such as the *Book of the Acts of Solomon*, the *Book of the Chronicles of the Kings of Israel*, and the *Book of the Chronicles of the Kings of Judah*. Similarly, the books of *Chronicles* cite the book of *Samuel* the Seer, the book of *Nathan* the Prophet, and the book of *Gad* the Seer for the history <term>Acts</term> <note>original: "Acts"; here referring to the deeds, achievements, or recorded history of a person’s reign</note> of *David*. For the history of *Solomon*, they cite the book of *Nathan* the Prophet, the prophecy of *Ahijah* the *Shilonite*, and the visions of *Iddo* the Seer. For the history of *Rehoboam* and *Abijah*, they refer to the book of *Shemaiah* <note>original: "Shemajah"</note> the Prophet and the book of *Iddo* the Seer concerning genealogies. For the history of Kings *Asa*, *Joash*, *Amaziah*, *Jotham*, *Ahaz*, *Hezekiah*, *Manasseh*, and *Josiah*, they cite the *Book of the Kings of Judah and Israel*. Finally, they cite the book of *Hanani* the Seer for the history of *Jehoshaphat* <note>original: "Jehosaphat"</note>, and the visions of *Isaiah* for the history of *Uzziah* and *Hezekiah*.

<meta>This page continues Isaac Newton’s argument that Ezra was the primary compiler of the Old Testament. ->Observations upon the Prophecies of Daniel. Part I.<-

[Ezra] joined those records <note>original: "Acts"</note> together in chronological order <note>original: "due order of time"</note>, copying the very words of the authors. This is evident from the fact that the books of *Kings* and *Chronicles* frequently agree with one another word-for-word for many sentences together. Where they agree in meaning, they also agree in their specific wording.

In the same way, he collected the prophecies of *Isaiah*, which were written at various times, into a single volume <note>original: "one body"</note>. He did the same for the prophecies of *Jeremiah* and the rest of the Prophets down to the days of the Second Temple. The book of *Jonah* is a history of *Jonah* written by another person. The book of *Daniel* is a collection of papers written at several different times. The last six chapters contain prophecies written at various times by *Daniel* himself; however, the first six are a collection of historical papers written by others. The fourth chapter is a decree of *Nebuchadnezzar*. The first chapter was written after *Daniel's* death, for the author says that *Daniel* continued until the first year of *Cyrus*—that is, to his first year ruling over the *Persians* and *Medes*, and his third year over *Babylon*. For the same reason, the fifth and sixth chapters were also written after his death, because they end with these words: *So this Daniel prospered in the reign of Darius, and in the reign of Cyrus the Persian.* Yet, these specific words might have been added by the collector of the papers, whom I believe to be *Ezra*.

## Chap. I. Prophecies of Daniel.

The *Psalms* composed by *Moses*, *David*, and others, seem to have also been collected by *Ezra* into one volume. I consider him the collector because in this collection I find *Psalms* written as late as the *Babylonian* captivity, but none later.

After these things, *Antiochus Epiphanes* <note>A Hellenistic king of the Seleucid Empire (reigned 175–164 BC) who famously persecuted the Jews.</note> plundered <note>original: "spoiled"</note> the Temple, commanded the *Jews* to forsake the *Law* upon pain of death, and caused the sacred books to be burned wherever they could be found. In these troubles, the *book* of the *Chronicles* of the *Kings* of *Israel* <note>Newton refers here to a lost historical record cited in the Bible, not the biblical Book of Chronicles.</note> was entirely lost. But upon recovering from this oppression, *Judas Maccabaeus* gathered together all those writings that could be found (*2 Maccabees 2:14*). In arranging them <note>original: "reducing them into order"</note>, part of the *Prophecies* of *Isaiah*, or some other *Prophet*, was added to the end of the *Prophecies* of *Zechariah*. Additionally, the *book* of *Ezra* was separated from the *book* of *Chronicles* and arranged in two different orders: in one order in the *book* of *Ezra* received into the *Canon* <note>The authorized list of books in the Bible.</note>, and in another order in the first *book* of *Esdras* <note>An ancient Greek version of the book of Ezra, often found in the Apocrypha.</note>.

After the *Roman* captivity <note>The Jewish Diaspora following the Roman destruction of Jerusalem in 70 AD.</note>, the *Jews*, for the sake of preserving their traditions, put them in writing in their *Talmud*. For the sake of preserving their scriptures, they agreed upon a specific *Edition*, added vowel points <note>original: "pointed it"; referring to the Masoretic points added to Hebrew text to indicate pronunciation.</note>, and counted the letters of every kind in every book. # Observations upon the Part I.

Older variant readings <note>original: "antienter various lections"</note>, except those that can be discovered through the *Septuagint* <note>The ancient Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible, which reflects a Hebrew text older than the Masoretic version.</note> version, are now lost. The *Jews*, before the *Roman* captivity, divided the sacred books into the Law, the Prophets, and the *Hagiographa* <note>From the Greek meaning "holy writings." In the Hebrew Bible, these are the *Ketuvim*.</note>; they read only the Law and the Prophets in their synagogues. *Christ* and his Apostles emphasized the religious importance <note>original: "laid the stress of religion upon"</note> of the Law and the Prophets (*Matthew 7:12; 22:4; Luke 16:16, 29, 31; 24:44; Acts 24:14; 26:22; Romans 3:21*). By the *Hagiographa*, they meant the historical books called *Joshua*, *Judges*, *Ruth*, *Samuel*, *Kings*, *Chronicles*, *Ezra*, *Nehemiah*, and *Esther*, as well as the book of *Job*, the *Psalms*, the books of *Solomon* <note>Proverbs, Ecclesiastes, and the Song of Solomon.</note>, and the *Lamentations*.

By reading the Law and the Prophets in the synagogues, those books have been kept freer from corruption than the *Hagiographa* <note>original: "Hagiographa"; the third section of the Hebrew Bible (Ketuvim), containing books like Psalms, Proverbs, and Job. These were not read as regularly in public worship as the Law and the Prophets.</note>.

In the infancy of the nation of *Israel*, when God had given them a Law and made a covenant with them to be their God if they would keep his commandments, he sent Prophets to reclaim them whenever they revolted to the worship of other gods. Upon their returning to him, they sometimes renewed the covenant which they had broken.

God continued to send these Prophets until the days of *Ezra*. But after their prophecies were read in the synagogues, those writings were thought sufficient. For if the people would not listen to *Moses* and the old Prophets, they would listen to no new ones—not even though they should rise from the dead <note>Newton is referencing the parable of the rich man and Lazarus in Luke 16:31.</note>.

At length, when a new truth was to be preached to the *Gentiles* <note>Non-Jewish people.</note>—namely, that *Jesus* was the *Christ*—God sent new Prophets and teachers. But after their writings were also received and read in the synagogues of the *Christians*, prophecy ceased a second time. We have *Moses*, the Prophets, and Apostles, and the words of *Christ* himself; and if we will not listen to them, we shall be more inexcusable than the *Jews*. <meta>This page continues Newton's argument that the Christian church, like ancient Israel before it, was destined to experience a period of apostasy or "falling away" from the original faith, and that the only sure guide through this corruption is the divine authority of the Prophets.</meta>

...that just as *Israel* often revolted and broke the covenant, and renewed it upon repentance; so there would be a falling away <note>original: "falling away"; a term for apostasy or the abandonment of original religious principles.</note> among the Christians, soon after the days of the Apostles; and that in the latter days God would destroy the unrepentant rebels, and make a new covenant with his people. 

Listening to the Prophets is a fundamental characteristic of the true Church. For God has so ordered the Prophecies, that in the latter days *the wise may understand, but the wicked shall do wickedly, and none of the wicked shall understand* (Daniel 12:9–10). The authority of emperors, kings, and princes is human. The authority of councils, synods, bishops, and presbyters <note>original: "Presbyters"; from the Greek "presbyteros," meaning elders or ministers in the early church.</note> is human. The authority of the Prophets is divine, and encompasses the entirety of religion, counting *Moses* and the Apostles among the Prophets; *and if an angel from heaven preaches any other gospel than what they have delivered, let him be accursed* <note>A reference to Galatians 1:8.</note>. 

Their writings contain the covenant between God and his people, along with instructions for keeping this covenant, examples of God's judgments upon those who break it, and predictions of things to come. While the people of God keep the covenant, they continue to be his people; when they break it, they cease to be his people or church, and become the *synagogue of Satan, who say they are Jews* <note>Newton is quoting Revelation 2:9 and 3:9, using "Jews" here in a symbolic or spiritual sense to refer to those who claim to be the true people of God but are not.</note> and are not, but do lie.

<summary>Newton argues that while secular and ecclesiastical authorities are merely human, the Prophets possess divine authority. He asserts that the true Church is defined by its adherence to the prophetic covenant, and warns of a predicted apostasy within Christianity.</summary>
<keywords>Apostasy, Covenant, Divine Authority, Prophets, Church History, Daniel 12, Galatians 1, Synagogue of Satan</keywords>

### Chapter 1. The Prophecies of Daniel.

...and are not. No power on earth is authorized to change this covenant.

The predictions of future events concern the state of the Church throughout every age. Among the ancient Prophets, *Daniel* is the most clear in his chronological order <note>original: "order of time"</note> and the easiest to understand. Therefore, regarding those matters that relate to the end times <note>original: "last times"</note>, his work must be used as the key to understanding all the others.

## CHAPTER 2.

### Of Prophetic Language.

To understand the prophecies, we must first familiarize ourselves with the figurative language used by the Prophets. This language is based on an analogy between the natural world and an empire or kingdom, which is viewed as a "political world."

Accordingly, the entire natural world—consisting of heaven and earth—represents the entire political world, consisting of thrones and people (or at least as much of it as the prophecy discusses). The objects in the natural world signify corresponding things in the political world. For the heavens and everything within them represent thrones and high offices <note>original: "dignities"</note>, as well as those who hold them. The earth and the things upon it represent the common people <note>original: "inferior people"</note>. The lowest parts of the earth, called *Hades* <note>original: the Greek term for the underworld or the grave</note> or Hell, signify the lowest or most miserable among them.

Because of this, "ascending toward heaven" and "descending to the earth" are used to describe rising and falling in power and honor. Similarly, "rising out of the earth" or "out of the waters," and "falling into them," represent rising to a position of authority <note>original: "dignity"</note> or dominion out of a lower <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "inferior."</note>

<summary>Newton explains the "prophetic language" as a system of symbols where elements of the natural world (heaven, earth, sea) represent elements of the political world (rulers, subjects, social classes).</summary>

## CHAP. II.

### Of the Prophetic Language.

To understand the Prophecies, we must first familiarize ourselves with the figurative language used by the Prophets. This language is based on the analogy between the natural world and an empire or kingdom viewed as a political world.

Consequently, the entire natural world—consisting of heaven and earth—represents the entire political world, consisting of rulers <note>original: "thrones"</note> and people, or at least the portion of it addressed in the prophecy. The elements within the natural world represent their corresponding counterparts in the political world. For the heavens and everything in them represent royal positions <note>original: "thrones"</note>, high offices <note>original: "dignities"</note>, and those who hold them. The earth and everything on it represent the common people <note>original: "inferior people"</note>. The lowest parts of the earth, called *Hades* or *Hell* <note>original: "Hades" is the Greek term for the underworld or the place of the dead</note>, represent the lowest or most miserable social classes. 

Therefore, ascending toward heaven and descending to the earth are used to describe rising and falling in power and honor. Rising out of the earth or waters, and falling back into them, represents rising to an office <note>original: "dignity"</note> or position of power <note>original: "dominion"</note> from an in-

Fire and meteors relate to both heaven and earth and signify the following: burning anything with fire represents its destruction by war. ...on the heads of their enemies. Rain (if not excessive), dew, and "living water" represent the graces and teachings of the Holy Spirit. Conversely, the lack of rain represents spiritual barrenness.

On the earth, dry land and collected waters—such as a sea, a river, or a flood—represent the people of various regions, nations, and governments <note>original: "dominions"</note>. Making waters bitter signifies the great suffering of a people caused by war and persecution. Turning things into blood represents the symbolic <note>original: "mystical"</note> death of political organizations <note>original: "bodies politic"</note>, which is to say, their total collapse or dissolution. 

The overflowing of a sea or river represents an invasion of the land <note>original: "earth politic"</note> by the people of the "waters" (a maritime or foreign nation). Conversely, the drying up of waters represents the conquest of those watery regions by the land-based power. Fountains of water stand for cities, which are the permanent sources of the political "rivers." Mountains and islands represent the cities of the land and sea-based powers, along with the territories and kingdoms belonging to those cities. Caves <note>original: "dens"</note> and rocks in the mountains represent the temples of those cities. The hiding of men in those caves and rocks represents the locking away of idols in their temples. Houses and ships represent families, assemblies, and towns within the land or sea-based nations. Finally, a fleet of warships signifies the army of the kingdom that is represented by the sea.

Animals and plants also represent people of various regions and conditions. Specifically, trees, herbs, and land animals represent the people of a land-based nation <note>original: "earth politic"</note>. <meta>This page continues the catalog of prophetic symbols, transitioning from geographical features to the specific meanings of animals, plants, and the hierarchies within "political" versions of the natural world.</meta>

<page-num>20</page-num>
<header>Observations upon the Part I.</header>

...fishes represent those who live in the political waters; birds and insects represent those of the political heaven and earth; a forest represents a kingdom; and a wilderness represents a desolate and sparsely populated people <note>original: "thin people"</note>.

If the political world, as considered in prophecy, consists of many kingdoms, they are represented by an equal number of parts of the natural world. The noblest kingdom is represented by the celestial frame; in this case, the Moon and clouds stand for the common people. Less noble kingdoms are represented by the earth, sea, and rivers, and by the animals, plants <note>original: "vegetables"</note>, or buildings within them. In these instances, the larger and more powerful animals and taller trees represent kings, princes, and nobles. 

Because a whole kingdom is the "political body" <note>original: "body politic"</note> of the king, the symbol used for the king—whether it be the Sun, a tree, a beast, a bird, or a man—is often used in a broad sense to signify the entire kingdom. Various animals, such as a lion, a bear, a leopard, or a goat, are used to represent different kingdoms and political entities according to their specific qualities. The sacrificing of beasts represents the slaughtering and conquering of kingdoms, while friendship between beasts represents peace between kingdoms. 

Yet sometimes plants and animals are given other meanings through specific descriptions or circumstances. For example, a tree might be called the *tree of life* or the *tree of knowledge*, or a beast might be called the *old serpent* or be worshipped.

# Chap. 2. Prophecies of Daniel.

When a Beast or Man represents <note>original: "is put for"</note> a kingdom, its parts and qualities represent the analogous parts and qualities of that kingdom. For example:
* The head of a Beast represents the great men who lead <note>original: "precede"</note> and govern.
* The tail represents the inferior people who follow and are governed.
* The heads, if there are more than one, represent the number of capital parts, dynasties, or dominions in the kingdom—whether they exist at the same time <note>original: "collateral"</note> or follow one after another—in regards to the civil government.
* The horns on any head represent the number of kingdoms within that head in regards to military power.
* Seeing represents understanding, and the eyes represent men of understanding and policy <note>In this context, "policy" refers to political craft or sagacity.</note>; in matters of religion, they represent Bishops <note>original Greek: *Ἐπίσκοποι* (Episkopoi)</note>.
* Speaking represents the making of laws.
* The mouth represents a lawgiver, whether civil or religious <note>original: "sacred"</note>.
* The loudness of a voice represents might and power, while its faintness represents weakness.
* Eating and drinking represent acquiring whatever is signified by the things eaten and drunk.
* The hair of a beast or man, and the feathers of a bird, represent the common people.
* The wings represent the number of kingdoms represented by the beast.
* The arm of a man represents his power, or any people in whom his strength and power resides.
* His feet represent the lowest of the people, or the final period <note>original: "latter end"</note> of the kingdom.
* The feet, nails, and teeth of beasts of prey represent armies and squadrons of armies.
* The bones represent strength and fortified places.

## CHAPTER III.

### *Regarding the vision of the Image composed of four Metals.*

The Prophecies of Daniel are all interconnected, as if they were several parts of one general prophecy given at different times. The first is the easiest to understand, and each subsequent prophecy adds something new to the previous one. The first was given in a dream to Nebuchadnezzar, King of Babylon, in the second year of his reign; but because the King forgot his dream, it was given again to Daniel in a dream and revealed by him to the King. Through this, Daniel immediately <note>original: "presently"</note> became famous for his wisdom and for revealing secrets—so much so that Ezekiel, his contemporary, spoke about him in this way to the King of Tyre in the nineteenth year of Nebuchadnezzar's reign: *Behold,* he says, *you are wiser than Daniel; there is no secret that they can hide from you (Ezekiel 28:3).* This same Ezekiel, in another place, groups Daniel with Noah and Job as those held in the highest favor by God *(Ezekiel 14:14, 16, 18, 20).* And in the final year of Belshazzar's reign, the Queen Mother said about him to the King: *Behold, there*

The king *Nebuchadnezzar* your father—the king, I say, your father—made him master of the *magicians, astrologers, Chaldeans,* and *soothsayers*. This was because *an excellent spirit, knowledge, and understanding—interpreting dreams, showing difficult sentences <note>original: "shewing of hard sentences"; meaning the explaining of riddles or enigmas</note>, and resolving <note>original: "dissolving"</note> doubts—were found in this same Daniel, whom the king named Belteshazzar* (Daniel 5:11, 12). *Daniel* was held in the highest regard <note>original: "greatest credit"</note> among the *Jews* until the reign of the *Roman* Emperor *Hadrian*. To reject his prophecies is to reject the *Christian* religion, for this religion is founded upon his prophecy concerning the *Messiah*.

Now, the foundation of all *Daniel's* prophecies is laid in this vision of the Image composed of four metals. It represents a body of four great nations which should reign over the earth successively: namely <note>original: "viz."</note>, the people of *Babylonia*, the *Persians*, the *Greeks*, and the *Romans*. Furthermore, by a stone cut out without hands—which fell upon the feet of the Image, broke all the four metals to pieces, became a great mountain, and filled the whole earth—it represents that a new kingdom should arise after the four and conquer all those nations,

The head of the statue <note>original: "Image"</note> was made of gold and signifies the nations of *Babylonia*, who reigned first, as *Daniel* himself interprets. "*You are this head of gold*," he says to *Nebuchadnezzar*. These nations reigned until *Cyrus* conquered *Babylon*; within a few months after that conquest, they revolted to the *Persians* and elevated them <note>original: "set them up"</note> above the *Medes*. 

The breast and arms of the statue were made of silver and represent the *Persians*, who reigned next. 

The belly and thighs of the statue were made of brass and represent the *Greeks*, who, under the dominion of *Alexander the Great*, conquered the *Persians* and reigned next after them. 

The legs were made of iron and represent the *Romans*, who reigned next after the *Greeks* and began to conquer them in the eighth year of *Antiochus Epiphanes*. For in that year, they conquered *Perseus*, King of *Macedon*—the primary <note>original: "fundamental"</note> kingdom of the *Greeks*. From then on, they grew into a mighty empire and reigned with great power until the days of *Theodosius the Great* <note>Theodosius I (reigned 379–395 AD) was the last emperor to rule over both the Eastern and Western halves of the Roman Empire</note>. Then, by the incursion of many northern nations, they broke into many smaller kingdoms, which are represented by the feet and toes of the statue, composed partly of iron and partly of clay. For then, says *Daniel*, *the kingdom shall be divided*, and

*...and there will be in it some of the strength of iron, but they shall not hold together <note>original: "cleave one to another"</note>.*

*“And in the days of these kings,”* says Daniel, *“the God of heaven will set up a kingdom which shall never be destroyed; and the kingdom shall not be left to other people, but it shall break in pieces and consume all these kingdoms, and it shall stand forever. Just as you saw that the stone was cut out of the mountains without hands, and that it broke in pieces the iron, the brass, the clay, the silver, and the gold.”*

over their provinces. *Cassander* reigned over *Macedon*, *Greece*, and *Epirus*; *Lysimachus* over *Thrace* and *Bithynia*; *Ptolemy* over *Egypt*, *Libya*, *Arabia*, *Coele-Syria*, and *Palestine*; and *Seleucus* over *Syria*.

<margin>Chapter 7</margin>
The fourth *Beast* was the empire which succeeded that of the *Greeks*, and this was the *Roman*. This beast was exceedingly dreadful and terrible, and had great iron teeth; it devoured and broke in pieces, and trampled the remainder <note>original: "residue"</note> with its feet. Such was the *Roman Empire*. It was larger, stronger, and more formidable and lasting than any of the former empires. It conquered the kingdom of *Macedon*, along with *Illyricum* and *Epirus*, in the eighth year of *Antiochus Epiphanes*, in the Year of Nabonassar <note>original: "Anno Nabonaff." By these and other conquests, it became greater and more terrible than any of the three former *Beasts*. This Empire continued in its greatness until the reign of *Theodosius the Great*, and then it broke into ten kingdoms, represented by the ten horns of this *Beast*. It continued in a broken form until the *Ancient of Days* sat on a throne like a fiery flame, *the judgment began <note>original: "the judgment was set"</note>, the books were opened, and the Beast was slain.*

### Chapter 4: Prophecies of Daniel

<margin>Chapter 7:13</margin>
*...and his body was destroyed and given to the burning flames; and one like the Son of Man came with the clouds of heaven, and came to the Ancient of Days, and received dominion over all nations, and judgment was given to the saints of the Most High, and the time came that they possessed the kingdom.*

<margin>Chapter 7:11, 12</margin>
"I watched," says *Daniel*, "*until the Beast was slain, and his body destroyed and given to the burning flames.* Regarding the rest of the *Beasts*, *their dominion was taken away; yet their lives were prolonged for a season and a time.*" Therefore, all four *Beasts* are still alive, although the dominion of the first three has been taken away. The nations of *Chaldea* and *Assyria* are still the first *Beast*. Those of *Media* and *Persia* are still the second *Beast*. Those of *Macedonia*, *Greece*, *Thrace*, *Asia Minor*, *Syria*, and *Egypt* are still the third. And those of *Europe*, on this side of *Greece* <note>meaning Western Europe</note>, are still the fourth. Since the body of the third *Beast* is confined to the nations on this side of the river *Euphrates*, and the body of the fourth *Beast* is confined to the nations on this side of *Greece*, we must look for all four heads of the third *Beast* among the nations on this side of the river *Euphrates*, and for all eleven horns of the fourth *Beast* among the nations on this side of *Greece*. And therefore—

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues Newton's historical account of the Germanic tribes, specifically the Franks, and their integration into or conflict with the Roman Empire during the 4th century AD. It follows his discussion of the division of the Roman Empire and the symbolic "horns" of the fourth beast.</meta>

<page-num>36</page-num>
<header>Observations upon the Part I.</header>

...and having been resisted, they settled themselves on the northern side of the Rhine, opposite Mainz <note>original: "Mentz"</note>. In the year 358, a group of the Salian Franks, along with their King, coming from the river Sala <note>likely the IJssel river in the Netherlands</note>, were welcomed into the Empire by the Emperor Julian and settled in Gaul <note>original: "Gallia"</note> between Brabant and the Rhine. Their King, Mellobaudes, was appointed Count of the Household <note>original: "Comes domesticorum"; a high-ranking military commander of the imperial guard</note> by the Emperor Gratian.

Richomer, another noble Salian Frank, was appointed Count of the Household and Master of Both Services <note>original: "Magister utriusque Militiæ"; the commander-in-chief of both the infantry and cavalry</note> by Theodosius. In 384 AD <note>original: "A. C." (Anno Christi)</note>, he served as Consul alongside Clearchus. He was a great favorite of Theodosius and accompanied him in his wars against the usurper Eugenius, but he died during the expedition. He left a son named Theudomir, who later became King of the Salian Franks in Brabant. During this war, some Franks from beyond the Rhine invaded Gaul under the leadership of Genobald, Marcomir, and Suno, but they were driven back by Stilicho <note>original: "Stilico"</note>. After Marcomir was killed, he was succeeded in Germany by his son, Pharamond.

While these nations remained peaceful within the Empire and subject to the Romans, many others stayed beyond the Danube until the death of Emperor Theodosius, at which point they rose up in arms. Paul the Deacon <note>original: "Paulus Diaconus"</note>, in the fourteenth book of his *Miscellaneous History* <note>original: "Historia Miscell. lib. xiv."</note>, speaking of the time immediately following the death of this Emperor, tells us: "At the same time... <note>original: "Eodem tem-" (Eodem tempore)</note>"

<page-num>37</page-num>

### Chapter 5. Prophecies of Daniel.

"...at that time there were the Goths and other very great nations living across the Danube. Of these, the four more civilized <note>original Latin: "rationabiliores," meaning more rational or developed</note> tribes were the Goths, the Visigoths <note>original: "Huisogothi"</note>, the Gepids, and the Vandals; they differed only in name and in nothing else. These groups crossed the Danube during the reigns of Arcadius and Honorius and were settled in Roman territory. The Gepids—from whom the Lombards and Avars later branched off—inhabited the villages around Singidunum <note>modern-day Belgrade</note> and Sirmium." *Procopius*, at the beginning of his *History of the Vandalic War* <note>original: "Historia Vandalica"</note>, writes to the same effect. Up until this point, the Western Empire had remained intact, but it now broke into many kingdoms.

Theodosius died in 395 AD <note>original: "A. C. 395"</note>. Immediately afterward, the Visigoths—led by Alaric, the successor of Fritigern <note>original: "Fridigern"</note>—rose up from their settlements in Thrace. For five years, they devastated Macedonia, Thessaly, Achaia, the Peloponnese, and Epirus with fire and sword. Turning westward, they then invaded Dalmatia, Illyricum, and Pannonia. From there, they entered Italy in 402 AD. The following year, they were so thoroughly defeated at Pollentia and Verona by Stilicho <note>original: "Stilico"</note>, the commander of the Western Empire’s forces, that the poet Claudian called the remains of Alaric's army "small remnants of so great a nation" <note>original: "tanta ex gente reliquias breves"</note>, and Prudentius called them "a destroyed people" <note>original: "Gentem deletam"</note>. Consequently, Alaric made peace with the Emperor. He was so thoroughly humbled that, as Orosius says, he begged "for the best peace possible and any lands whatsoever" <note>original: "pro pace optima & quibuscunque sedibus"</note>...

<keywords>Goths, Visigoths, Alaric, Theodosius, Stilicho, Western Roman Empire, migrations, Procopius, Singidunum</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the barbarian invasions during the late 4th century, specifically following Alaric's movements and the subsequent migration of northern tribes into Roman territory.</meta>

<page-num>38</page-num>

...to pray humbly and simply <note>original Latin: "suppliciter & simpliciter orare"</note>. This peace was ratified by an exchange of hostages; Aetius <note>a high-ranking Roman general who spent time as a hostage among both the Goths and the Huns</note> was sent as a hostage to Alaric, and Alaric remained an independent Prince in the territories now granted to him.

When Alaric took up arms, the nations beyond the Danube began to move; and during the next winter, between 395 and 396 AD <note>original: "A. C. 395 and 396"</note>, a great body of Huns, Alans, Ostrogoths, Gepids, and other northern nations crossed the frozen Danube, having been invited by Rufinus <note>a powerful Eastern Roman statesman accused by historians of inviting invaders to destabilize his rivals</note>. At the same time, their kinsmen who had already obtained lands within the Empire also rose up in arms. Jerome <note>St. Jerome, the prominent Christian scholar and historian</note> calls this great multitude Huns, Alans, Vandals, Goths, Sarmatians, Quadians <note>original: "Quades"</note>, and Marcomanni <note>original: "Marcomans"</note>; he says that they invaded every region between Constantinople and the Julian Alps, devastating Scythia, Thrace, Macedonia, Dardania, Dacia, Thessaly, Achaia, Epirus, Dalmatia, and all of Pannonia. The Suebi <note>original: "Suevians"</note> also invaded Raetia <note>original: "Rhætia"</note>. While the Romans were occupied defending Raetia, Alaric ravaged Pannonia; this gave Alaric an opportunity to invade Italy, as the poet Claudian mentions:

> "Only by treachery did the Goths find a time when we were vulnerable; they burst in while Raetia occupied our strength, and our troops were exhausted by another war." <note>original Latin: "Non nisi perfidiâ nacti penetrable tempus, / Irrupere Getæ, nostras dum Rhætia vires / Occupat, atque alio desudant Marte cohortes."</note>

# Chapter 5. Prophecies of Daniel.

And when Alaric went from those parts into Italy, some other barbarian nations invaded Noricum and Vindelicia <note>Roman provinces located in what is now Austria and southern Germany</note>, as the same poet Claudian thus writes:

-> "Already the nations had broken their treaties, and emboldened by the news of Rome’s defeat, they occupied the forests of Vindelicia and the fields of Noricum." <note>original Latin: *Jam fœdera gentes Exuerant, Latiique auditâ clade feroces Vendelicos saltus & Norica rura tenebant.*</note> <-

This occurred in the years 402 and 403 AD. Among these nations, I include the Suebi <note>original: "Suevians"</note>, Quadi <note>original: "Quades"</note>, and Marcomanni <note>original: "Marcomans"</note>; for they were all at war during this time. The Quadi and Marcomanni were Suebian tribes; they and the Suebi originated from Bohemia and the river Suevus (also called the Spree <note>original: "Sprake"</note>) in Lusatia. They were now united under a single common king named Hermeric <note>original: "Ermeric"</note>, who soon after led them into Gaul <note>original: "Gallia"</note>.

The Vandals and Alans may also have expanded into Noricum around this time. Uldin, also leading a large force of Huns, crossed the Danube around the time of John Chrysostom’s banishment—that is, 404 AD—and devastated Thrace and Moesia <note>original: "Mæsia"</note>. Radagaisus, King of the Greuthungi <note>original: "Gruthunni"</note> and successor to Winithar, invited more barbarians from beyond the Danube and invaded Italy with an army of over two hundred thousand Goths. Within a year or two (in 405 or 406 AD), he was defeated by Stilicho <note>original: "Stilico"</note>,

In this war, *Stilicho* <note>original: "Stilico"</note> was assisted by a large force of *Huns* <note>original: "Hunns"</note> and *Ostrogoths* under the command of *Uldin* and *Sarus*, who were hired by the Emperor *Honorius*. In all this confusion, it was necessary for the *Lombards* in *Pannonia* <note>a Roman province located in the Danube region, including parts of modern-day Hungary and Austria</note> to arm themselves in their own defense and assert their liberty, as the *Romans* were no longer able to protect them.

And now *Stilicho*, intending to make himself Emperor, secured a military command <note>original: "prefecture"</note> for *Alaric* and sent him into the *East* to serve *Honorius*, the *Western* Emperor. He placed some *Roman* troops under *Alaric's* command to strengthen his army of *Goths*, promising to follow soon after with his own army. His pretense was to recover certain regions of *Illyricum* <note>a Roman province on the eastern coast of the Adriatic Sea</note> which the *Eastern* Emperor was accused of wrongfully withholding <note>original: "detain injuriously"</note> from the *Western* Emperor; however, his secret design was to make himself Emperor with the assistance of the *Vandals* and their allies, for he himself was a *Vandal*.

To facilitate this plan, he invited a large body of barbarian nations to invade the *Western Empire* while he and *Alaric* invaded the *Eastern*. These nations, under their various kings—the *Vandals* under *Godegisilus*, the *Alans* in two groups (one under *Goar* and the other under *Respendial* <note>original: "Resplendial"</note>), and the *Suebi* <note>original: "Suevians"</note>, *Quadi* <note>original: "Quades"</note>, and *Marcomanni* <note>original: "Marcomans"</note> under *Hermeric* <note>original: "Ermeric"</note>—marched through *Raetia* <note>original: "Rhætia," a Roman province including parts of modern Switzerland and Germany</note> to the banks of the *Rhine*,

The rest escaped only because a group of *Respendial’s* <note>original: "Resplendial’s"</note> *Alans* arrived in time to assist them.

Then the *British* soldiers, alarmed by the rumors of these events, revolted and established "tyrants" <note>usurpers or local rulers not recognized by the central Roman government</note> there. First they chose *Marcus*, whom they killed immediately; then *Gratian*, whom they killed within four months; and finally *Constantine* <note>Constantine III</note>, under whose leadership they invaded *Gaul* <note>original: "Gallia"</note> in 408 AD, supported by *Goar* and *Gundicar*. Once *Constantine* had taken control of a large part of *Gaul*, he named his son *Constans* as *Caesar* <note>a title for a junior emperor or designated heir</note> and sent him into *Spain* in 409 AD to manage his affairs there.

...had gone over to the *Romans*, led his army from the *Rhine*; and, together with the *Suevians* and the rest <note>original: "residue"</note> of the *Vandals*, headed toward *Spain*. Meanwhile, the *Franks* followed up their victory so effectively that they retook *Trier* <note>original: "Triers"</note>; after plundering the city, they left it to the *Romans*. The *Barbarians* <note>a term used by the Romans for non-Roman peoples, such as the Germanic tribes</note> were at first stopped by the *Pyrenees* <note>original: "Pyrenean mountains"</note>, which caused them to spread throughout *Aquitaine* <note>a region in southwestern France</note>. However, the following year, some soldiers serving under *Constans* <note>the son of the usurper Constantine III</note> betrayed the mountain pass to them. Entering *Spain* on September 28 <note>original: "4 Kal. Octob." (the fourth day before the Kalends of October)</note>, A.D. 409 <note>A.C. stands for Anno Christi, synonymous with A.D.</note>, each group conquered whatever territory they could. Eventually, in A.D. 411, they divided their conquests by lot. The *Vandals* obtained *Baetica* <note>southern Spain</note> and part of *Gallaecia* <note>northwest Spain</note>; the *Suevians* took the rest of *Gallaecia*; and the *Alans* took *Lusitania* <note>modern Portugal</note> and the *Carthaginian* Province <note>Carthaginensis, in southeastern Spain</note>. In A.D. 413, the Emperor—seeking peace—officially granted them these territories <note>original: "seats"</note>.

The *Roman Franks* mentioned above, having appointed *Theudomir* as their King, began to invade their neighbors immediately after their victory over the *Vandals*. The first they attacked were
<margin>The *Arboric Gauls* <note>original: "Galli Arborici"</note>: from which the region was named *Arboricbant*, and by contraction, *Brabant*.</margin>
the *Gauls* of *Brabant*. However, after meeting significant resistance, the Franks sought an alliance with them. Consequently, those *Gauls* abandoned their loyalty to the *Romans* and formed a close league with the *Franks*, agreeing to become one people and intermarrying with one another,

... each other <note>referring to the Franks, Romans, and Gauls mentioned on the previous page</note> and conforming to one another’s manners, until they became one people without distinction. Thus, by the arrival of these Gauls and of the foreign *Franks* who afterwards crossed the *Rhine*, the *Salian* kingdom <note>the Salians were a subgroup of the Franks who eventually founded the Merovingian dynasty</note> soon grew very large and powerful.

*Stilicho’s* <note>original: "Stilico"</note> expedition against the *Greek* Emperor <note>the Eastern Roman Emperor in Constantinople</note> was stopped by the order of *Honorius*. Then *Alaric* <note>King of the Visigoths</note> moved out of *Epirus* <note>a region in modern-day Greece and Albania</note> into *Noricum* <note>a Roman province in modern-day Austria and Slovenia</note> and requested a sum of money in exchange for his military service. The Senate was inclined to deny him, but they granted the payment through *Stilicho’s* mediation. 

However, after some time, *Stilicho* was accused of a treacherous conspiracy with *Alaric* and was executed on August 23, A.D. 408 <note>original: "10 Kal. Sept. A. C. 408" (the tenth day before the Kalends of September)</note>. Because of this, *Alaric* was denied his money and was regarded as an enemy of the Empire. He then broke straight into *Italy* with the army he had brought from *Epirus* and sent word to his brother *Adolphus* <note>also known as Athaulf</note> to follow him with whatever forces he had in *Pannonia* <note>a province covering parts of modern Hungary, Austria, and the Balkans</note>. These forces were not large, but they were not to be overlooked. 

Fearing that he would be trapped in *Rome*, *Honorius* retreated to *Ravenna* in *October*, A.D. 408. From that time on, *Ravenna* continued to be the seat of the *Western* Emperors. In those days, the *Huns* <note>original: "Hunns"</note> also invaded *Pannonia*; by seizing the abandoned territories <note>original: "seats"</note> of the *Vandals*, *Alans*, and *Goths*, they founded a new kingdom there. *Alaric*, advancing toward *Rome*, besieged it,

# Observations upon the Part I.

And on August 24, A.D. 410 <note>original: "9 Kal. Sept. A. C. 410"—the ninth day before the Kalends of September, 410 years after Christ</note>, Alaric took Rome. Afterward, while attempting to pass into *Africa*, he was shipwrecked. Following this, the Emperor *Honorius* made peace with him and gathered an army to send against the usurper <note>original: "Tyrant"</note> *Constantine*.

At the same time, *Gerontius*, one of *Constantine's* generals <note>original: "captains"</note>, rebelled against him and proclaimed *Maximus* as Emperor in *Spain*. Consequently, *Constantine* sent *Edobec*, another of his generals, to recruit the assistance of the barbarians in Gaul <note>original: "Gallia"</note> under the leadership of *Goar* and *Gundicar*, as well as reinforcements of *Franks* and *Alemans* from beyond the *Rhine*. He also entrusted the defense of *Vienne* in *Gallia Narbonensis* <note>a Roman province in southern France</note> to his son, *Constans*. 

As *Gerontius* advanced, he first killed *Constans* at *Vienne* and then began to besiege *Constantine* at *Arles*. However, because *Honorius* sent *Constantius* with an army for the same purpose at that very time, *Gerontius* fled. *Constantius* then continued the siege, strengthened by the arrival of the majority of *Gerontius's* soldiers. 

After a four-month siege, *Edobec* having obtained reinforcements, the barbarian kings at Mainz <note>original: "Ments"</note>, *Goar* and *Gundicar*, proclaimed *Jovinus* as Emperor, and together with him set out to relieve the siege at *Arles*. As they approached, *Constantius* retreated. They pursued him, but he defeated them in a surprise attack. Although he did not follow up on his victory, and the barbarians soon recovered, it was not enough to prevent the eventual downfall of the usurpers *Constantine*, *Jovinus*, and

### Chapter 5: Prophecies of Daniel

...and *Maximus*. *Britain* could not be recovered by the Empire, but remained a distinct kingdom forever after.

The next year, A.D. 412 <note>original: "A. C. 412"—After Christ</note>, the *Visigoths*, having been beaten in *Italy*, were granted *Aquitaine* <note>a large region in southwestern France</note> to retreat into. They invaded it with much violence, forcing the *Alans* and *Burgundians*—who were then plundering the area—to retreat. At the same time, the *Burgundians* were brought to a peace agreement, and the Emperor granted them an inherited region along the *Rhine* which they had previously invaded. I presume the Emperor did the same for the *Alans*. 

However, not long after, the *Franks* retook and burned *Trier* <note>original: "Triers"</note>. In A.D. 415, *Castinus* was sent against them with an army; he routed them and killed their king, *Theudomir*. This was the second time the *Franks* captured *Trier*. In total, the city was taken four times: once by the *Vandals* and three times by the *Franks*. *Theudomir* was succeeded by *Pharamond*, the prince or king of the *Salian Franks* in *Germany*. From there, he brought new forces, reigned over the entire group, and was granted settlements <note>original: "seats"</note> for his people within the Empire near the *Rhine*.

By this time, the barbarians were all pacified and settled into several kingdoms within the Empire—not only through conquest but also through the official grants of the Emperor *Honorius*. As *Rutilius* wrote in his *Itinerary* <note>original: "Itinerary"—likely referring to the poem "De reditu suo" by Rutilius Claudius Namatianus, which describes a voyage from Rome to Gaul</note>, written in the autumn of the year...

<summary>This page details the formal settlement of the Visigoths, Alans, Burgundians, and Franks within the Western Roman Empire's borders between A.D. 412 and 415, noting that these groups established kingdoms through a mix of military force and imperial land grants.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Western Roman Empire's decline, specifically citing contemporary 5th-century authors like Rutilius Namatianus and Orosius to show how barbarian tribes were settled into formal kingdoms by A.D. 417.</meta>

<page-num>46</page-num>

<header>Observations upon the Prophecies of Daniel. Part I.</header>

In the year of the City <note>original: "Urbis"—referring to the founding of Rome (A.U.C.)</note> 1169—that is, according to Varro's calculation then in use, A.D. 416 <note>original: "A. C. 416"—After Christ</note>—Rutilius thus laments the wasted fields:

->Those fields are indeed too disfigured by long wars;<-
<note>original: "Illa quidem longis nimium deformia bellis;"</note>

And then he adds:

->Now it is time to build even humble shepherds' huts<-
->upon the torn estates after the long fires.<-
<note>original: "Jam tempus laceris post longa incendia fundis / Vel pastorales ædificare casas."</note>

And a little later:

->May the Rhine plow for you forever.<-
<note>original: "Æternum tibi Rhenus aret." This refers to the Rhine river being used for peaceful agriculture rather than as a war-torn border.</note>

And Orosius, in the conclusion of his history, which was finished in A.D. 417, describes a general pacification of the barbarian nations using the words "restrain, confine, and assign the most savage nations" <note>original: "comprimere, coangustare, addicere gentes immanissimas"</note>. He calls them "assigned to the Empire" <note>original: "imperio addictas"</note> because they had obtained settlements <note>original: "seats"</note> in the Empire by alliance and treaty <note>original: "league and compact"</note>. He describes them as "confined" <note>original: "coangustatas"</note> because they no longer invaded all regions at their pleasure, but by the same treaty remained quiet in the territories then granted to them. 

And these are the kingdoms of which the feet of the Image <note>referring to the statue in Nebuchadnezzar's dream in Daniel 2:31-45</note> were composed from that time forward. They are represented by the intermixed iron and clay, which did not bond together and were of different strengths.

# CHAPTER VI.

## Of the ten Kingdoms represented by the ten horns of the fourth Beast.

Now, by the wars described above, the *Western Empire* of the *Romans*—around the time that *Rome* was besieged and taken by the *Goths*—became broken into the following ten kingdoms:

1. The kingdom of the *Vandals* and *Alans* in *Spain* and *Africa*.
2. The kingdom of the *Suevians* <note>The Suebi, a large group of Germanic peoples</note> in *Spain*.
3. The kingdom of the *Visigoths*.
4. The kingdom of the *Alans* in *Gaul* <note>original: "Gallia"</note>.
5. The kingdom of the *Burgundians*.
6. The kingdom of the *Franks*.
7. The kingdom of the *Britons* <note>original: "Britains"</note>.
8. The kingdom of the *Huns* <note>original: "Hunns"</note>.
9. The kingdom of the *Lombards*.
10. The kingdom of *Ravenna* <note>Referring to the Exarchate of Ravenna, the center of Byzantine power in Italy following the collapse of the Western Empire</note>.

Seven of these kingdoms are mentioned by *Sigonius* <note>Carlo Sigonio (c. 1524–1584), an Italian scholar known for his histories of the Roman Empire and the Middle Ages</note> in this way: <gloss>1</gloss> During the reign of Honorius <note>original: "Honorio regnante"</note>, <gloss>2</gloss> into Pannonia <note>a Roman province in Central Europe, primarily in modern-day Hungary</note> <gloss>3</gloss> came the Huns <note>original: "Hunni"</note>, <gloss>4</gloss> and into Spain came the Vandals, Alans, Suevi <gloss>5</gloss> and Goths <note>original: "& Gothi"</note>,

<meta>catchword: Gothi,</meta>

He begins to cite the historian Sigonius to provide chronological evidence for their establishment during the reign of Emperor Honorius.</summary>

...Goths; into Gaul <note>original: Gallia</note>, the Alans, Burgundians, and Goths were received and granted fixed settlements <note>original: certis sedibus permissis, accepti</note>. 1. The Kings of the Vandals were: in A.D. <note>original: A.C. (Anno Christi)</note> 407 Godegefilus, 407 Gunderic, 426 Geiseric, 477 Hunneric, 484 Gundemund, 496 Thrasamund, 523 Geiseric <note>Most historical records identify the king following Thrasamund as Hilderic; Geiseric was the founder</note>, and 530 Gelimer. Godegefilus led them into Gaul <note>original: Gallia</note> in A.D. 406, Gunderic into Spain in A.D. 409, and Geiseric into Africa in A.D. 427; Gelimer was eventually conquered by Belisarius <note>A famous Byzantine general under Emperor Justinian I</note> in A.D. 533. Their kingdom lasted 126 years in total across Gaul, Spain, and Africa; they were especially powerful in Africa.

The Alans had only two kings of their own in Spain: Resplendial and Ataces (also called Utacus or Othacar). Under Resplendial, they entered France in A.D. 407 and Spain in A.D. 409. Ataces was killed along with almost his entire army by Vallia, King of the Visigoths, in A.D. 419. Following this, the remaining Alans submitted themselves to Gunderic, King of the Vandals, in Baetica <note>original: Bætica; a Roman province in southern Spain, modern-day Andalusia</note>, and later moved with them into Africa, as I have learned from Procopius <note>A prominent 6th-century Byzantine historian</note>. Because of this, the Kings of the Vandals styled themselves "Kings of the Vandals and Alans," as seen in the Edict of Hunneric recorded by Victor <note>Victor Vitensis, a bishop who wrote a history of the Vandal persecutions in Africa</note> in his account of the Vandal persecution. Along with the Chatti <note>An ancient Germanic tribe</note>, these Alans gave the name Catalonia <note>original: Cathalaunia, or Catth-Alania</note> to the province which is still called by that name today. There, they were subjects of the Huns until the death of Attila in A.D. <note>original: A. C. (Anno Christi)</note> 454, and were eventually conquered by the Ostrogoths.

The kings of the Suebi <note>original: Suevians; a large group of Germanic peoples</note> were: Ermeric in A.D. 407, Rechila in 438, Rechiarius in 448, Maldra in 458, Frumarius in 460, and Regismund in 463. After several other kings whose names are unknown, the following reigned: Theudomir in A.D. 558, Miro in 568, Euboricus in 582, and Andeca in 583. Once this kingdom was established in Spain, it remained situated in Galicia <note>original: Gallæcia</note> and Lusitania <note>A Roman province covering most of modern-day Portugal and part of western Spain</note>. After the fall of the Alan kingdom, Ermeric expanded his rule across all of Galicia, forcing the Vandals to retreat into Baetica <note>original: Bætica; modern Andalusia</note> and the Carthaginian province <note>Carthaginiensis; a Roman province in southeastern Spain</note>. According to the historian Isidore of Seville <note>original: Isidorus</note>, this kingdom lasted 177 years; it was then conquered by Leovigild, King of the Visigoths, and turned into a province of his kingdom in A.D. 585.

The region is now called *Campania* or *Champagne*. In that battle, 162,000 men were killed on both sides.

Under *Beurgus* (or *Biorgor*), they raided the surrounding areas of *Gaul* until the reign of Emperor *Maximus*. They then crossed the *Alps* in winter and entered *Liguria* <note>a region in north-western Italy</note>, but they were defeated there, and *Beurgus* was killed by *Ricimer*, the commander of the Emperor’s forces, in A.D. 464. 5. The Kings of the Burgundians were: Gundicar (A.D. 407), Gundioc (436), Bilimer (467), Gundobald <note>original: Gundobaldus</note> with his brothers (473), Sigismund (510), and Godomar <note>original: Godomarus</note> (517). Under Gundicar, they invaded Gaul <note>original: Gallia</note> in A.D. 407 and were granted territories <note>original: seats</note> by the Emperor near the Rhine in *Gallia Belgica* <note>a Roman province in the north of Gaul</note> in A.D. 412. They had Saxons among them and were by then so powerful that Orosius wrote of them in A.D. 417: "The Gauls even today are witnesses that the force of the Burgundians is very powerful, for they remain in the territories they have occupied." <note>original: Burgundionum esse prævalidam manum, Galliæ hodieque testes sunt, in quibus præsumpta possessione consistunt.</note> Around the year 435, they suffered great defeats at the hands of Ætius, and shortly after by the Huns; but five years later, they were granted Savoy to be shared with the inhabitants. From that time, they again became a powerful kingdom, bounded by the river Rhône <note>original: Rhodanus</note>, though later they extended much further into the heart of Gaul. Gundobald conquered the regions around the rivers Saône <note>original: Araris</note> and Rhône, along with the territories of Marseilles; and, invading Italy during the reign of the Emperor Glycerius, he conquered all his brothers. Godomar made Orleans his royal seat, which is why the kingdom was called the Kingdom of the Orleanians <note>original: Regnum Aurelianorum</note>. He was conquered by Clotaire <note>original: Clotharius</note> and Childebert, Kings of the

*Franks, A.D. 526.* From that time forward, this kingdom <note>the kingdom of Burgundy</note> was sometimes united to the kingdom of the *Franks*, and sometimes divided from it, until the reign of *Charlemagne* <note>original: Charles the great</note>, who made his son *Carloman* <note>original: Carolottus; referring to Charles the Younger or Carloman I</note> King of *Burgundy*. For about 300 years after that, it was ruled by its own kings; it was then broken into the *Dukedom of Burgundy*, the *County of Burgundy*, and the *County of Savoy*; and later, these were broken into other smaller counties.

6. The Kings of the *Franks* were: *Theudomir* (A.D. 407), *Pharamond* (417), *Clodio* (428), *Merovech* <note>original: Merovæus</note> (448), *Childeric* (456), *Clovis* <note>original: Clodovæus</note> (482), etc. *Wendelin* <note>original: Windeline</note> and *Bucher*, two of the most diligent researchers into the origins of this kingdom, date its beginning to the same year as the *Barbarian* invasions of *Gaul* <note>original: Gallia</note>—that is, A.D. 407. Regarding the first kings, there is this record in *Labbe’s Manuscript Library* <note>original: Labbe’s Bibliotheca M.S.</note>:

> *Certain historical excerpts from an ancient genealogical tree of the Kings of France.* <note>original: Historica quædam excerpta ex veteri stemmate genealogico Regum Franciæ.</note>
>
> *Genobald, Marcomer, Suno, and Theudomir. These were dukes or minor kings from the beginning of the Frankish people at various times. However, historians leave it uncertain exactly how they succeeded one another in the line of descent.* <note>original: Genobaldus, Marcomerus, Suno, Theodemeris. Isti duces vel reguli extiterunt à principio gentis Francorum diversis temporibus. Sed incertum relinquunt historici quali sibi procreations lineâ successerunt.</note>
>
> *Pharamond: under this, their first king, the Franks submitted themselves to laws which their leaders—Wisogast, Atrogast, and Salegast—had established.* <note>original: Pharamundus : sub hoc rege suo primo Franci legibus se subdunt, quas primores eorum tulerunt Wisogastus, Atrogastus, Salegastus.</note>

*Clodio* <note>original: Chlochilo</note>. *Merovech* <note>original: Merovechus</note>. As for Genobald, Marcomer, and Suno, they were captains of the Transrhenane <term>Transrhenane</term> <note>meaning: those living on the far side of the Rhine river, outside the Roman Empire</note> Franks in the reign of Theodosius, and they do not concern us here. We are to begin with Theudomir, the first king of the rebelling Salii <note>the Salian Franks</note>, called Didio by Ivo of Chartres <note>original: Ivo Carnotenſis</note>, and Thiedo or Theudemerus by Beatus Rhenanus. His face is depicted on a gold coin found with this inscription: THEUDEMIR REX <note>King Theudomir</note>. This was published by Petavius and still exists (or did recently), as Wendelin <note>original: Windeline</note> testifies. This shows that he was a king, and that he reigned in Gaul <note>original: Gallia</note>; since unrefined Germany did not understand the minting of money at that time, nor did they use Latin words or letters. He was the son of Ricimer (or Richomer), the favorite of the Emperor Theodosius. Being a Roman Frank and of the Salian royal blood, the people therefore made him king upon their rebellion. The entire duration of his reign is stated in the *Excerpts of Gregory of Tours* by Fredegar <note>original: Excerptis Gregorii Turonenſis è Fredigario</note>, chapters 5, 6, 7, and 8. # Chap. 6. Prophecies of Daniel.

The events following the death of this King are recorded as a series of successive things set down in this order:

"After the generals among the Franks had died out, kings were once again chosen from the same lineage as before. At the same time, Jovinus assumed the royal regalia. Constantine fled toward Italy; however, assassins sent by Prince Jovinus caught him at the river Mincio <note>original: Mentio</note> and beheaded him. Many of the nobles were captured at Auvergne <note>original: Avernis</note> by Jovinus’s order and were cruelly executed by the generals of Honorius. The city of Trier <note>original: Trevirorum</note> was captured and burned by the Franks, due to the faction of a senator named Lucius." <note>original: Extinctis Ducibus in Francis, denuo Reges creantur ex eadem stirpe qua prius fuerant. Eodem tempore Jovinus ornatus regios assumpsit. Constantinus fugam versus Italiam dirigit; missis a Jovino Principe percussoribus super Mentio flumine, capite truncatur. Multi nobilium jussu Jovini apud Avernis capti, & a ducibus Honorii crudeliter interempti sunt. Trevirorum civitas, factione unius ex senatoribus nomine Lucii, à Francis capta & incensa est.</note>

—"Castinus, the Count of the Domestics <term>Count of the Domestics</term> <note>a high-ranking commander of the Roman imperial guard</note>, undertook an expedition against the Franks, etc." <note>original: Castinus Domesticorum Comes expeditionem accipit contra Francos, &c.</note>

Then, returning to speak of *Theudomir*, he adds:

"The Franks, diligently searching for a long-haired <term>long-haired</term> <note>Frankish kings traditionally wore their hair long as a symbol of their royal blood and right to rule</note> king to elect for themselves—as was the ancient custom—from the lineage of Priam, Frigus, and Francio, established as their ruler a man named Theudemer, the son of Richemer. He was the one killed by the Romans in the battle I mentioned above" <note>original: Franci electum à se regem, sicut prius fuerat, crinitum inquirentes diligenter ex genere Priami, Frigi & Francionis, super se crearunt nomine Theudemerum filium Richemeris, qui in hoc prælio quod supra memini, à Romanis interfectus est</note>—that is, in the battle with *Castinus's* army. Of his death, *Gregory of Tours* <note>original: Gregory Turonensis</note> makes this further mention: "We read in the consular records that Theodemer, king of the Franks and son of the late Ricimer, along with his mother Ascila, were killed by the sword." <note>original: In consularibus legimus Theodemerem regem Francorum filium Ricimeris quondam, & Ascilam matrem ejus, gladio interfectos.</note>

<header>Observations upon the Part I.</header>

*Chlochilo.* <note>original: Chlochilo; likely Chlodio</note> *This man, having crossed the Rhine, defeated the Romans in the Charcoal Forest <note>original: Carbonaria sylva</note>, captured and held Cambrai <note>original: Camaracum</note>, and reigned for 20 years. Under this king, the Franks advanced as far as the Somme <note>original: Summam</note>.*

*Merovechus.* <note>Merovech, the legendary founder of the Merovingian dynasty</note> *Under this king, the Franks destroyed Trier <note>original: Trevirim</note>, burned Metz <note>original: Metim</note>, and reached as far as Orléans <note>original: Aurelianum</note>.*

Now, as for *Genobaldus*, *Marcomer*, and *Suno*, they were captains of the Transrhenane Franks <term>Transrhenane Franks</term> <note>Franks living on the "other side" (east) of the Rhine river, outside Roman territory</note> during the reign of *Theodosius*, and they do not concern us here. We are to begin with *Theudomir*, the first King of the rebelling Salii <term>Salii</term> <note>the Salian Franks, a group that moved into Roman territory and eventually founded the French monarchy</note>, who is called *Didio* by *Ivo of Chartres* <note>original: Ivo Carnotensis</note>, and *Thiedo* or *Theudemerus* by *Rhenanus*. 

His likeness is preserved on a gold coin found with the inscription, **KING THEUDEMIR** <note>original: THEUDEMIR REX</note>, which was published by *Petavius* and is still—or was until recently—in existence, as *Wendelin* <note>original: Windeline</note> testifies. This coin shows that he was indeed a King and that he reigned in Gaul <note>original: Gallia</note>, since the uncivilized people of Germany did not understand the minting of money at that time, nor did they use Latin words or letters. He was the son of *Ricimer* (or *Richomer*), who was a favorite of the Emperor *Theodosius*. Because he was a "Roman Frank" <note>a Frank serving in the Roman military or administration</note> and of the Salian royal blood, the people made him King when they rebelled. 

You can find the entire timeline of his reign stated in the *Excerpts of Gregory of Tours from Fredegarius, chapters 5, 6, 7, and 8* <note>original: Excerptis Gregorii Turonensis è Fredigario</note>. These records describe his appointment as King, the tyranny of *Jovinus*, the slaughter of *Jovinus's* associates, the second capture of Trier <note>original: Triers</note> by the Franks, and their war with *Castinus*, in which

The events following the death of this King are set down in order as a series of successive occurrences:

Trevirorum civitas, factione unius ex senatoribus nomine Lucii, a Francis capta & incensa est. — Castinus Domesticorum Comes expeditionem accipit contra Francos, &c."</note>

"The Franks, searching diligently for a <term>long-haired king</term> <note>original: crinitum; the "long-haired kings" was a distinctive title for the Merovingian dynasty, symbolizing their royal power</note> chosen by themselves from the lineage of Priam, Frigus, and Francio <note>these names refer to the legendary Trojan ancestry the Franks claimed for themselves</note>—just as it had been in the past—established over themselves a king named Theudemer, the son of Ricimer, who was killed by the Romans in the battle I mentioned above" <note>original Latin: "Franci electum à se regem, sicut prius fuerat, crinitum inquirentes diligenter ex genere Priami, Frigi & Francionis, super se crearunt nomine Theudemerum filium Richemeris, qui in hoc prælio quod supra memini, à Romanis interfectus est;"</note>; that is, in the battle with Castinus’s army.

<page-num>56</page-num>

<header>Observations upon the Part I.</header>

<margin>In Bucher, book 14, chapter 9, note 8. <note>original: Apud Bucherum, l. 14. c. 9. n. 8.</note></margin>

Ordericus Vitalis <note>a 12th-century chronicler</note> mentions: "When the Gauls had previously rebelled against the Romans, the Franks allied themselves with them; and being joined together, they appointed Pharamond, the son of Duke Suno, as their king." <note>original Latin: "Cum Galli prius contra Romanos rebellâssent, Franci iis sociati sunt, & pariter juncti, Ferramundum Sunonis ducis filium, sibi regem præfecerunt."</note> Prosper <note>Prosper of Aquitaine, a 5th-century Christian writer</note> records the date: "In the 25th year of Honorius, Pharamond reigns in Gaul." <note>original Latin: "Anno 25 Honorii, Pharamundus regnat in Francia."</note>

Bucher <note>Jean-Baptiste Bucher, a Jesuit historian</note> correctly observes that this refers to the end of the year 416 or the beginning of the next year, counting the years of Emperor Honorius's reign from the death of Valentinian. He argues convincingly that at this time, Pharamond was not only king by the established customs of the Franks, but was also crowned with the consent of Honorius and was granted a portion of Gaul by a formal treaty. This may be the reason Roman writers considered him the first king. Some who did not understand this have mistakenly claimed he founded the kingdom with an army of <term>Transrhenane Franks</term> <note>Frankish tribes living across or beyond the Rhine River</note>. While he may have arrived with such an army, he succeeded Theudomir by right of blood and the consent of the people.

For the previously cited passage from Fredegar <note>original: Fredigarius; a 7th-century chronicler</note>—"When the leaders had died out, kings were again chosen from the same lineage as before" <note>original Latin: "Extinctis Ducibus, in Francis denuo Reges creantur ex eadem stirpe quâ prius fuerant"</note>—implies that the kingdom remained with this newly elected family throughout the reigns of several kings. If one calculates the years of Honorius's reign from the death of his father, Pharamond’s reign might have begun two years later than Bucher suggests. The <term>Salic laws</term> <note>original: Salique laws; the ancient Frankish civil law code</note> were established during his

<summary>The author identifies Pharamond as the first Frankish king recognized by the Romans, noting he ruled by both hereditary right and imperial treaty around the year 416-417.</summary>
