<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the legitimacy and location of the early Frankish kingdom, specifically analyzing the Salic laws and the historical records of the kings Pharamond, Clodio, and Merovech.</meta>

<page-num>57</page-num>

### Chap. 6. Prophecies of Daniel.

The laws of his reign, which still exist today, show by their name that it was the kingdom of the **Salian Franks** over which he reigned. Furthermore, the financial fines <note>original: "pecuniary mulcts"</note> specified in these laws show that the region where he ruled was wealthy in currency, and consequently must have been located within the Roman Empire; for unrefined **Germany** <note>referring to the tribes of Germania beyond the Roman frontier</note> did not know the use of money until they integrated with the **Romans**. 

In the preface to the **Salic** laws—written and attached to them shortly after the conversion of the **Franks** to the Christian religion (that is, at the end of the reign of **Merovech** <note>original: Merovæus</note>, or soon after)—the origin of this kingdom is described as follows: "For this nation, being brave and powerful in its strength, threw off the very hard yoke of the Romans from its neck by fighting, etc." <note>original Latin: "Hæc enim gens, quæ fortis dum esset & robore valida, Romanorum jugum durissimum de suis cervicibus excussit pugnando, &c."</note> This kingdom, therefore, was established not by an outside invasion but by an internal rebellion, as was described previously. **Prosper** <note>Prosper of Aquitaine, a 5th-century chronicler</note>, in listing their kings in order, tells us: "Pharamond reigns in Francia; Clodio reigns in Francia; Merovech reigns in Francia" <note>original Latin: "Pharamundus regnat in Francia; Clodio regnat in Francia; Merovæus regnat in Francia"</note>. Who can imagine that he meant anything other than the same "Francia" in all these instances? And yet it is certain that the Francia of Merovech was located within **Gaul** <note>original: Gallia</note>.

However, since the father of **Pharamond** was king of a group of **Franks** in **Germany** during the reign of the Emperor **Theodosius** (as mentioned above), Pharamond might have ruled over those same Franks in Germany before he succeeded **Theodomir** <note>original: Theudomir</note> in the kingdom...

<meta>catchword: of</meta>

<summary>Newton argues that the early Frankish kingdom was established through rebellion within Roman Gaul rather than external invasion, citing the Salic laws' use of Roman currency and early chronicles as evidence.</summary>

# Observations upon the Prophecies of Daniel, Part I.

...it continued as a distinct kingdom, freed from subjection to the Empire. The Emperor was unable to spare soldiers to be sent there to receive and keep the island and therefore neglected it, as we learn from unquestionable records. 

For **Prosper** <note>Prosper of Aquitaine, a Christian writer and chronicler</note> tells us: "In the year 410 AD, during the consulship of Varanes: At this time, because of the weakness of the Romans, the strength of Britain was completely diminished." <note>original Latin: "A. C. 410, Variane Cos. Hac tempestate præ valetudine Romanorum, vires funditùs attenuatæ Britanniæ."</note> 

And **Sigebert** <note>Sigebert of Gembloux, a medieval chronicler</note>, connecting this with the siege of Rome, says: "The strength of the Britons was diminished, and they withdrew themselves from Roman rule." <note>original Latin: "Britannorum vires attenuatæ, & subtrahunt se à Romanorum dominatione."</note> 

And **Zosimus** <note>A Greek historian writing in the late 5th century</note>, in Book 6: "The Barbarians from across the Rhine, invading all places, reduced the inhabitants of the island of Britain, and also certain Celtic nations, to such a state that they broke away from the Roman Empire. No longer being obedient to Roman laws, they lived by their own rules <note>original Greek: κατ’ εαυτὸν βιατεύειν</note>, according to their own pleasure. The Britons, therefore, took up arms and risked themselves for their own safety, freeing their cities from the threatening Barbarians. In like manner, all of **Brabant** <note>A region in the Low Countries, modern-day Belgium and the Netherlands</note> and some other provinces of the **Gauls** <note>The inhabitants of Roman France</note>, imitating the Britons, freed themselves also, ejecting the Roman governors <note>original: "presidents"</note> and forming themselves into a sort of commonwealth according to their own pleasure. This rebellion of Britain and the Celtic nations happened when **Constantine** <note>Constantine III, a Roman general who declared himself Emperor in Britain in 407 AD</note> usurped the kingdom." 

So also **Procopius** <note>A prominent 6th-century Byzantine historian</note>, in Book 1 of the *Vandal Wars*, speaking of the same Constantine,

<meta>This page continues the historical argument that Britain became permanently independent of the Roman Empire in the early 5th century. It cites several ancient and medieval historians to confirm that after the fall of the usurper Constantine III and the Goths' sack of Rome, Roman authority in the island ended, leading to a period of local rule by "tyrants" and the eventual rise of King Vortigern.</meta>

### Chapter 6. Prophecies of Daniel.

faith: *Constantine being overcome in battle, was slain with his children.* "The Romans, however, were no longer able to recover Britain; rather, from that time it remained under the rule of tyrants." <note>original Greek: Βρετανίαν μέν τοι Ῥωμαῖοι ἀνασώσασθαι οὐκέτι ἔχον· ἀλλ’ ὅσα ὑπὸ τυράννος ἀπ’ αὐτῶ ἔμενε. Newton provides his own translation immediately following this.</note> *Yet the Romans could not recover Britain any more, but from that time it remained under Tyrants.* 

And **Bede** <note>The Venerable Bede, an 8th-century English monk and historian</note>, Book 1, Chapter 11: "Rome was broken by the Goths in the year 1164 of its founding <note>1164 years from the founding of Rome, or 410 AD</note>; from which time the Romans ceased to reign in Britain." <note>original Latin: "Fracta est Roma à Gothis anno 1164 suæ conditionis; ex quo tempore Romani in Britannia regnare cessaverunt."</note> 

And **Ethelweard** <note>A 10th-century historian</note>: "From the time Rome was conquered by the Goths, the Roman empire ceased in the island of Britain, and in many other lands which they held under the yoke of servitude." <note>original Latin: "A tempore Romæ à Gothis expugnatæ, cessavit imperium Romanorum à Britannia insula, & ab aliis, quas sub jugo servitutis tenebant, multis terris."</note> 

And **Theodoret** <note>A theologian and bishop of Cyrrhus</note>, in his ninth discourse *On the Treatment of Greek Affections*, written about the year 424 AD, reckons the **Britons** among the nations which were not then in subjection to the Roman Empire. 

Thus **Sigonius** <note>Carlo Sigonio, a 16th-century Italian humanist and historian</note> writes: "At the year 411, there was no Roman empire in Britain after the death of Constantine." <note>original Latin: "ad annum 411, Imperium Romanorum post excessum Constantini in Britannia nullum fuit."</note>

Between the death of **Constantine** <note>Constantine III, a Roman general who declared himself Emperor in Britain and Gaul</note> and the reign of **Vortigern** <note>A 5th-century warlord or king of the Britons</note> was an interregnum of about 14 years. During this time, the Britons had wars with the **Picts** and **Scots** <note>Tribes from modern-day Scotland and Ireland who raided Roman Britain</note> and twice obtained the assistance of a Roman Legion. These soldiers drove out the enemy, but they told the Britons positively at their departure that they would come no more. 

Regarding the beginning of Vortigern’s reign, there is this record in an old Chronicle in **Nennius** <note>A Welsh monk traditionally credited with the *Historia Brittonum*</note>, quoted by **Camden** <note>William Camden, a famous Elizabethan antiquarian</note> and others: "Vortigern held power in Britain during the consulship of Theodosius and Valentinian" <note>This identifies the year as 425 AD</note>. [namely,

->A.D. 425<-

"It is certain," he says, "that after the *Goths* set out from *Illyricum*, the *Huns* followed them and first took possession of *Pannonia*. For *Honorius*, being lacking in the strength to resist during such great difficulties, could not entirely stop them; but by better counsel, with his mind turned toward peace, he made a treaty with them, giving and receiving hostages. Among those given, *Aetius*, who had also been delivered to *Alaric*, is particularly mentioned" <note>original Latin: "Constat... quod Gothis ex Illyrico profectis, Hunni successerunt, atque imprimis Pannoniam tenuerunt. Neque enim Honorius viribus ad resistendum in tantis difficultatibus destitutus, prorsus eos prohibere potuit, sed meliore consilio, animo ad pacem converso, foedus cum eis, datis acceptisque obsidibus fecit; ex quibus qui dati sunt, Aetius, qui etiam Alarico tributus fuerat, praecipue memoratur."</note>. 

The story of how *Aetius* <note>Flavius Aetius (c. 391–454), a powerful Roman general and statesman</note> was a hostage to the *Goths* and *Huns* is told by *Frigeridus* <note>Renatus Profuturus Frigeridus, a late 5th-century historian</note>. After mentioning that *Theodosius*, Emperor of the *East*, had sent harsh commands to *John* (who had seized the crown of the *Western* Empire after the death of *Honorius*), he adds: "Moved by these things, *John* sent *Aetius*—who at that time was managing the palace affairs—to the *Huns* <note>original: Chunnos</note> with a great weight of gold. These people were known to him from the time of his own stay as a hostage and were bound to him by close friendship—" <note>original Latin: "Iis permotus Johannes, Aetium id tempus curam palatii gerentem cum ingenti auri pondere ad Chunnos transmisit, notos sibi obsidiatus sui tempore & familiari amicitia devinctos"</note>.

And a little later: "*Aetius* was a hostage of *Alaric* for three years, and then of the *Huns*; afterwards, he became the son-in-law of *Carpilio*, having been a Count of the Household <note>original: Comite domesticorum</note> and high steward <note>original: curopalatae</note> to *John*" <note>original Latin: "Aetius tribus annis Alarici obses, dehinc Chunnorum, postea Carpilionis gener ex Comite domesticorum & Joannis curopalatae."</note>.

Now the scholar *Bucher* <note>Jean-Denis Bucher, a 17th-century Jesuit chronologer</note> shows that *Aetius* was a hostage to *Alaric* until the year 410, when *Alaric* died, and to the *Huns* between the years 411 and 415. He became the son-in-law to *Carpilio* around the year 417 or 418, and served as the <term>Curopalates</term> <note>A high-ranking Byzantine/Roman court official</note> to *John* around the end of the year 423. Using their friendship and aid, he obtained peace from the emperors for his interrupted authority." <note>original Latin: "Ætio & Valerio Coff. Ætius depositâ potestate profugus ad Hunnos in Pannonia pervenit, quorum amicitiâ auxilioque usus, pacem principum interpellatae potestatis obtinuit."</note> 

From this, it appears that at this time Rugila (or as Maximus <note>Maximus of Turin</note> calls him, Rechilla) reigned over the Huns in Pannonia. It also shows that Pannonia was no longer even considered part of the territory of the Empire, having been previously granted to the Huns. Furthermore, these were the very same group of Huns with whom Aetius had formed a friendship during his time as a hostage. By virtue of this friendship, just as he had previously asked them for help to support John the Tyrant <note>An usurper who seized the Western throne from 423–425 AD</note> in 424 AD, he now obtained their intervention on his own behalf with the Emperor. Around that time, the *Burgundians* <note>A Germanic tribe that settled in Gaul</note>, having been recently troubled by the *Huns* <note>original: Hunns</note>, received word of *Octar’s* death. Seeing the Huns without a leader, they attacked them suddenly with such vigor that 3,000 *Burgundians* killed 10,000 *Huns*. 

You have already heard that *Rugila* <note>A king of the Huns and uncle to Attila</note> was then King in *Pannonia* <note>A Roman province in the modern-day Balkans/Hungary</note>. He died in 433 AD <note>original: A. C. 433</note> and was succeeded by *Bleda*, as the chroniclers *Prosper* and *Maximus* inform us. This *Bleda*, along with his brother *Attila*, had previously been Kings of the *Huns* beyond the *Danube*, as their father *Munzuc’s* kingdom was divided between them; now they united the kingdom of *Pannonia* with their own. 

For this reason, *Paulus Diaconus* <note>Paul the Deacon, an 8th-century historian</note> says they "ruled a kingdom within Pannonia and Dacia" <note>original: *regnum intra Pannoniam Daciamque gerere*</note>. In the year 441, they began to invade the Empire once more, adding new and massive armies from *Scythia* <note>A vast region of Central Eurasia</note> to the *Pannonian* forces. However, this war was quickly resolved <note>original: presently composed</note>. 

Then *Attila*, seeing that *Bleda* was inclined toward peace, killed him in 444 AD, inherited his dominions, and invaded the Empire again. At length, after various major wars with the *Romans*, *Attila* died in 454 AD. His sons quarreled over his dominions, which gave the *Gepids* <note>original: Gepides</note>, *Ostrogoths*, and other nations who were their subjects the opportunity to rebel and wage war against them. That same year, the *Ostrogoths* were granted territories <note>original: seats</note> in *Pannonia* by the Emperors *Marcian* and *Valentinian*; and with the *Romans*...

After the death of *Vinethar*, the son of *Hermaneric* <note>Ermanaric, a powerful king of the Ostrogoths who died around 376 AD</note>, the *Ostrogoths* lived without kings of their own nation for about forty years while being subject to the *Huns* <note>original: Hunns</note>. And when *Attila* <note>The OCR text reads "Alaric," but the date 444 and the names Valamir, Theodomir, and Videmir confirm the author is referring to Attila the Hun</note> began to make war upon the *Romans* in the year 444, he appointed *Valamir* and his brothers *Theodomir* and *Videmir*—the grandsons of *Vinethar*—as captains or kings of these *Ostrogoths* under his authority. In the twelfth year of *Valamir's* reign, counting from that time, the *Huns* were driven out of *Pannonia* <note>A Roman province located in modern-day Hungary and surrounding regions</note>.

Yet the *Huns* were not so completely expelled that they did not have further conflicts with the *Romans*, until the head of *Dengizich* <note>original: Densix</note>, the son of *Attila*, was carried to *Constantinople* in 469 AD, during the consulship of *Zeno* and *Marcian*, as the historian *Marcellinus* relates. Nor were they yet totally ejected from the Empire; for besides those remaining in *Pannonia*, the historian *Sigonius* <note>Carlo Sigonio, a 16th-century Italian scholar</note> tells us that when the Emperors *Marcian* and *Valentinian* granted *Pannonia* to the *Goths* in the year 454, they also granted part of *Illyricum* <note>A region on the eastern coast of the Adriatic Sea</note> to some of the *Huns* and *Sarmatians* <note>An ancient nomadic people from the Eurasian steppes</note>. And in the year 526, when the *Lombards* <note>A Germanic people</note> moved into *Pannonia* and made war there with the *Gepids* <note>original: Gepides</note>, the *Avars* <note>original: Avares</note>—a group of *Huns* who had taken the name "Avars" from one of their kings—assisted the *Lombards* in that war. The *Lombards* afterwards, when they moved into *Italy*, left their lands in *Pannonia* to the *Avars* as a reward for their friendship.

### Chap. 6. Prophecies of Daniel.

From that time the Huns <note>original: Hunns</note> grew again very powerful; their Kings, whom they called Khagan <note>original: Chagan; a title for a supreme ruler among Steppe peoples, similar to "Great Khan"</note>, troubled the Empire much in the reigns of the Emperors Maurice <note>original: Mauritius; reigned 582–602 AD</note>, Phocas <note>reigned 602–610 AD</note>, and Heraclius <note>reigned 610–641 AD</note>. This is the origin of the present kingdom of *Hungary*, which from these Avars <note>original: Avares</note> and other Huns mixed together, took the name of *Hun-Avaria*, and by contraction, *Hungary*.

9. The *Lombards*, before they came over the *Danube*, were commanded by two captains, *Ibor* and *Ayon*. After their deaths, they had Kings: *Agilmund*, *Lamisso*, *Lechu*, *Hildehoc*, *Gudehoc*, *Classo*, *Tato*, *Wacho*, *Walter*, *Audoin*, *Alboin*, *Cleophis*, and others. *Agilmund* was the son of *Ayon*, and became their King, according to Prosper <note>Prosper of Aquitaine, a 5th-century chronicler</note>, during the Consulship of *Honorius* and *Theodosius* in 389 AD <note>original: A. C. 389; Anno Christi</note>. He reigned thirty-three years according to Paul the Deacon <note>original: Paulus Warnefridus; an 8th-century historian of the Lombards</note>, and was slain in battle by the *Bulgarians*. Prosper places his death in the Consulship of *Marinianus* and *Asclepiodorus*, in 423 AD. *Lamisso* routed the *Bulgarians* and reigned three years, and *Lechu* reigned almost forty. *Gudehoc* was a contemporary of *Odoacer*, King of the *Heruli* in *Italy*, and led his people from *Pannonia* into *Rugia*, a country on the north side of *Noricum* <note>A Roman province in what is now Austria and Slovenia</note> next beyond the *Danube*; from where *Odoacer* then carried his people into *Italy*. *Tato* overthrew the kingdom of the *Heruli* beyond the *Danube*.

### Chapter 6. Prophecies of Daniel.

From that time the **Huns** <note>original: Hunns</note> grew very powerful again; their Kings, whom they called the **Khagan** <note>original: Chagan; a title for a supreme ruler among Turkic and Mongolic peoples</note>, troubled the Empire significantly during the reigns of the Emperors **Maurice** <note>original: Mauritius</note>, **Phocas**, and **Heraclius**. This is the origin of the present kingdom of **Hungary**, which took the name **Hun-Avaria**—and by contraction, **Hungary**—from these **Avars** <note>original: Avares</note> and other **Huns** mixing together.

9. The **Lombards**, before they crossed the **Danube**, were led by two captains, **Ibor** and **Ayon**. After their deaths, they were ruled by kings: **Agilmund**, **Lamisso**, **Lechu**, **Hildehoc**, **Gudehoc**, **Claffo**, **Tato**, **Wacko**, **Walter**, **Audoin**, **Alboin**, **Cleophis**, and others. **Agilmund** was the son of **Ayon**, who became their King—according to **Prosper** <note>Prosper of Aquitaine, a 5th-century Christian chronicler</note>—during the consulship of **Honorius** and **Theodosius** in 389 AD <note>original: A. C. 389; Anno Christi</note>. He reigned for thirty-three years, according to **Paul the Deacon** <note>original: Paulus Warnefridus, an 8th-century historian of the Lombards</note>, and was killed in battle by the **Bulgarians**. **Prosper** records his death during the consulship of **Marinianus** and **Asclepiodorus** in 423 AD. **Lamisso** defeated the **Bulgarians** and reigned for three years, and **Lechu** reigned for almost forty. **Gudehoc** was a contemporary of **Odoacer**, King of the **Heruli** in **Italy**, and led his people from **Pannonia** into **Rugia**, a country on the north side of **Noricum** <note>a Roman province in the Eastern Alps, primarily modern-day Austria</note> just beyond the **Danube**; from there, **Odoacer** then led his people into **Italy**. **Tato** destroyed the kingdom of the **Heruli** beyond the **Danube**.

<header>Observations upon the Part I.</header>

**Wacho** conquered the **Suebi** <note>original: Suevians; a Germanic people who migrated into the Roman Empire</note>, a kingdom then bounded on the east by **Bavaria**, on the west by **France**, and on the south by the **Burgundians**. **Audoin** returned to **Pannonia** <note>a Roman province covering parts of modern-day Hungary, Austria, and the Balkans</note> in 526 AD <note>original: A. C. 526; Anno Christi</note>, and there defeated the **Gepids** <note>original: Gepides; an East Germanic tribe related to the Goths</note>. In 551 AD, **Alboin** overthrew the kingdom of the **Gepids** and killed their King **Cunimund** <note>original: Chunnimund</note>. In 563 AD, he assisted the **Byzantine** <note>original: Greek</note> Emperor against **Totila**, King of the **Ostrogoths** in **Italy**. Finally, in 568 AD, he led his people out of **Pannonia** into **Lombardy**, where they reigned until the year 774.

According to **Paul the Deacon** <note>original: Paulus Diaconus; an 8th-century historian of the Lombards</note>, the **Lombards**, along with many other **Gothic** nations, entered the Empire from beyond the **Danube** during the reigns of **Arcadius** and **Honorius**—that is, between the years 395 and 408. However, they might have arrived a little earlier. We are told that the **Lombards**, under their captains **Ibor** and **Ayon**, defeated the **Vandals** in battle. **Prosper** <note>Prosper of Aquitaine, a 5th-century chronicler</note> places this victory during the consulship of **Ausonius** and **Olybrius**, which was in 379 AD. Before this war, the **Vandals** had remained peaceful for forty years in the settlements granted to them in **Pannonia** by **Constantine the Great**. Therefore, if these were the same **Vandals**, this war must have taken place in **Pannonia**. It might have been caused by the **Lombards** crossing the **Danube** into **Pannonia** a year or two before the battle, thus ending the peace that had lasted forty years.

# Chap. 6. Prophecies of Daniel.

...years. After **Gratian** and **Theodosius** had quieted the **Barbarians**, those tribes might have either retired across the **Danube** or remained peaceful under **Roman** rule until the death of **Theodosius**. At that point, they either invaded the Empire anew or threw off all subjection to it. Based on their wars—first with the **Vandals** and then with the **Bulgarians** (a **Scythian** nation named after the **Volga** River from which they originated)—it appears that even in those days they were a kingdom of significant power <note>original: "not contemptible"</note>.

10. With these nine kingdoms having been torn away, we must next consider the remainder <note>original: "residue"</note> of the **Western Empire**. While this Empire remained whole, it was the "Beast" itself <note>A reference to the symbolic Beast in the biblical prophecies of Daniel and Revelation</note>; however, its remainder is considered only a part of it. Now, if this part is viewed as a "horn" <note>In biblical prophecy, "horns" on a beast represent individual kings or kingdoms that emerge from an empire</note>, the reign of this horn may be dated from the transfer <note>original: "translation"</note> of the imperial seat from **Rome** to **Ravenna**, which occurred in October 408 AD <note>original: "A. C. 408"; Anno Christi, or "In the year of Christ"</note>. 

At that time, Emperor **Honorius** feared that **Alaric** <note>the King of the Visigoths</note> would besiege him in **Rome** if he stayed there, so he retreated to **Milan** <note>original: "Millain"</note> and then to **Ravenna**. The subsequent siege and sacking of **Rome** confirmed his residence there, and he and his successors made it their home from then on. Accordingly, **Machiavelli** <note>original: "Macchiavel"; the Italian Renaissance political philosopher</note> writes in his *History of Florence* <note>original: "Florentine history"</note> that **Valentinian** left **Rome** and moved the seat of the Empire to **Ravenna**.

# Observations upon the Prophecies of Daniel, Part I.

**Wacho** conquered the **Suebi** <note>original: "Suevians"</note>, a kingdom then bordered on the east by **Bavaria**, on the west by **France**, and on the south by the **Burgundians**. **Audoin** returned to **Pannonia** <note>An ancient Roman province located in the modern-day border regions of Hungary, Austria, and the Balkans</note> in 526 AD <note>original: "A. C." or "Anno Christi"</note>, and there he defeated the **Gepids** <note>original: "Gepides"</note>. In 551 AD, **Alboin** overthrew the kingdom of the **Gepids** and killed their King **Chunnimund**. In 563 AD, he assisted the **Byzantine** <note>original: "Greek"</note> Emperor against **Totila**, King of the **Ostrogoths**, in **Italy**; and in 568 AD, he led his people out of **Pannonia** into **Lombardy**, where they ruled until the year 774.

According to **Paul the Deacon** <note>original: "Paulus Diaconus," an eighth-century historian of the Lombards</note>, the **Lombards**, along with many other **Gothic** nations, entered the Empire from beyond the **Danube** during the reigns of **Arcadius** and **Honorius**—that is, between the years 395 and 408 AD. However, they might have arrived a little earlier; for we are told that the **Lombards**, under their leaders <note>original: "captains"</note> **Ibor** and **Ayon**, defeated the **Vandals** in battle. The chronicler **Prosper** places this victory in the consulship of **Ausonius** and **Olybrius**, which was in 379 AD. Before this war, the **Vandals** had remained peaceful for forty years in the territories granted to them in **Pannonia** by **Constantine the Great**. Therefore, if these were the same **Vandals**, this war must have taken place in **Pannonia**. It might have been caused by the **Lombards** crossing the **Danube** into **Pannonia** a year or two before the battle, thereby ending the peace that had lasted forty 

<meta>Catchword: years.</meta>

<summary>This page traces the early migrations and military victories of the Lombards and their leaders, such as Alboin and Wacho, as they moved from Pannonia into Italy. It also attempts to reconcile different historical accounts regarding the exact timing of their entry into the Roman Empire and their initial conflicts with the Vandals.</summary>

### Chapter 6: Prophecies of Daniel

...years. After *Gratian* and *Theodosius* had pacified the *Barbarians*, the tribes might have either retreated across the *Danube* or remained peaceful under the *Romans* until the death of *Theodosius*. At that point, they either invaded the Empire again or completely rejected Roman authority. Based on their wars—first with the *Vandals* and then with the *Bulgarians* (a *Scythian* nation named after the *Volga* river from which they originated)—it is clear that even then they were a significant kingdom.

10. Now that these nine kingdoms have been torn away, we must next consider the remains <note>original: "residue"</note> of the *Western Empire*. While this Empire remained whole, it represented the "Beast" itself; however, the remaining portion is only a part of it. If we consider this part as a "horn" <note>In the Book of Daniel, horns represent kings or kingdoms emerging from a great empire</note>, its reign can be dated from the relocation <note>original: "translation"</note> of the imperial capital from *Rome* to *Ravenna* in *October 408 AD* <note>original: "A. C." or Anno Christi</note>. At that time, Emperor *Honorius*—fearing that *Alaric* <note>The King of the Visigoths</note> would besiege him in *Rome* if he stayed there—fled to *Milan* <note>original: "Millain"</note> and then to *Ravenna*. The subsequent siege and sacking of *Rome* confirmed his decision to stay there, so he and his successors made it their permanent home from then on. Similarly, *Machiavelli* <note>original: "Macchiavel"</note> writes in his *History of Florence* that *Valentinian*, having left *Rome*, moved the seat of the Empire to *Ravenna*.

<meta>catchword: Raetia</meta>

<summary>Newton identifies the tenth "horn" of the prophetic Beast as the remaining portion of the Western Roman Empire after its initial fragmentation, dating its distinct identity from the relocation of the capital to Ravenna in 408 AD.</summary>

# Observations upon the Prophecies of Daniel: Part I.

**Raetia** <note>A Roman province comprising parts of modern-day Switzerland, Austria, and southern Germany</note> belonged to the **Western Emperors** as long as that Empire stood. It then passed, along with **Italy** and the **Roman Senate**, to **Odoacer**, King of the **Heruli** in Italy, and after him to **Theodoric**, King of the **Ostrogoths**, and his successors, by the grant of the **Greek Emperors** <note>The Eastern Roman or Byzantine Emperors</note>. Upon the death of **Valentinian III** <note>original: "Valentinian the second"; historical records show Valentinian III died in 455 AD</note>, the **Alamanni** and **Suebi** <note>original: "Alemans and Suevians"</note> invaded **Raetia** in 455 AD <note>original: "A. C." or Anno Christi</note>. However, I do not find that they established any settled kingdom there; for in the year 457, while they were still plundering **Raetia**, they were attacked and defeated by **Burto**, Master of the Horse to the Emperor **Majorian**. After this, I hear nothing more of them invading **Raetia**.

**Clovis** <note>original: "Clodovæus"</note>, King of the **Franks** <note>original: "France"</note>, in or about the year 496, conquered a kingdom belonging to the **Alamanni** and killed their last king, **Ermeric**. But this kingdom was located in **Germany** and only bordered upon **Raetia**. Its people fled from **Clovis** into the neighboring kingdom of the **Ostrogoths** under **Theodoric**, who received them as friends and wrote a diplomatic letter to **Clovis** on their behalf. By this means, they became inhabitants of **Raetia** as subjects under the dominion of the **Ostrogoths**.

When the **Greek Emperor** conquered the **Ostrogoths**, he succeeded them in the **Kingdom of Ravenna**, not only by right of conquest but also by right of inheritance, the **Roman Senate**

<meta>catchword: still</meta>

<summary>Newton traces the political control of the province of Raetia from the Western Roman Empire through the Heruli, Ostrogoths, and Alamanni, eventually leading to Byzantine (Greek) rule. This historical timeline is used to identify the shifting powers that made up the fragments of the Roman Empire.</summary>

<keywords>Raetia, Odoacer, Theodoric the Great, Ostrogoths, Alamanni, Clovis I, Majorian, Ravenna, Byzantine Empire, Western Roman Empire</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the list of the ten kingdoms arising from the Western Roman Empire, concluding with the role of the Roman Senate and the Exarchate of Ravenna in maintaining imperial continuity after the Goth invasions.</meta>

<page-num>73</page-num>
<header>Chapter 6: Prophecies of Daniel.</header>

...still aligned with this kingdom. Therefore, we may conclude that this kingdom continued through the Exarchate <term>a province governed by an "exarch," or viceroy, of the Byzantine Empire</term> of *Ravenna* and the Senate of *Rome*. The remainder of the *Western Empire* followed the Senate of *Rome*, because of the right which this Senate still held—and eventually exercised—to choose a new *Western Emperor*.

I have now listed the ten kingdoms into which the *Western Empire* was divided during its initial collapse—that is, at the time *Rome* was besieged and captured by the *Goths*. Some of these kingdoms eventually fell and new ones emerged; however, regardless of their actual number later on, they are still called the *Ten Kings* <note>This refers to the "ten horns" of the fourth beast in the biblical Book of Daniel, which Newton interprets as ten distinct kingdoms.</note> based on their original number.

## C H A P. VII.

### *Of the eleventh horn of Daniel’s fourth Beast.*

<margin>Chapter 7:8.</margin> NOW *Daniel considered the horns, and behold, there came up among them another horn, before which three of the first horns were plucked up by the roots. And behold, in this horn were eyes like the eyes of a man, and a mouth speaking great things—and its look*
<margin>Verses 20, 21.</margin> *was more imposing <note>original: "stout"</note> than its companions—and the same horn made war with the saints, and prevailed against them. And one who stood by and explained the interpretation of these things to Daniel,*
<margin>Verse 24.</margin> *told him that the ten horns were ten kings that should arise, and another should arise after them and be different from the first, and he should subdue three kings and speak great words*
<margin>Verse 25.</margin> *against the Most High, and wear out the saints, and think to change times and laws. And they should be given into his hands until a "time and times and half a time" <note>In biblical prophecy, this phrase is often interpreted as three and a half years, representing 1,260 days—or, in many historicist interpretations, 1,260 years.</note>.* "Kings" are used here to represent kingdoms, as established above; and therefore, the little horn is a little kingdom. It was a horn of the fourth Beast <note>The "fourth beast" in the Book of Daniel is historically identified as the Roman Empire.</note> and uprooted three of its first horns;

<page-num>75</page-num>
<header>Chapter 7: Prophecies of Daniel</header>

And therefore we are to look for it among the nations of the *Latin* Empire <note>The Western Roman Empire.</note>, after the rise of the ten horns. But it was a kingdom of a different kind from the other ten kingdoms, having a life or soul peculiar to itself, with eyes and a mouth. By its eyes, it was a Seer; and by its mouth speaking great things and changing times and laws, it was a Prophet as well as a King. And such a Seer, a Prophet, and a King, is the Church of *Rome*.

A Seer, an overseer <note>original Greek: Ἐπίσκοπος (Episkopos)</note>, is a Bishop in the literal sense of the word; and this Church claims the universal bishopric.

With his mouth, he gives laws to kings and nations as an Oracle; and claims <note>original: "pretends to," which in this period meant "to lay claim to" rather than "to feign."</note> Infallibility, and that his dictates are binding to the whole world; which is to be a Prophet in the highest degree.

In the eighth century, by uprooting and subduing the Exarchate of *Ravenna* <note>A lordship in Italy held by the Byzantine Empire until 751 AD.</note>, the kingdom of the *Lombards*, and the Senate and Dukedom of *Rome*, he acquired *Peter's* Patrimony <note>The "Patrimony of St. Peter" refers to the land and secular power held by the Pope, forming the basis of the Papal States.</note> out of their dominions; and thereby rose up as a temporal Prince or King, or a "horn" of the fourth Beast.

In a small book printed at *Paris* AD <note>original: "A. C." (Anno Christi)</note> 1689, entitled, *An Historical Dissertation on Some Coins of Charles the Great <note>Charlemagne</note>, Louis the Pious, Lothar, and Their Successors Stamped at Rome,*

<page-num>76</page-num>
# Observations upon the Prophecies of Daniel, Part 1.

It is recorded that in the days of Pope Leo X, there remained in the Vatican—and was exposed to public view until those days—an inscription in honor of Pepin <note>original: Pipin</note>, the father of Charlemagne <note>original: Charles the Great</note>, in these words:

> "That Pepin the Pious was the first who opened a way to the grandeur of the Church of Rome, by conferring upon her the Exarchate of Ravenna and many other offerings." <note>original Latin: *Pipinum pium, primum fuisse qui amplificandæ Ecclesiæ Romanæ viam aperuerit, Exarchatu Ravennate, & plurimis aliis oblatis*</note>

In and before the reign of the Emperors Gratian and Theodosius, the Bishop of Rome lived splendidly; but this was through the offerings <note>original: oblations</note> of Roman ladies, as the historian Ammianus <note>Ammianus Marcellinus, a 4th-century Roman historian</note> describes. After those reigns, Italy was invaded by foreign nations and did not escape its troubles until the fall of the Kingdom of the Lombards. It was certainly through the victory of the See of Rome over the Greek <note>Byzantine</note> Emperor, the King of the Lombards, and the Senate of Rome that the Church acquired the Patrimony of St. Peter <note>The land and secular power held by the Pope</note> and rose to its great power. 

The "Donation of Constantine" the Great <note>A forged Roman imperial decree by which the 4th-century emperor Constantine the Great supposedly transferred authority over Rome and the western part of the Roman Empire to the Pope</note> is a fiction, as is the donation of the Cottian Alps <note>original: Alpes Cottiæ</note> to the Pope by Aripert, King of the Lombards. In reality, the Cottian Alps were a part of the Exarchate <note>A territory in Italy governed by the Byzantine Empire</note> and, in the days of Aripert, belonged to the Greek Emperor.

<summary>This page analyzes the historical origins of the Papacy's territorial power, identifying Pepin the Short's 8th-century conquests as the true source of the "Patrimony of St. Peter" while dismissing earlier legendary claims like the "Donation of Constantine."</summary>

# Chapter 7: Prophecies of Daniel

<margin>Sigonius, *On the Kingdom of Italy*, for the year 726.</margin>

The practice of praying to the dead <note>original: invocation of the dead</note> and the veneration of their images was gradually introduced during the 4th, 5th, 6th, and 7th centuries. In 711 or 712 AD <note>original: A.C.</note>, the Greek <note>Byzantine</note> Emperor *Philippicus* spoke out against the latter. To put a stop to image worship, the Emperor *Leo the Isaurian* <note>Leo III</note> called a meeting of counselors and bishops in his palace in 726 AD. Following their advice, he issued an edict against that worship and wrote to Pope *Gregory II* suggesting that a general council should be convened. 

However, the Pope instead called a council at *Rome*, confirmed the worship of images, excommunicated the Greek Emperor, released the people from their allegiance, and forbade them to pay taxes <note>original: tribute</note> or otherwise obey him. Consequently, the people of *Rome*, *Campania*, *Ravenna*, and *Pentapolis* <note>A territory on the Adriatic coast of Italy</note>, along with the cities under their jurisdiction, revolted and attacked their magistrates. They killed the Exarch <note>A provincial governor of the Byzantine Empire</note> *Paul* at *Ravenna* and removed *Peter*, the Duke of *Rome*, who had become blind. 

When *Exhileratus*, the Duke of *Campania*, incited the people against the Pope, the Romans invaded *Campania* and killed him along with his son *Hadrian*. Then a new Exarch, *Eutychius*, arriving at Naples, secretly sent agents to assassinate the Pope and the nobles of *Rome*. But when the plot was discovered, the Romans revolted completely from the Greek Emperor and took an oath to preserve the life of the

From this point forward, the authority of the Senate in civil affairs was absolute, while the authority of the Pope extended no further than to the affairs of the Church.

By releasing Childeric’s subjects from their oath of allegiance, the Pope gave the kingdom to *Pepin* <note>Pepin the Short</note>, the Mayor of the Palace, and thereby gained a new and powerful friend.

### Chapter 7: Prophecies of Daniel

[...to take] the Exarchate <note>The territory governed by the Byzantine representative in Italy</note> and *Pentapolis* <note>A region of five cities on the Adriatic coast</note> from the *Lombards*, and give it to *St. Peter* <note>Meaning the Papacy</note>. Accordingly, *Pepin* <note>original: Pipin</note> came into *Italy* with an army in 754 AD <note>original: A. C. (Anno Christi)</note> and forced *Aistulphus*, King of the *Lombards*, to promise the surrender. However, the next year *Aistulphus*, seeking revenge against the Pope, instead besieged the city of *Rome*. 

Consequently, the Pope sent letters to *Pepin*, telling him that if he did not come quickly to fight the *Lombards*, he would be excommunicated "by the power given to him [the Pope], and would be alienated from the kingdom of God and eternal life" <note>original Latin: *pro data sibi potentia, alienandum fore à regno Dei & vita æterna*</note>. *Pepin*, fearing a revolt of his subjects and being indebted to the Church of *Rome*, quickly returned to *Italy* with an army. He raised the siege, trapped the *Lombards* in *Pavia*, and forced them to surrender the Exarchate and the region of *Pentapolis* to the Pope to be held as a perpetual possession. 

In this way, the Pope became the Lord of *Ravenna* and the Exarchate, with the exception of a few cities. The keys to these cities were sent to *Rome* and laid upon the "confession of *St. Peter*"—that is, upon his tomb at the high altar—"as a sign of true and perpetual dominion, granted by the King’s free piety" <note>original Latin: *in signum veri perpetuique dominii, sed pietate Regis gratuita*</note>, as the inscription on one of *Pepin’s* coins states. This occurred in the year 755 AD. From this point forward, the Popes, now being temporal <note>Secular or political</note> Princes, stopped recording the regnal years of the *Greek* <note>Byzantine</note> Emperors in their letters and official decrees <note>original: Epistles and Bulls</note>, as they had always done before.

After this, the *Lombards* invaded the Pope's territories. Pope *Adrian* sent for *Charlemagne* <note>original: Charles the Great</note>, the son and successor of *Pepin*, to come to his assistance. Accordingly, *Charles* entered *Italy* with an army and invaded the *Lombards*. He overthrew their kingdom and became master of their territories. He restored to the Pope not only what they had taken from him but also the rest of the *Exarchate* <note>The administrative lands formerly held by the Byzantine Empire in Italy</note>. The *Lombards* had promised *Pepin* they would surrender this area but had kept it until then. 

He also gave the Pope several *Lombard* cities. In return, *Charles* was named *Patricius* <note>Patrician, a title of high rank and protection</note> by the *Romans*, and he was granted the authority to confirm the elections of the Popes. These events took place in the years 773 and 774. This kingdom of the *Lombards* was the second kingdom to fall before the "little horn" <note>A reference to the biblical prophecy in the Book of Daniel, which Newton interprets as the rise of the Papacy</note>. However, *Rome*, which was intended to be the seat of his kingdom, was not yet the Pope's own property.

<margin>Sigonius, *On the Kingdom of Italy*, year 796.</margin>
In the year 796, after *Leo III* was made Pope, he announced his election to *Charlemagne* through his legates <note>Papal envoys</note>. As a gift, he sent the golden keys of the Confession of *St. Peter* <note>The tomb of St. Peter</note> and the Banner of the city of *Rome*. The keys were sent as an acknowledgment that the Pope held the cities of the *Exarchate* and *Lombardy* through the grant of *Charles*. The banner was a sign that *Charles* should come and subdue the Senate and people of *Rome*, just as he had done with the *Exarchate* and the

The next year, *Charles* himself went to *Rome*, and upon an appointed day, he presided over a Council of *Italian* and *French* Bishops to hear both parties.

<margin>See Anastasius <note>original: *Vide Anastasium*. Refers to the *Liber Pontificalis*, often attributed at the time to the 9th-century historian Anastasius Bibliothecarius.</note></margin>
But when the Pope’s adversaries expected to be heard, the Council declared that he who was the supreme judge of all men was above being judged by anyone other than himself. Thereupon, he made a solemn declaration of his innocence before all the people, and by doing so, he was considered acquitted.

Soon after, on *Christmas Day*, the people of *Rome*—who until then had elected their Bishop and believed that they and their Senate inherited the rights of the ancient Senate and people of *Rome*—voted *Charles* their Emperor. They subjected themselves to him in the same way that the old *Roman Empire* and their Senate were subjected to the old *Roman Emperors*. The Pope crowned him, anointed him with holy oil, and worshiped him on his knees after the manner of adoring the old <note>The text likely continues to describe the "old Roman Emperors." This moment marks the birth of the Holy Roman Empire.</note>

But hearing that his new laws were not being observed by the judges in delivering the law, nor by the people in obeying it; and that the noblemen were taking servants from among free men and from the Churches and Monasteries to labor in their vineyards, fields, pastures, and houses, and continued to demand cattle and wine from them and to oppress those who served the Churches: he wrote to his son *Pepin* <note>original: "Pipin"</note> to remedy these abuses, to take care of the Church, and see that his laws were enforced.

It is also noteworthy that **Charlemagne** <note>original: "Charles the great"</note>, between the years 775 and 796, conquered all of **Germany** from the **Rhine** and **Danube** rivers northward to the **Baltic Sea**, and eastward to the **Tisza** <note>original: "Teis"</note> River. He also extended his conquests into **Spain** as far as the **Ebro** River. Through these conquests, he laid the foundation of the new Empire. At the same time, he spread the **Roman Catholic** religion throughout all his conquered territories, forcing the **Saxons** and **Hunns**—who were heathens—to receive the **Roman** faith. He divided his northern conquests into bishoprics, granting tithes to the clergy and **Peter's Pence** <note>A tax of a penny per household paid to the Papacy.</note> to the Pope. By all these means, the **Church of Rome** was significantly expanded, enriched, exalted, and established.

In the previously mentioned study <note>original: "dissertation"</note> regarding some coins of **Charlemagne**, **Louis the Pious** <note>original: "Ludovicus Pius"</note>, **Lothair** <note>original: "Lotharius"</note>, and their successors—which were minted in **Rome**—there is a drawing <note>original: "draught"</note> of a mosaic that Pope **Leo III** commissioned for his palace near the **Church of St. John Lateran**. This mosaic was created to commemorate his sending of the skillfully crafted <note>original: "curiously wrought"</note> standard or banner of the city of **Rome** to **Charlemagne**. This mosaic still remained there at the time of the publication of—

This monument gives the title of "King" to **Charles**, which proves it was erected before he was crowned Emperor <note>Charlemagne was crowned Holy Roman Emperor in 800 AD.</note>. It was created at the time when **Peter** was symbolically bestowing the **pallium** on the Pope and the Pope was sending the city's banner to **Charles**—specifically, in the year 796 AD. The words above—*Most Holy Our Lord Leo, Pope, to Our Lord King Charles*—relate to the official message being sent. The words below—*Blessed Peter, grant life to Pope Leo, and grant victory to King Charles*—are a prayer that, in this political and military undertaking, God would preserve the Pope's life and give the King victory over the **Romans** <note>Referring to the volatile factions within Rome that had previously attacked the Pope.</note>. 

The three keys in **Peter's** lap signify the keys to the three parts of his "Patrimony" <term>The "Patrimony of St. Peter" refers to the land and territories under the direct temporal rule of the Pope.</term>: 
1. That of **Rome** with its Duchy, which the Pope claimed and was currently conquering.
2. Those of **Ravenna** with the **Exarchate** <note>The Exarchate of Ravenna was a center of Byzantine power in Italy that had recently fallen.</note>.
3. And those of the territories...

<header>Observations upon the Part I.</header>

## CHAPTER 8.

### Of the power of the eleventh horn of Daniel’s fourth Beast to change times and laws.
<note>In the Book of Daniel, Chapter 7, a "little horn" (which Newton calls the eleventh) emerges from the fourth beast. Newton interprets this horn as the Papacy, which gains power over the other "horns" or kingdoms of Europe.</note>

In the reign of the Greek Emperor **Justinian**, and again in the reign of **Phocas**, the Bishop of Rome obtained some dominion over the **Greek Churches**, but it did not last for long. His permanent dominion was only over the nations of the **Western Empire**, represented by **Daniel’s** fourth Beast. And this jurisdiction was established by the following edict from the Emperors **Gratian** and **Valentinian**:

<margin>See the *Annals* of Baronius <note>Caesar Baronius (1538–1607) was a Cardinal and historian who wrote the "Annales Ecclesiastici."</note>, the year 381 AD, Section 6.</margin>

> "We desire that whoever has been condemned by the judgment of **Damasus** <note>Damasus I, Pope from 366 to 384 AD.</note>—which he held with a council of five or seven bishops—or by the judgment or council of those who are Catholics, if he should unjustly wish to retain his church, as one who through defiance did not attend the priestly judgment: that by the authority of the illustrious **Praetorian Prefects of Gaul and Italy** <term>High-ranking administrative officials in the late Roman Empire.</term>, he be sent back to the episcopal judgment; or be brought to the city of **Rome** under escort by the **Consulars** or **Vicars** <term>Types of provincial governors and administrative deputies.</term>. Or if in more distant parts... <note>original Latin: "Volumus ut quicunque judicio Damasi, quod ille cum Concilio quinque vel ſeptem habuerit Epiſcoporum, vel eorum qui Catholici ſunt judicio vel Concilio condemnatus fuerit, ſi juſte voluerit Eccleſiam retentare, ut qui ad ſacerdotale judicium per contumeliam non ivisſet: ut ab illuſtribus viris Præfectis Prætorio Galliæ atque Italiæ, authoritate adhibita, ad Epiſcopale judicium remittatur, ſive à Conſularibus vel Vicariis, ut ad Urbem Romam ſub proſecutione perveniat. Aut ſi in longinquioribus partibus..."</note>

<meta>The text ends mid-sentence with the catchword "alicu-" suggesting the Latin "alicujus" (of someone) or "aliquis" (someone) follows on the next page.</meta>

<summary>This page explores the legal origins of the Pope's authority in Western Europe, arguing that his power was formalized through imperial edicts by Gratian and Valentinian rather than just religious tradition.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the legal edicts that established the Pope's authority over the Western Church. <page-num>91</page-num>
<header>Chapter 8. Prophecies of Daniel.</header>

> ...if anyone’s stubbornness should emerge in such a way that he refuses to attend judgment, the investigation of his entire case should be brought before the Metropolitan Bishop of that same province. Or if he himself is a Metropolitan, he must necessarily go to Rome without delay, or to those whom the Roman Bishop has appointed as judges. But if the injustice or favoritism of a Metropolitan Bishop or of any priest is suspected, it shall be allowed to appeal to the Roman Bishop, or to a council of fifteen neighboring bishops who have been summoned; provided that after the examination has been held, what has been decided shall not be reopened. <note>original: "alicujus ferocitas talis emerserit, omnis ejus causae edictio ad Metropolitae in eadem Provincia Episcopi deduceretur examen. Vel si ipse Metropolitanus est, Romam necessario, vel ad eos quos Romanus Episcopus judices dederit, sine delatione contendat.——Quod si vel Metropolitani Episcopi vel cujuscunque sacerdotis iniquitas est suspecta, aut gratia; ad Romanum Episcopum, vel ad Concilium quindecim finitimorum Episcoporum accersitum liceat provocare; modo ne post examen habitum, quod definitum fuerit, integretur."</note>

This Edict, lacking the names of both **Valens** and **Theodosius** in its title, was issued in the period between their reigns—that is, in late 378 or early 379 AD. It was addressed to the **Praetorian Prefects of Italy and Gaul**, and was therefore general in its application. This is because the **Praetorian Prefect of Italy** governed Italy, **Western Illyricum** <note>A Roman province encompassing parts of the modern-day Balkans, including Croatia and Serbia.</note>, and Africa; and the **Praetorian Prefect of Gaul** governed **Gaul**, Spain, and Britain.

The granting of this jurisdiction to the Pope gave several bishops the opportunity to write to him for rulings on difficult cases. In response, he began to answer using **decretal epistles** <term>Formal letters from the Pope that established church law or settled points of doctrine.</term>; and from that time forward, he issued laws to the Western Churches through these epistles. **Himerius**, the Bishop of **Tarragona** <note>original: "Tarraco," the capital of the Roman province of Hispania Tarraconensis.</note>, the primary city of a province in Spain, wrote to Pope **Damasus** for guidance concerning

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how the Bishop of Rome established legal authority over the Western Church. It transitions from the reign of Pope Damasus to his successor, Pope Siricius, focusing on the first known "Decretals"—papal letters that carried the force of law.</meta>

...certain ecclesiastical matters. Because the letter did not arrive at **Rome** until after the death of **Damasus** in 384 AD, his successor, **Siricius**, answered it with legislative authority. Regarding one matter, he told him: “Since this is being done—the general decrees sent to the provinces by my predecessor of venerable memory, **Liberius**, forbid it.” <note>original: "Cum hoc fieri — missa ad Provincias à venerandæ memoriæ prædeceſſore meo Liberio generalia decreta, prohibeant."</note>

Regarding another matter, he said: “Let them know that they are cast down by the authority of the **Apostolic See** from every ecclesiastical honor which they have unworthily used.” <note>original: "Noverint se ab omni eccleſiaſtico honore, quo indignè uſi ſunt, Apoſtolicæ Sedis auctoritate, dejectos."</note>

Regarding yet another: “The chief bishops of all the provinces should know henceforth that if anyone of such a sort should be assumed into holy orders voluntarily, a fitting sentence must be pronounced by the **Apostolic See** regarding both their own status and the status of those whom they have promoted against the **Canons** <term>Canon: a rule or law set by a church council</term> and our prohibitions.” <note>original: "Scituri poſthac omnium Provinciarum ſummi Antiſtites, quod ſi ultrò ad ſacros ordines quenquam de talibus eſſe aſſumendum, & de ſuo & de aliorum ſtatu, quos contra Canones & interdicta noſtra provexerint, congruam ab Apoſtolicâ Sede promendam eſſe ſententiam."</note>

And he concludes the epistle thus: “We have explained, I believe, dearest brother, all the matters that were gathered into the complaint; and to each individual cause which you referred to the **Roman Church**, as if to the head of your body, we have given sufficient answers, in my opinion. Now we urge your brotherly mind more and more toward observing the canons and upholding the established **decretals** <term>Decretal: a papal letter that creates or clarifies church law</term>. Furthermore, see to it that these things which we have written in response to your inquiries are brought to the attention of all your fellow bishops; and not only those who are established in your own diocese, but also to all those of **Carthage**, **Baetica**, **Lusi-**”

<meta>catchword: tanos (Lusitanos)</meta>

<summary>This page illustrates the rise of papal legislative power through Pope Siricius, who issued authoritative "Decretals" to resolve church disputes and enforce discipline across Western Europe. He famously describes the Roman Church as the "head" of the ecclesiastical body.</summary>
<keywords>Pope Siricius, Pope Damasus, Pope Liberius, Decretals, Canon Law, Apostolic See, Roman Church, Carthage, Baetica, Lusitania</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the transcription of a letter from Pope Siricius to Himerius, Bishop of Tarragona, dated February 385 AD. It further illustrates the growing legislative authority of the Bishop of Rome over the Western Church by citing earlier precedents from Popes Liberius and Damasus.</meta>

<page-num>93</page-num>
<header>Chapter 8. Prophecies of Daniel.</header>

<margin>ᵃ The peoples of Gaul <note>original: Galliciæ</note>.</margin>

> ...[Tarragoni]ans and the ᵃ **Gauls** <note>original: Gallicos</note>, or those who border the neighboring provinces on either side—let these measures, which we have arranged by a beneficial decree, be sent out by you in a series of letters. And although no priest of the Lord is permitted to be ignorant of the statutes of the **Apostolic See** or the venerable definitions of the **Church Canons** <term>Canon: a rule or law set by a church council</term>, it will nevertheless be very useful and glorious for your devoted self, given the long history of your priesthood, if these things which were written to you specifically but for general application are brought to the attention of all our brothers through your diligent care. This ensures that what we have beneficially established—not rashly, but providently with great caution and deliberation—may remain inviolate, and that the path to any future excuses (which can no longer be accepted by us) may be blocked. Given on February 11th <note>original: 3 Id. Febr.</note>, during the consulship of the most illustrious men **Arcadius** and **Bauto**, in the year 385 AD. <note>original: tanos atque Gallicos... A. C. 385.</note>

Pope **Liberius**, during the reign of **Jovian** or **Valentinian I**, sent general decrees to the provinces ordering that the **Arians** <term>Arians: followers of Arius, who taught that Jesus was a created being, subordinate to God the Father</term> should not be rebaptized. He did this in support of the **Council of Alexandria**, requiring only that they renounce their opinions to be readmitted to the church. Pope **Damasus** is also said to have decreed in a Roman council that **tithes** <term>Tithes: a tenth part of income or produce given to support the church</term> and tenths should be paid under penalty of an **anathema** <term>Anathema: a formal curse or excommunication by the church</term>; he also ordered that the hymn *Glory be to the Father, etc.* <note>original: Gloria Patri</note> should be said or sung at the end of the **Psalms**.

<meta>catchword: But</meta>

<summary>This page concludes a decree from Pope Siricius that demands provincial bishops follow the laws of the Roman See. It also references earlier mandates by Popes Liberius and Damasus regarding the treatment of heretics and the administration of tithes and liturgy.</summary>

<page-num>95</page-num>
<header>Observations upon the &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Part I.</header>

*...priests or clerics who are pretenders, because no document of the **Official Letters** <note>original: Formatarum; these were canonical letters of recommendation used to verify a cleric's identity and legitimate standing</note> exists by which they can be refuted, steal into positions of honor and earn undue reverence. Therefore, dearest brothers, whoever comes to us without the aforementioned Official Letter—whether he is a bishop, priest, deacon, or even of a lower rank—let him know that he cannot be received at all. It is clear that we have sent this authority everywhere, so that it may be known in all regions that what we have decreed must be observed in every way. If anyone, however, should willfully attempt to violate these beneficial regulations, let him know that he is severed from our communion. Moreover, we have granted this privilege of issuing Official Letters to our brother and fellow bishop, **Saint Patroclus**, in special consideration of his merits.* <note>original: presbyteri sive ecclesiastici simulantes... meritorum ejus speciali contemplatione, concessimus</note> And that the Bishop of **Arles** was sometimes the **Pope’s Vicar** <term>Vicar: a representative or deputy of a high-ranking church official who acts with their authority</term> over all **France** <note>original: Gallias</note>, is affirmed also by all the Bishops of the Diocese of Arles in their Letter to **Pope Leo I**. *“To whom this honor and dignity was also conferred,”* they say, *“so that he might not only govern these provinces by his own power, but also keep all the **Gauls** <note>original: Gallias</note>—entrusted to him as a representative of the **Apostolic See**—under every ecclesiastical rule.”* <note>original: Cui id etiam honoris dignitatisque collatum est... sub omni ecclesiastica regula contineret</note> And **Pope Pelagius I**, in the year 556 AD <note>original: A. C. 556</note>, in his Letter to **Sapaudus**, Bishop of Arles, wrote: *“Desiring, by the working of God’s mercy, to follow in the footsteps of our ancestors and to imitate their deeds in all things according to divine judgment: To your Charity throughout the whole...”* <note>original: Majorum nostrorum... Charitati tuae per universam</note>

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the expansion of the Bishop of Rome's authority, moving from the jurisdiction over Gaul and Spain to the region of Illyricum, documenting the appointment of various bishops as papal vicars.</meta>

<page-num>97</page-num>
<header>Chap. 8. Prophecies of Daniel.</header>

*“We entrust to Gaul the duties of the Holy Apostolic See, over which we preside by divine grace.”* <note>original: Galliam, sanctæ Sedis Apostolicæ, cui divinâ gratiâ præsidemus, vices injungimus.</note>

By the influence of the same imperial edict, not only **Spain** and **Gaul** <note>original: Gallia</note>, but also **Illyricum** <note>a region in the western Balkans</note> became subject to the Pope. **Damasus** appointed **Ascholius** (or **Acholius**), the Bishop of **Thessalonica**—the capital city of **Eastern Illyricum**—as his **vicar** <term>vicar: a representative or deputy of a high-ranking church official who acts with their authority</term> for the hearing of legal cases. In the year 382, Acholius, having been summoned by Pope Damasus, attended a church council at **Rome**. Pope **Siricius**, the successor of Damasus, decreed that no bishop should be ordained in Illyricum without the consent of **Anysius**, the successor of Acholius. 

And the subsequent popes gave **Rufus**, the successor of Anysius, the power to call provincial councils. In the collections of **Holstenius** <note>Lucas Holstenius (1596–1661) was a German scholar and Vatican librarian who collected ancient church documents</note>, there is an account of a Council of Rome convened under **Pope Boniface II**, in which letters were produced from the Bishops of Rome—Damasus, Siricius, **Innocent I**, **Boniface I**, and **Celestine**—to Ascholius, Anysius, and Rufus, the Bishops of Thessalonica. In these letters, they entrusted to them the hearing of legal cases in Illyricum, a power granted by the Lord and the holy **canons** <term>canons: church laws or decrees</term> to the **Apostolic See** throughout that province. 

And Pope Siricius says in his epistle to Anysius: *“Even a while ago, dearest brother, through Bishop Candidianus, who has preceded us to the Lord, we had given letters of this kind, so that there would be no permission granted, without your consent, in Illyricum...”* <note>original: Etiam dudum, frater charissime, per Candidianum Episcopum, qui nos præcessit ad Dominum, hujusmodi literas dederamus, ut nulla licentia esset, sine consensu tuo in Illy-</note>

<meta>catchword: rico</meta>

...[Illy]ricum, [certain bishops] presume to ordain bishops; I could not know whether word of these actions reached you. Multa enim gesta sunt per contentionem ab Episcopis in ordinationibus faciendis, quod tua melius caritas novit.</note> And a little after: To suppress all such audacity, your diligence ought to be watchful, with the Holy Spirit burning within you: so that either you yourself, if you are able, or those bishops whom you judge suitable, may be sent with letters. Thus, with consent given, a Catholic bishop of proven life and morals—according to the statutes of the Council of Nicaea or the Roman Church—may be ordained as a deserving clergyman from the clergy in the place of one who has died or been deposed. <note>original: Ad omnem enim hujusmodi audaciam comprimendam vigilare debet instantia tua, Spiritu in te Sancto fervente : ut vel ipse, si potes, vel quos judicaveris Episcopos idoneos, cum literis dirigas, dato consensu qui possit, in ejus locum qui defunctus vel depositus fuerit, Catholicum Episcopum vitâ & moribus probatum, secundum Nicænæ Synodi statuta vel Ecclesiæ Romanæ, Clericum de Clero meritum ordinare.</note>

And **Pope Innocent I** says in his Epistle to **Anysius**: To whom [Anysius] also my predecessors—bishops of such great standing, namely **Damasus** and **Siricius** of holy memory, and the man mentioned above—deferred in such a way that they committed all things occurring in those regions to your Holiness, which is full of justice, to be acknowledged. <note>original: Cui [Anysio] etiam anteriores tanti ac tales viri prædecessores mei Episcopi, id est, fanctæ memoriæ Damasus, Siricius, atque supra memoratus vir ita detulerunt ; ut omnia quæ in omnibus illis partibus gererentur, Sanctitati tuæ, quæ plena justitiæ est, traderent cognoscenda.</note>

And in his Epistle to **Rufus**, the successor of Anysius: Thus, let it be learned that counsel must be provided for the churches separated from me by such long distances; so that, by the favor of Christ the Lord, I may decree that the care and legal cases, if any arise, should be committed to your prudence and gravity throughout the churches of **Achaia**, **Thessaly**, **Old Epirus**, **New Epirus**, **Crete**, **Mediterranean Dacia**, **Ripensian Dacia** <term>Ripensian Dacia: a province along the banks of the Danube</term>, **Moesia**, **Dardania**, and **Prevalis**. For truly to his most sacred... <note>original: Ita longis intervallis disterminatis à me ecclesiis discat consulendum ; ut prudentiæ gravitatique tuæ committendam curam causasque, siquæ exoriantur, per Achaiæ, Thessaliæ, Epiri veteris, Epiri novæ, & Cretæ, Daciæ mediterraneæ, Daciæ ripensis, Mæsiæ, Dardaniæ, & Prævali ecclesias, Christo Domino annuente, censeam. Verè enim ejus sacratissimis</note>

### Chapter 8: Prophecies of Daniel.

...we enjoin this responsibility upon your most excellent sincerity, foresight, and virtue. We are not establishing these rules for the first time, but are imitating our apostolic predecessors, who wished for these duties to be assigned to the most blessed **Acholius** and **Anysius** according to their merits. <note>original: monitis lectissimæ sinceritatis tuæ providentiæ & virtuti hanc injungimus sollicitudinem: non primitàs hæc statuentes, sed Præcessores nostros Apostolicos imitati, qui beatissimis Acholio & Anyislo injungi pro meritis ista voluerunt.</note> And **Boniface I**, in his <term>decretal: a papal letter that formulated a decree or authoritative ruling on church law</term> letter to **Rufus** and the rest of the bishops in **Illyricum**, wrote: "As I have frequently said, no one should presume to perform anyone's ordination without his [the Bishop of **Thessalonica's**] knowledge. To him, as was stated above, we commit all things as our representative." <note>original: Nullus, ut frequenter dixi, alicujus ordinationem citra ejus [Episcopi Thessalonicensis] conscientiam celebrare præsumat: cui, ut supra dictum est, vice nostrâ cuncta committimus.</note>

And Pope **Celestine**, in his decretal letter to the bishops throughout **Illyricum**, says: "Know that our authority <note>original: Vicem nostram; literally "our place" or "vicarship."</note> has been entrusted to [**Rufus**] throughout your province, so that, dearest brothers, whatever legal matters arise should be referred to him. Let no one be ordained without his advice. Let no one usurp the province committed to him without his knowledge; let no bishop presume to hold an assembly without his consent." <note>original: Vicem nostram per vestram Provinciam noveritis [Rufo] esse commissam, ita ut ad eum, fratres carissimi, quicquid de causis agitur, referatur. Sine ejus consilio nullus ordinetur. Nullus usurpet, eodem inconscio, commissam illi Provinciam; colligere nisi cum ejus voluntate Episcopus non præsumat.</note>

And in the case of **Perigenes**, in the title of his letter, he lists the provinces under this bishop as follows: "To **Rufus** and the other bishops established throughout **Macedonia**, **Achaia**, **Thessaly**, **Old Epirus**, **New Epirus**, **Praevalis**, and **Dacia**."

# Chapter 8. Prophecies of Daniel.

*...[with the] warnings of your most chosen sincerity, we enjoin this concern upon your providence and virtue; not establishing these rules for the first time, but imitating our Apostolic predecessors, who wished these things to be enjoined upon the most blessed* **Acholius** *and* **Anysius** *according to their merits.* <note>original: "...monitis lectissimæ sinceritatis tuæ providentiæ & virtuti hanc injungimus sollicitudinem: non primitùs hæc statuentes, sed Præcessores nostros Apostolicos imitati, qui beatissimis Acholio & Anysio injungi pro meritis ista voluerunt."</note> And **Boniface I**, in his <term>decretal Epistle</term> <note>A decretal epistle is a formal letter from a Pope that establishes a ruling on church law or discipline.</note> to **Rufus** and the rest of the Bishops in **Illyricum**, says: *As I have frequently said, let no one presume to perform anyone's ordination without his [the Bishop of Thessalonica's] knowledge; to him, as stated above, we commit all things as our representative.* <note>original: "Nullus, ut frequenter dixi, alicujus ordinationem citra ejus [Episcopi Thessalonicensis] conscientiam celebrare præsumat: cui, ut supra dictum est, vice nostrâ cuncta committimus."</note>

Observations upon the Part I.

*...[he should] distinguish and define any legal actions that arise among brothers, as is common; and whatever is done by individual priests should be referred to him. Let there be a Council as often as cases arise, or as often as he decrees based on the necessity of emerging needs.* <note>original: "...inter fratres nascantur, ut assolent, actiones distinguat atque definiat; & ad eum, quicquid à singulis sacerdotibus agitur, referatur. Sit Concilium, quotiens causæ fuerint, quotiens ille pro necessitatum emergentium ratione decreverit."</note> And Pope **Leo I**, in his <term>decretal Epistle</term> to **Anastasius**, Bishop of **Thessalonica**, wrote: *Moreover, just as this power is granted to individual Metropolitan bishops so that they have the right of ordaining in their own provinces, so we wish those Metropolitans to be ordained by you, though only after mature and well-considered judgment.* <note>original: "Singulis autem Metropolitanis sicut potestas ista committitur, ut in suis Provinciis jus habeant ordinandi; ita eos Metropolitanos à te volumus ordinari; maturo tamen & decocto judicio."</note>

**Western Illyricum** <note>original: "Occidental Illyricum"</note> included **Pannonia Prima** and **Secunda**, **Savia**, **Dalmatia**, **Mediterranean Noricum**, and **Riparian Noricum**; and its <term>Metropolis</term> <note>The capital or chief city of a province, serving as the seat of the highest-ranking bishop (the Metropolitan).</note> was **Sirmium**, until **Attila** the Hun destroyed this city. Afterwards, **Laureacum** became the Metropolis of **Noricum** and both **Pannonias**, and **Salona** became the Metropolis of **Dalmatia**.

<margin>
See Carolus a Sancto Paulo’s *Sacred Geography*, pages 72, 73. <note>original: "Vide Caroli a S. Paulo Geographiam sacram, P. 72, 73."</note>
</margin>
Now the Bishops of **Laureacum** and **Salona** received the **Pallium** <note>A circular band of white wool worn over the shoulders, symbolizing the authority of a Metropolitan bishop and their communion with the Pope.</note> from the Pope. Furthermore, **Zosimus**, in his decretal Epistle to **Hesychius**, Bishop of **Salona**, directed him to announce the Apostolic decrees to the Bishops of his own province as well as to those of the neighboring provinces. The subjection of these provinces to the <term>See of Rome</term> <note>The jurisdiction or office of the Pope.</note> seems to have begun with **Anemius**, who was ordained Bishop of **Sirmium** by **Ambrose**, Bishop of **Milan**, and who—in the **Council of Aquileia** under **Pope Damasus** in the year 381—declared

# Chap. 8. Prophecies of Daniel.

...his sentence in these words: "The head of **Illyricum** is none other than the city of **Sirmium**: I am therefore the Bishop of that city. Whoever does not confess the Son of God to be eternal and co-eternal with the Father, who is everlasting, I declare <term>anathema</term> <note>original: "anathema dico" — a formal curse by a church authority, excommunicating someone.</note>." The next year, **Anemius** and **Ambrose**, along with **Valerian**, Bishop of **Aquileia**, **Acholius**, Bishop of **Thessalonica**, and many others, went to the **Council of Rome**. This council met to overrule the **Greek** Church by a majority of votes and to exalt the authority of the **Apostolic See** <note>The seat of the Pope's authority in Rome.</note>, just as had been attempted before at the **Council of Sardica**.

**Aquileia** was the second city of the **Western Empire**, and some called it the "second **Rome**." It was the <term>Metropolis</term> <note>The chief city of a province, serving as the seat of the highest-ranking bishop (the Metropolitan).</note> of **Istria**, **Forum Julium**, and **Venetia**. Its subjection to the **See of Rome** is shown by the <term>decretal Epistle</term> <note>An official letter from the Pope establishing a ruling on church law.</note> of **Leo I** addressed to **Nicetas**, Bishop of this city. The Pope begins his letter this way: 

"My son **Adeodatus**, a Deacon of our See, having returned to us, mentions that your devotion requested that you might receive the authority of the **Apostolic See** from us regarding those matters which indeed seem to present great difficulty in judgment." <note>original: "Regressus ad nos filius meus Adeodatus Diaconus Sedis nostræ, dilectionem tuam poposcisse memorat, ut de his à nobis authoritatem Apostolicæ Sedis acciperes, quæ quidem magnam difficultatem dijudicationis videntur afferre."</note> 

Then he provides an answer to the questions proposed by **Nicetas** and concludes like this: "Moreover, you shall see to it that this letter of ours, which we have sent in response to your inquiry, reaches all your brothers and fellow provincial bishops, so that the authority granted may be of use in the observance of everyone. Given on the 12th..." <note>original: "Hanc autem Epistolam nostram, quam ad consultationem tuæ fraternitatis emisimus, ad omnes fratres & comprovinciales tuos Episcopos facies pervenire, ut in omnium observantia, data prosit authoritas. Data 12"</note>

# Observations upon the Part I.

->*On the Calends of April <note>The first day of the month.</note>, in the consulship of Majorian Augustus, 458 AD.*<-

<margin>Gregory the Great, Book 1, Indiction 9, Epistle 16.</margin> Gregory the Great, in 591 AD, summoned Severus, Bishop of Aquileia, to appear before him for judgment at a council in Rome.

The Bishops of Aquileia and Milan ordained one another and therefore possessed equal authority, while both were similarly subject to the See of Rome. <margin>From Gratian, concerning the Bishops of Milan and Aquileia.</margin> Pope Pelagius, around the year 557, testified to this in the following words: "It was an ancient custom," he said, "that because the distance or the difficulty of the journey made it burdensome for them to be ordained by the Apostolic See, the Bishops of Milan and Aquileia should have ordained one another." <note>original: "Mos antiquus fuit, ut quia pro longinquitate vel difficultate itineris, ab Apostolico illis onerosum fuerit ordinari, ipsi se invicem Mediolanensis & Aquileiensis ordinare Episcopos debuissent."</note> These words imply that the right to ordain these two bishops originally belonged to the See of Rome. 

When Laurentius, Bishop of Milan, had excommunicated Magnus (one of his <term>presbyters</term> <note>A priest or elder within the early Christian church.</note>) and then died, <margin>Gregory the Great, Book 3, Epistle 26; and Book 4, Epistle 1.</margin> Gregory the Great absolved Magnus. He then sent the <term>pallium</term> <note>A woolen vestment worn by an archbishop as a symbol of authority granted by the Pope.</note> to the newly elected Bishop Constantius. The following year, Gregory reprimanded Constantius for partiality in his judgment of Fortunatus <margin>Gregory, Book 5, Epistle 4.</margin> and commanded him to send Fortunatus to Rome to be judged there. 

<margin>Gregory, Book 9, Epistles 10 and 67.</margin> Four years later, he appointed the Bishops of Milan and Ravenna to hear the case of a man named Maximus.
