### Chap. 8. Prophecies of Daniel.

...that by the authority of his letters *Deusdedit* should be ordained, and that the man whom the *Lombards* <note>A Germanic people who settled in Italy; their presence often complicated the relationship between local churches and Rome.</note> had ordained was an unworthy successor of *Ambrose*. From this, I gather that the Church of *Milan* had continued in this state of subordination to the See of *Rome* ever since the days of *Ambrose*; for *Ambrose* himself acknowledged the authority of that See.

<margin>Ambrose, Book 3, On the Sacraments, chapter 1.</margin>
"The *Roman Church*," he says, "does not have this custom; yet we follow its pattern and form in all things." <note>original: "Ecclesia Romana... hanc consuetudinem non habet, cujus typum in omnibus sequimur, & formam."</note> And a little after: "In all things, I desire to follow the *Roman Church*." <note>original: "In omnibus cupio sequi Ecclesiam Romanam."</note> And in his *Commentary upon 1 Timothy 3*: "Although the whole world belongs to God, yet His house is called the Church, of which *Damasus* <note>Pope Damasus I, who reigned from 366 to 384 AD.</note> is the ruler today." <note>original: "Cum totus mundus Dei sit, tamen domus ejus Ecclesia dicitur, cujus hodie rector est Damasus."</note>

In his oration on the death of his brother *Satyrus*, he relates how his brother, upon coming to a certain city in *Sardinia*, "called for the local bishop and asked him whether he agreed with the Catholic bishops—that is, with the *Roman Church*?" <note>original: "advocavit Episcopum loci, percontatusque est ex eo utrum cum Episcopis Catholicis hoc est cum Romana Ecclesia conveniret?"</note> And in conjunction with the Synod of *Aquileia* in 381 AD, in a synodical letter to the Emperor *Gratian*, he says: "Your Clemency must be entreated not to allow the *Roman Church*—the head of the entire Roman world—and that most holy faith of the Apostles to be disturbed; for from there, the rights of venerable communion flow out to everyone." <note>original: "Totius orbis Romani caput Romanam Ecclesiam, atque illam sacrosanctam Apostolorum fidem, ne turbari sineret, obsecranda fuit clementia vestra; inde enim in omnes venerandæ communionis jura dimanant."</note>

*Auxentius*, the predecessor of *Ambrose*, was not subject to the See of *Rome*; consequently, the subjection of the Church of *Milan* <note>original: "Millain"</note> began under *Ambrose*. This Diocese of *Milan* contained *Liguria* with *Insubria*, the *Cottian Alps*, and *Raetia*; it was divided from the Diocese of *Aquileia* by the river *Adda* <note>original: "Addua"</note>. In the year 844, the Bishop of *Milan* broke away from the See of *Rome* and continued in this separation for about 200 years, as is related by the historian *Sigonius*: "In the same year, Angilbert, Archbishop of Milan, departed from the Roman Church for a cause little known; his example was so influential for the future that it was not until two hundred years later that the Church of Milan returned to the obedience and authority of Rome." <note>original: "Eodem anno Angilbertus Mediolanensis Archiepiscopus ab Ecclesia Romana parum comperta de causa descivit, tantumque exemplo in posterum valuit, ut non nisi post ducentos annos Ecclesia Mediolanensis ad Romanae obedientiam auctoritatemque redierit."</note>

<margin>See Baronius, Year 433, Section 24.</margin>
The Bishop of *Ravenna*, the metropolis of the provinces of *Flaminia* and *Aemilia*, was also subject to the Pope. For example, in AD 417, *Zosimus* excommunicated some of the priests <note>original: "Presbyters"</note> of that church and wrote a letter of warning about them to the local clergy, treating them as a branch of the Roman Church: "In his own," he says, "that is, in our Roman Church." <note>original: "In sua, hoc est, in Ecclesia nostra Romana."</note> When the people of *Ravenna*, having elected a new bishop, notified Pope *Sixtus*, the Pope set their choice aside and ordained *Peter Chrysologus* in his place. *Chrysologus*, in his letter to *Eutyches* <note>A theologian later condemned as a heretic for his views on the nature of Christ.</note> which is preserved in the Acts of the Council of *Chalcedon*, wrote the following:

# Observations upon Part I.

In this sense, Pope Leo I applied this canon to Rome in a decretal epistle <note>original: "Epistle"; a formal ruling or letter from a pope on points of church law</note> to the bishops of Sicily, written during the consulship of Alippio and Ardabure in AD 447.

> "Because it was most beneficially established," <note>original Latin: "Quia saluberrime... cum coram Apostolo Petro semper in communione tractatum fuerit, ut omnia Canonum Decreta apud omnes Domini sacerdotes inviolata permaneant."</note> he says, "by the holy fathers that there ought to be two gatherings of bishops every year, three of you should always meet in Rome on the 29th of September <note>original: "the third day before the Kalends of October"</note> to join the eternal council. And let this custom be observed by you without evasion, since, with the help of God's grace, it will be easier to ensure that no scandals and no errors arise in the churches of Christ; when matters are always handled in communion before the Apostle Peter, so that all the decrees of the canons may remain inviolate among all the Lord’s priests."

The province of Rome, therefore, included Sicily, along with as much of Italy and the neighboring islands as sent bishops to the annual councils of Rome; but it did not extend into the provinces of Ravenna, Aquileia, Milan <note>original: "Millain"</note>, Arles, etc., as those provinces had councils of their own. The bishops in every province of the Roman Empire were convened in council by the metropolitan <term>the bishop of a provincial capital who had authority over other bishops</term> or bishop of the head city of the province, and this bishop presided in that council: but the Bishop of Rome did not only preside in his own council of the

### Chap. 8. Prophecies of Daniel.

...the bishops of the suburbicarian regions <note>original: "suburbicarian regions"; these were the provinces in central and southern Italy and the islands over which the Bishop of Rome exercised direct metropolitan authority</note>, but also gave orders to the metropolitans of all the other provinces in the Western Empire as their universal governor. This can be further seen in the following instances:

Pope *Zosimus*, in AD 417, summoned *Proculus*, the Bishop of *Marseille*, to appear before a council at *Rome* for performing illegitimate ordinations. The Pope condemned him, as he mentions in several of his letters <note>original: "Epistles"</note>. In AD 419, Pope *Boniface I*, following a complaint from the clergy of *Valence* <note>original: "Valentia"</note> against a bishop named *Maximus*, summoned the bishops of all *Gaul* <note>original: "Gallia"</note> and the seven provinces to meet in a council against him; he stated in his letter that his predecessors had done the same. Pope *Leo I* called a general council of all the provinces of *Spain* to meet in *Galicia* <note>original: "Gallæcia"</note> against the *Manicheans* and *Priscillianists* <note>Two groups deemed heretical: Manicheans believed in a dualism between good and evil; Priscillianists were followers of a strict, ascetic movement from Spain</note>, as he records in his formal decree <note>original: "decretal Epistle"</note> to *Turribius*, a *Spanish* bishop. 

In another of his formal decrees to *Nicetas*, the Bishop of *Aquileia*, he commanded him to call a council of the bishops of that province against the *Pelagians* <note>Followers of Pelagius, who were accused of denying original sin and overemphasizing human free will over divine grace</note>. This council was to ratify all the synodal <term>synodal: relating to a church council or synod</term> decrees that had already been confirmed by the See of *Rome* against this heresy. Furthermore, in his formal decree to *Anastasius*, the Bishop of *Thessalonica*, he ordered that the bishop should hold two provincial councils every year and refer the more difficult cases to the See of *Rome*.

# Observations upon the Prophecies of Daniel, Part I.

...and if upon any extraordinary occasion it should be necessary to call a Council, he should not be troublesome to the Bishops under him, but content himself with two Bishops out of every Province, and not detain them above fifteen days. In the same letter <note>original: "Epistle"</note> he describes the form of Church government then set up, which consisted of a subordination of all the Churches to the See of Rome <note>The "See" refers to the seat of authority or jurisdiction of a bishop; in this case, the Papacy</note>:

> "From this model," he says, "a distinction among Bishops has also arisen, and by a great arrangement it has been provided that not everyone should claim everything for themselves. Instead, in each Province, there should be individual bishops whose opinion would be considered primary among their brothers. Furthermore, certain others appointed in the larger cities should take on a greater responsibility, so that through them the care of the universal Church might flow toward the one See of Peter, and nothing should ever be at odds with its head. Therefore, he who knows he has been placed in authority over others should not take it poorly that someone else is placed in authority over him; rather, he should render the same obedience that he himself demands. And just as he does not wish to carry the load of a heavy burden, he should not dare to impose an unbearable weight on others." <note>original: "De qua forma... Episcoporum quoque est orta distinctio, & magna dispositione provisum est ne omnes sibi omnia vindicarent, sed essent in singulis Provinciis singuli quorum inter fratres haberetur prima sententia, & rursus quidam in majoribus urbibus constituti sollicitudinem sumerent ampliorem, per quos ad unam Petri Sedem universalis Ecclesiæ cura conflueret, & nihil usque à suo capite dissideret. Qui ergo scit se quibusdam esse præpositum, non moleste ferat aliquem sibi esse præpositum; sed obedientiam quam exigit etiam ipse dependat; et sicut non vult gravis oneris sarcinam ferre, ita non audeat aliis importabile pondus imponere."</note>

These words sufficiently show the monarchical form of government then established in the Churches of the Western Empire under the Bishop of Rome, by means of the imperial Decree of Gratian, and the appeals and formal decrees <note>original: "decretal Epistles"; these were letters from the Pope that formulated church law</note> grounded upon it.

# Chapter 8. Prophecies of Daniel.

The same Pope Leo, having passed sentence in a Council at Rome upon Hilary, Bishop of Arles, for actions he had taken in a provincial Council in Gaul <note>original: "Gallia"</note>, used this opportunity to obtain the following Edict from the Western Emperor Valentinian III. This was done to establish more absolutely the authority of his office <note>original: "See"</note> over all the Churches of the Western Empire.

->**The Emperors Theodosius and Valentinian, Augusti, to Aetius,**<-
->**illustrious man, Count and Master of both branches of the military, and Patrician.**<-
<note>original: "Impp. Theodosius & Valentinianus AA. Aetio Viro illustri, Comiti & Magistro utriusque militiæ & Patricio."</note>

> It is certain that for both us and our empire, our only protection lies in the favor of the supreme Deity; to earn this favor, the Christian faith and our venerable religion especially support us. Therefore, since the merit of Saint Peter—who is the leader of the assembly of bishops <note>original: "princeps est Episcopalis coronæ," literally "prince of the episcopal crown"</note>—the dignity of the city of Rome, and the authority of a sacred Council <note>original: "Synodi"</note> have confirmed the primacy of the Apostolic See, let no presumptuous person attempt to do anything unlawful without the authority of that See. For the peace of the Churches will only be preserved everywhere if the whole Church <note>original: "Universitas"</note> recognizes its Ruler.
>
> While these rules have been kept without violation until now, Hilary of Arles—as we have learned from the reliable report of the venerable Pope Leo of Rome—has with stubborn defiance attempted certain unlawful acts. Consequently, he has [disturbed] the Transalpine Churches with an abominable

<meta>This page continues the text of the Imperial Edict issued by Emperors Theodosius and Valentinian III to Aetius. Observations upon the Part I.

...disturbances have invaded the churches, as a very recent example especially proves. For Hilary, who is called the Bishop of Arles <note>original: "Episcopus Arelatensis"</note>, without consulting the Pontiff of the church of the city of Rome, has through mere rashness usurped the power of ordaining bishops, a right which was not his. For he has incompetently removed some bishops and indecently ordained others against the will and resistance of the citizens. 

Because these newly appointed men were not easily accepted by those who had not elected them, Hilary gathered an armed band of men. In a hostile manner, he either surrounded walled enclosures with a siege or broke them open by assault. Thus, he brought war to a place of rest, even as he was supposedly traveling there to preach peace. 

Because these acts were committed against the majesty of the Empire and against the reverence due to the Apostolic See <note>original: "Apostolicæ Sedis"</note>, the facts were investigated in order by the Pope of the City, a man of religious character. Consequently, a definitive sentence was passed against Hilary regarding those whom he had wrongly ordained. 

Indeed, that sentence would have been valid throughout the provinces of Gaul <note>original: "per Gallias"</note> even without an Imperial Sanction <note>original: "Imperiali Sanctione"</note>; for what is not permitted by the authority of the Pontiff? However, this reasoning also prompted our own command. 

Neither Hilary—whom only the mild kindness of the Prelate <note>the Pope</note> still allows to be called a Bishop—nor anyone else shall be permitted to mingle weapons with church affairs or to oppose the commands of the Roman Bishop <note>original: "Romani Antistitis"</note>. For by such daring acts, the loyalty and reverence due to our Empire is violated. 

Nor do we only forbid this, which is a crime of the highest order; we also act to ensure that not even a minor disturbance arises among the Churches, and that the [discipline] is not diminished in any way...

### Chap. 8. Prophecies of Daniel.

*...the discipline of religion may be maintained, we decree by this eternal sanction that it shall not be lawful for the Bishops of Gaul <note>original: "Episcopis Gallicanis"</note> or those of other provinces to attempt anything contrary to ancient custom without the authority of the venerable Pope of the Eternal City <note>original: "Papæ Urbis æternæ"</note>. Instead, whatever the authority of the Apostolic See <note>original: "Apostolicæ Sedis"</note> has ordained or shall ordain shall be law for them and for all others. Thus, if any Bishop summoned to the judgment of the Roman Prelate <note>original: "Romani Antistitis"</note> should neglect to appear, he shall be compelled to attend by the Governor <note>original: "Moderatorem"</note> of that same province; in all things, those privileges which our divine ancestors granted to the Roman Church shall be preserved, O Aetius <note>original: "Aetî"</note>, most dear father of the Emperor <note>original: "Augusti"</note>. Therefore, your illustrious and distinguished magnificence shall, by the authority of this present Edictal Law, ensure that the statutes above are observed, with a fine of ten pounds of gold to be immediately demanded from any judge who allows our commands to be violated. May the Divinity preserve you for many years, most dear father.* Given on the 8th of the Ides of June at Rome, in the sixth consulship of Valentinian Augustus, in the year of Christ 445.

By this Edict, the Emperor **Valentinian** enjoined an absolute obedience to the will of the Bishop of **Rome** through all the Churches of his Empire; and declares, that for the Bishops to attempt anything without the Pope’s authority is contrary to ancient custom, and that the Bishops summoned to appear before his judgment <note>original: "judicature"</note> must be carried there by the Governor of the Province; and he ascribes these privileges of the See of **Rome** to the concessions of

the authority of his deceased ancestors—that is, the Edict of **Gratian** and **Valentinian II**, as mentioned above. By this calculation, the dominion of the Church of Rome had now existed for sixty-six years; and if it had not been sufficiently established during all that time, this new Edict was enough to firmly establish it beyond any doubt throughout the **Western Empire**.

Consequently, all the Bishops of the province of **Arles**, in their letter to Pope **Leo** in AD 450 petitioning for the restoration of the privileges of their Metropolitan <term>Metropolitan: the bishop of a capital city who has authority over other bishops in a province</term>, wrote: "Through the blessed Peter, the prince of the Apostles, the most holy Roman Church held the leadership over all the churches of the entire world." <note>original: "Per beatum Petrum Apostolorum principem, sacrosancta Ecclesia Romana tenebat supra omnes totius mundi Ecclesias principatum."</note> And **Ceratius**, **Salonius**, and **Veranus**, three Bishops of **Gaul** <note>original: "Gallia"</note>, said in their letter to the same Pope: "Furthermore, we who are your own subjects are filled with a certain great and inexpressible joy, because that special document of your teaching is so celebrated throughout the assemblies of all the churches that it is declared by the truly harmonious consensus of all; the leadership of the Apostolic See is deservedly established there, from where the oracles of the Apostolic spirit are still delivered." <note>original: "Magna praeterea & ineffabili quadam nos peculiares tui gratulatione succrescimus, quod illa specialis doctrinae vestrae pagina ita per omnium Ecclesiarum conventicula celebratur, ut vere consona omnium sententia declaretur ; merito illic principatum Sedis Apostolicae constitutum, unde adhuc Apostolici spiritus oracula referentur."</note>

And **Leo** himself, in his letter to the Metropolitan <margin>Letter 25 in Holstenius.</margin> Bishops throughout **Illyricum** <note>a Roman province in the Western Balkans</note>, stated: "Because our care extends through all the churches—for the Lord demands this of us, who entrusted the primacy of the Apostolic dignity to the most blessed Apostle Peter as a reward for his faith, establishing the universal Church upon the solidity of his foundation." <note>original: "Quia per omnes Ecclesias cura nostra distenditur, exigente hoc à nobis Domino, qui Apostolicae dignitatis beatissimo Apostolo Petro primatum, fidei sui remuneratione commisit, universalem Ecclesiam in fundamenti ipsius soliditate constituens."</note>

### Chapter 8: Prophecies of Daniel

While this church-based power was growing, the northern barbarian nations invaded the **Western Empire** and founded several kingdoms there, with religions different from those of the **Church of Rome**. However, over time, these kingdoms adopted the Roman faith and simultaneously submitted to the Pope's authority. The **Franks** in **Gaul** <note>modern-day France</note> submitted at the end of the fifth century; the **Goths** in **Spain** at the end of the sixth; and the **Lombards** in **Italy** were conquered by **Charlemagne** <note>original: "Charles the great"</note> in AD 774 <note>original: "A. C. 774"</note>.

Between the years 775 and 794, the same **Charlemagne** extended the Pope's authority across all **Germany** and **Hungary** as far as the **Tisza River** <note>original: "river Theysse"</note> and the **Baltic Sea**. He then placed the Pope above all human law <term>judicature: the administration of justice or the power of a court</term> and helped him conquer the City and Duchy of **Rome**. By converting these ten kingdoms to the Roman religion, the Pope only expanded his spiritual influence; he had not yet emerged as a "horn of the Beast" <note>This refers to the visions in the biblical Book of Daniel, where "horns" represent kings or kingdoms that arise from a great empire</note>. It was his worldly <term>temporal: relating to earthly, political power as opposed to spiritual or eternal matters</term> power that made him one of the "horns." He acquired this power in the latter half of the eighth century by conquering three of the previous "horns," as described earlier.

<margin>Daniel 7:20</margin>
<margin>Verse 25</margin>
Now having achieved worldly power and an authority above all human laws, he reigned *with a look more imposing than his fellows*, and *times and laws* were from then on *given into his hands, for a time, times, and half a* <note>Newton is quoting Daniel 7:25; in prophetic interpretation, "a time, times, and half a time" is often calculated as 1,260 years</note>

<summary>This page describes how the Pope transitioned from a spiritual leader to a political sovereign (a "temporal" power) by gaining the submission of barbarian kingdoms and the military support of Charlemagne.</summary>

Chapter 9. Prophecies of Daniel.

### CHAPTER IX.

#### Of the kingdoms represented in Daniel by the Ram and He-Goat.

The second and third empires, which were represented earlier by the Bear <note>The Bear symbolizes the Medo-Persian Empire</note> and the Leopard <note>The Leopard symbolizes the Greek Empire under Alexander the Great</note>, are represented again here by the Ram and the He-Goat. However, there is a difference: the Ram represents the kingdoms of the **Medes** and **Persians** from the very beginning of the four empires, and the Goat represents the kingdom of the **Greeks** until the end of them. In this way, the eras of all four empires are described once more through the symbols <term>type: a person, object, or event in the Old Testament that foreshadows something in the future</term> of the Ram and the He-Goat:

<margin>Chapter 8, Verse 3</margin>
> *“I lifted up my eyes,” says Daniel, “and looked, and behold, there stood before the river [Ulai] a Ram which had two horns. The two horns were high, but one was higher than the other, and the higher one came up last. ... And the Ram having two horns represents the kings of Media and Persia: not two specific people, but two kingdoms—the kingdoms of Media and Persia. The kingdom of Persia was the 'higher horn' and emerged last. The kingdom of Persia rose to power when Cyrus, having recently conquered Babylon,”*

...revolted from **Darius**, King of the **Medes**, and defeated him at **Pasargadae** <note>original: Pasargadæ; the capital of Cyrus the Great and the site of his final victory over the Medes in 550 BC</note>, and established the **Persians** as superior to the **Medes**. This was the horn that emerged last. The horn that emerged first was the kingdom of the **Medes**, dating from the time that **Cyaxares** and **Nebuchadnezzar** overthrew **Nineveh** and shared the **Assyrian** Empire between them. The Empires of **Media** and **Babylon** existed at the same time and rose together through the fall of the **Assyrian** Empire. The prophecy of the "Four Beasts" <note>Found in Daniel 7</note> begins with one of them <note>Babylon, symbolized by the Lion</note>, while the prophecy of the **Ram and He-Goat** <note>Found in Daniel 8</note> begins with the other <note>Media</note>. Just as the Ram represents the kingdom of **Media** and **Persia** from the beginning of the four empires, the He-Goat represents the empire of the **Greeks** until the end of those monarchies. During the reign of its "great horn" and the four horns that followed it, the Goat represents this empire during the era of the **Leopard** <note>The third beast in Daniel 7, representing the Greek Empire under Alexander and his successors</note>. During the reign of its "little horn"—which arose in the later period of the four kingdoms and became mighty after their fall (though not by its own power)—it represents the empire during the reign of the **fourth beast** <note>The Roman Empire</note>.

The rough Goat, says **Daniel**, is the King of **Greece** (that is, the kingdom itself), and the great horn between its eyes is the first King. This does not refer to the first individual monarch, but the first state of the kingdom: that which lasted during the reign of **Alexander the Great**,

**Daniel** differentiates these time periods by providing a very detailed description of the actions of the Kings of the North <note>the Seleucid Empire in Syria</note> and the South <note>the Ptolemaic Empire in Egypt</note>. These were the two of the four horns that bordered **Judea**. He provides this detail until the **Romans** conquered **Macedonia**; after that point, he only mentions the major revolutions that occurred within the territories originally represented by the **Goat**. During this later period, the **little horn** was to rise and become powerful, though not by its own strength <note>Newton interprets the "little horn" as Rome, which gained power not as a direct successor of the Greek nation, but as an outside force that eventually inherited the Greek dominion</note>.

The first three of **Daniel's Beasts** <note>the Lion (Babylon), the Bear (Persia), and the Leopard (Greece)</note> had their political rule taken away as each subsequent Beast rose to power. However, their lives were prolonged, and in a sense, they are all still alive <note>Newton believed that even after an empire falls, its people and geographic territory remain distinct entities within the prophetic timeline</note>. ### Chapter 9: Prophecies of Daniel

The third Beast, or Leopard, reigned through its four heads until the rise of the fourth Beast, the Empire of the **Latins** <note>The Romans</note>; and its life was prolonged under their power. This Leopard, reigning with its four heads, represents the same thing as the **He-Goat** reigning with its four horns. Therefore, the **He-Goat** reigned with its four horns until the rise of **Daniel’s** fourth Beast, the Empire of the **Latins**. At that point, its dominion was taken away by the **Latins**, but its life was prolonged under their power. The **Latins** are not included among the nations represented by the **He-Goat** in this prophecy; their power over the **Greeks** is only mentioned to distinguish the era when the **He-Goat** was mighty by its own power from the era when it was mighty, but not by its own power. It was mighty by its own power until its dominion was taken away by the **Latins**. After that, its life was prolonged under their rule, and this prolonging of its life occurred during the days of its last horn; for during the reign of this horn, the Goat became mighty, but not by its own power.

Now, because this horn was a horn of the Goat, we must look for it among the nations that composed the body of the Goat. Among those nations, it was to rise up and grow

<margin>Verses 13, 14.</margin>
I heard, says Daniel, one holy one <note>original: "saint"; in this context, it refers to an angelic being.</note> speaking, and another holy one said to that certain holy one who spoke, "How long shall the vision last concerning the daily sacrifice and the transgression of desolation—the giving over of both the sanctuary and the host to be trampled underfoot?" And he said to me, "Until two thousand three hundred days; then the sanctuary shall be cleansed." In Daniel's prophecies, "days" represent years; and these years may perhaps be calculated either from the destruction of the Temple by the Romans in the reign of Vespasian <note>Vespasian was the Roman Emperor (69–79 AD) who began the suppression of the Great Jewish Revolt.</note>, or from the desecration of the Sanctuary by the worship of Jupiter Olympius, or from the desolation of Judea made at the end of the Jewish war by the banishment of all the Jews out of their own country, or from some other period which time will eventually reveal. 

From that point forward, the last horn of the Goat <note>In Newton’s interpretation, the "little horn" of the goat represents the Roman Empire as it expanded into the East.</note> remained powerful under the Romans until the reign of Constantine the Great and his sons. Then, by the division of the Roman Empire between the Greek and Latin Emperors, it separated from the Latins and became the Greek Empire alone, though still under the rule of a Roman family. At present, it remains powerful under the dominion of the Turks <note>The Ottoman Empire, which conquered the remains of the Greek (Byzantine) Empire in 1453.</note>.

<header>Chapter 9. Prophecies of Daniel.</header>

Some interpret this last horn as *Antiochus Epiphanes*, but not very wisely. In prophecy, a horn of a beast never represents a single person; it always signifies a new kingdom, and the kingdom of *Antiochus* was an established one. *Antiochus* ruled over one of the four horns <note>The four horns represent the four kingdoms that emerged from Alexander the Great's empire: the Seleucid, Ptolemaic, Antigonid, and Attalid/Lysimachian kingdoms.</note>, whereas the little horn was a fifth kingdom under its own kings. This horn started small and grew exceedingly great, but *Antiochus* did not. It is described as greater than all the previous horns, which *Antiochus* was not. On the contrary, his kingdom was weak and paid tribute <note>original: "tributary"</note> to the *Romans*, and he did not expand it. 

The horn was a *king of fierce countenance, who destroyed wonderfully, and prospered and took action*; <note>original: "practised"</note> that is, he succeeded in his actions against the holy people. However, *Antiochus* was frightened out of *Egypt* by a simple message from the *Romans*, and was later defeated and frustrated by the *Jews*. The horn was powerful through the strength of another, while *Antiochus* acted on his own power. The horn stood up against the Prince of the heavenly host, the Prince of Princes; this describes the character of *Antichrist*, not *Antiochus*. The horn threw the Sanctuary down to the ground, but *Antiochus* did not; he left it standing. The Sanctuary and the host were trampled underfoot for 2,300 days; and in *Daniel*’s prophecies, "days" represent years. However, the desecration of the Temple during the reign of *Antio-*

# Chap. 9. Prophecies of Daniel.

In the eighth year of *Antiochus*’s reign—the year in which he profaned the Temple and set up heathen gods throughout all *Judea*, and the *Romans* conquered the kingdom of *Macedon*—the prophetic Angel stops describing the affairs of the kings of the *South* <note>original: "South"; referring to the Ptolemaic Kingdom in Egypt</note> and the *North* <note>original: "North"; referring to the Seleucid Empire in Syria</note>, and begins to describe those of the *Greeks* under the dominion of the *Romans*, in these words:

<margin>Dan. 11:31</margin>
*And after him Arms* [the *Romans*] *shall stand up, and they shall pollute the sanctuary of strength.* Just as the Hebrew word *mimmelekh* <note>original: ממלך</note> signifies "after the king" in Daniel 11:8, so here the word *mimmenu* <note>original: ממנו</note> may signify "after him." In the same way, *min-ha-achat* <note>original: מן-האחת</note> may signify "after one of them" in Daniel 8:9. 

In these prophecies of *Daniel*, "arms" are everywhere used to represent the military power of a kingdom, and they "stand up" when they conquer and grow powerful. The *Romans* conquered *Illyricum*, *Epirus*, and *Macedonia* in the year 580 of the Era of Nabonassar <note>The "Era of Nabonassar" was a calendar system used by ancient astronomers; the year 580 corresponds to roughly 168 BC, the year of the Battle of Pydna</note>. Sixty-nine years after that, they conquered the kingdom of *Syria* and reduced it to a province. Thirty-four years later, they did the same to *Egypt*. By all these steps, the Roman arms "stood up" over the *Greeks*. And after ninety-five years more, by making war

# Chap. 10. Prophecies of Daniel.

Now *Nehemiah* came to *Jerusalem* in the 20th year of this same *Artaxerxes*, while *Ezra* still continued there (*Nehemiah 12:36*), and found the city lying waste, with the houses and wall unbuilt (*Nehemiah 2:17, 7:4*). He finished the wall on the 25th day of the month *Elul* (*Nehemiah 6:15*), in the 28th year of the King—that is, in *September* in the year 4278 of the *Julian Period* <note>The Julian Period is a chronological system used by historians to provide a continuous count of years; year 4278 corresponds to approximately 436 BC.</note>. 

Count now from this year sixty-two weeks <note>original: "threescore and two weeks"</note> of years—that is, 434 years—and the reckoning will end in *September* in the year 4712 of the *Julian Period*. This is the year in which *Christ* was born, according to *Clement of Alexandria*, *Irenaeus*, *Eusebius*, *Epiphanius*, *Jerome*, *Orosius*, *Cassiodorus*, and other ancients <note>original: "antients"</note>. This was the general opinion until *Dionysius Exiguus* <note>A 6th-century monk who invented the Anno Domini (AD) dating system used today.</note> invented the common calendar <note>original: "vulgar account"</note>, in which *Christ’s* birth is placed two years later. 

If you agree with some who reckon that *Christ* was born three or four years before the common calendar, his birth will still fall in the latter part of the last week, which is sufficient. It is well known how, after these weeks, *Christ* was cut off, and the city and sanctuary were destroyed by the *Romans*.

*And in the middle of a week <note>original: "half a week"</note> he shall cause the sacrifice and offering <note>original: "oblation"</note> to cease.* This refers to the war of the *Romans* against the *Jews*. Thus, the war lasted three and a half years.

*And upon a wing of abominations he shall cause desolation, even until the completion <note>original: "consummation"</note>, and that which is determined shall be poured upon the desolate.* When the Prophets represent kingdoms as beasts and birds, they use outstretched wings over a country to symbolize armies sent to invade and rule it. Therefore, a "wing of abominations" refers to an army of false gods. In Scripture, the term "abomination" is often used for a false god; for example, *Chemosh* is called the abomination of *Moab*, and *Molech* the abomination of *Ammon*. The meaning, therefore, is that the people of a future Prince will destroy the sanctuary and abolish the daily worship—

For *Christ* tells us that the "abomination of desolation" spoken of by *Daniel* was to be set up during the era of the *Roman Empire* (*Matthew* 24:15).

Thus, in this short prophecy, we have a prediction of all the main periods relating to the coming of the *Messiah*: the time of his birth, the time of his death, the rejection of the *Jews*, the duration of the *Jewish* war by which he caused the city and sanctuary to be destroyed, and the time of his second coming. Therefore, the interpretation given here is more full, complete, and better suited to the overall design than if we were to limit it only to his first coming, as interpreters usually do. We also avoid distorting <note>original: "doing violence to"</note> the language of *Daniel* by combining the *seven weeks* and *sixty-two weeks* into a single number. Had that been *Daniel’s* meaning, he would have said "sixty-nine weeks," and not "seven weeks and sixty-two weeks"—a way of counting used by no nation. --- Page 160 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of ancient calendars, specifically how the 360-day prophetic year relates to the actual solar and lunar calendars used by the Egyptians, Chaldeans, Greeks, and Jews.</meta>

<page-num>138</page-num>

<header>Observations upon Part I.</header>

in his history of the Flood, and by *John* in the *Apocalypse* <note>The Book of Revelation</note>, where a "time, times, and half a time," 42 months, and 1,260 days are treated as equal in value <note>original: "equipollent"</note>. However, when counting many of these years together, one must account for the extra days that were added to the end of these years. For the *Egyptians* added five days to the end of this year; the *Chaldeans* did the same long before the time of *Daniel*, as is shown by the *Era of Nabonassar* <note>An era used by ancient astronomers, beginning in 747 BC</note>. The *Persian Magi* also used the same year of 365 days until the Empire of the *Arabians*. The ancient *Greeks* likewise used the same solar year of 12 equal months, or 360 days, but every other year they added an extra <note>original: "intercalary"</note> month consisting of 10 and 11 days alternately.

The year of the *Jews*, even from the time of their departure from *Egypt*, was *luni-solar* <note>A calendar based on both the moon's phases and the sun's position</note>. It was solar because the harvest always followed the Passover, and the fruits of the land were always gathered before the Feast of Tabernacles (*Leviticus* 23). But the months were lunar, because the people were commanded by *Moses* to blow trumpets and offer burnt offerings and drink offerings at the beginning of every month (*Numbers* 10:10, 28:11, 14). This ceremony was kept on the new moons (*Psalm* 81:3, 4, 5; 1 *Chronicles* 23:31). *Moses* called these months the first, second, third, and fourth month, and so on. The first month was also called *Abib*, the second *Zif*, the seventh *Ethanim*, and the eighth *Bul* (*Exodus* 13:4; 1 *Kings* 6:37, 38, 8:2). However, during the *Babylonian* captivity, the *Jews* adopted the names of the *Chaldean* months and applied them to their own year. Consequently, the *Jewish* months lost their old names and are now known by the *Chaldean* names.

The *Jews* began their civil year from the autumnal equinox <note>the start of autumn, around September 22</note> and their sacred year from the vernal equinox <note>the start of spring, around March 20</note>. The first day of the first month fell on the visible new moon nearest to the equinox.

Whether *Daniel* used the *Chaldean* or *Jewish* year is not particularly important, as the difference is only six hours per year—amounting to only four months over a period of 480 years. However, I believe his months were *Jewish* for several reasons. First, because *Daniel* was a *Jew*, and the *Jews* understood the months of their own year even when using *Chaldean* names. Second, because this prophecy is based on *Jeremiah's* prophecy concerning the 70-year captivity; therefore, it must use the same type of years as *Jeremiah*, which were *Jewish*, since that prophecy was given in *Judea* before the captivity. Finally, *Daniel* reckons by "weeks of years," which is a method of counting unique to the *Jewish* calendar. Just as their days were grouped in sevens, with the last day of every seven being a Sabbath, so their years were grouped in sevens. The last year of every seven was a sabbatical year, and seven such "weeks of years" made a *Jubilee* <note>A 50-year cycle where debts were forgiven and land was returned to its original owners</note>.

<summary>Newton explains the differences between ancient 360-day calendars and the Jewish luni-solar calendar, arguing that Daniel’s prophecy uses Jewish years based on sabbatical cycles.</summary>
<keywords>Chronology, Calendars, Daniel’s Prophecy, Luni-solar, Sabbatical Year, Jubilee, Chaldean months, Nabonassar</keywords>

...ought to be; and the *seventy weeks* of years are *Jewish weeks* ending with *sabbatical years* <note>A sabbatical year, or "shmita," is the seventh year of a seven-year cycle in the Jewish calendar when the land was meant to lie fallow</note>, which is very remarkable. For they end either with the year of the birth of *Christ* (two years before the common calendar <note>original: "vulgar account"</note>), or with the year of his death, or with the seventh year after it: all of which are *sabbatical years*. Other scholars either count by *Lunar* years, or by weeks that are not *Jewish*. Worst of all, they base their interpretations on a flawed timeline, with the exception of *Funccius's* <note>Johann Funck (1518–1566), a German chronologist and theologian</note> opinion about the *seventy weeks*, which is the same as ours. For these other scholars place *Ezra* and *Nehemiah* in the reign of *Artaxerxes Mnemon* <note>Artaxerxes II, who reigned 404–358 BC</note>, and the building of the Temple in the reign of *Darius Nothus* <note>Darius II, who reigned 423–404 BC</note>, and they date the weeks of *Daniel* from those two reigns.

The basis for the chronology followed here, I will now set down as briefly as I can.

<margin>1</margin> The *Peloponnesian* war began in the spring of the *87th Olympiad*, as *Diodorus*, *Eusebius*, and all other authors agree. By comparing these eclipses with the prophecies of *Haggai* and *Zechariah* <note>original: "Zechary"</note>, it is evident that his regnal years began after the 24th day of the 11th *Jewish* month and before the 25th day of *April*—and consequently, around *March*. *Xerxes* <note>Xerxes I, the Great</note> therefore began his reign in the spring of the year 4229 of the *Julian Period*: for *Darius* died in the fifth year after the Battle of *Marathon*, as mentioned by *Herodotus* in Book 7 <note>original: "lib. 7"</note> and by *Plutarch*. That battle took place in October of the year 4224 of the *Julian Period*, ten years before the Battle of *Salamis*. *Xerxes*, therefore, began within less than a year after October of the year 4228 of the *Julian Period*—presumably in the following spring—for he

Xerxes spent his first five years, and a little more, in preparations for his expedition against the Greeks. This expedition took place during the time of the Olympic games, in the first year of the 75th Olympiad, while Calliades was Archon at Athens <note>original: "Calliade Athenis Archonte"</note>. It was also the year 273 from the founding of the city of Rome <note>original: "Anno Urbis conditæ 273"</note>, during the consulship of Fabius and Furius <note>original: "Fabio &amp; Furio Coss."</note>.

The passage of Xerxes’s army over the Hellespont <note>the Dardanelles strait connecting the Aegean to the Sea of Marmara</note> began at the end of the fourth year of the 74th Olympiad—that is, in June of the year 4234 of the Julian Period—and took one month to complete. In autumn, three months later, on the full moon of the 16th day of the month of Munychion <note>an Athenian month</note>, the battle at Salamis took place. A little after that, there was an eclipse of the sun, which by calculation fell on October 2. 

His sixth year, therefore, began a little before June—suppose in the spring of the year 4234 of the Julian Period—and his first year consequently began in the spring of the year 4229, as stated above. Now, he reigned for almost twenty-one years, according to the consensus of all writers. If you add the seven months of Artabanus <note>a regent who seized power after assassinating Xerxes</note>, the sum will be 21 years and about four or five months, which ends between midsummer and autumn in the year 4250 of the Julian Period. At this time, therefore, the reign of his successor Artaxerxes began, as was to be proved.

The same thing is also confirmed by Julius Africanus <note>a Christian historian of the 3rd century</note>, who informs us from earlier writers that the 20th year of this Artaxerxes was the 115th year from the beginning of the reign of...

# CHAPTER XI.

## Of the *Times* of the *Birth* and *Passion* of *Christ*.

The times of the Birth and Passion <note>original: "Passion"; referring to the suffering and crucifixion of Jesus</note> of *Christ*, along with similar details <note>original: "niceties"</note>, were not considered essential to religion and were little regarded by the Christians of the first age. Those who first began to celebrate these dates placed them at the cardinal points <note>original: "cardinal periods"; the four main solar events of the year: the two equinoxes and two solstices</note> of the year. For instance, they placed the Annunciation of the Virgin *Mary* on the 25th of *March*, which was the vernal equinox when *Julius Caesar* corrected the Calendar; the feast of *John the Baptist* on the 24th of *June*, which was the summer solstice; the feast of *St. Michael* on *September* 29, which was the autumnal equinox; and the birth of *Christ* on the winter solstice, *December* 25, along with the feasts of *St. Stephen*, *St. John*, and the *Innocents* <note>original: "Innocents"; referring to the Massacre of the Innocents, the children killed by King Herod</note>, placed as near to it as they could. Because the solstice shifted over time from the 25th of *December* to the 24th, the 23rd, the 22nd, and so on backwards, some in the following centuries placed the birth of *Christ* on *December* 23, and eventually on *December* 20: and

<summary>Newton argues that the specific dates for Christ's birth and death were not documented by early Christians but were later symbolically assigned to the solstices and equinoxes of the Julian calendar.</summary>

<keywords>Birth of Christ, Passion of Christ, Chronology, Julian Calendar, Solstices, Equinoxes, Feast Days, Annunciation, John the Baptist, Saint Michael</keywords>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>Newton continues his argument that early Christians did not have historical records for the dates of holy days, leading later scholars to align these festivals with the solar cycle and the Julian calendar.</meta>

<page-num>145</page-num>
<header>Chap. 11. Prophecies of Daniel.</header>

And for the same reason, they seem to have set the feast of *St. Thomas* on *December* 21, and that of *St. Matthew* on *September* 21. They also placed the days of other Saints at the Sun’s entry into all the signs in the *Julian* Calendar <note>The calendar introduced by Julius Caesar in 46 BC; it was slightly longer than the solar year, causing dates like the solstice to drift over time</note>. For example, they placed the conversion of *Paul* on *January* 25, when the Sun entered Aquarius <note>original symbol: ♒</note>; *St. Matthias* on *February* 25, when it entered Pisces <note>original symbol: ♓</note>; *St. Mark* on *April* 25, when it entered Taurus <note>original symbol: ♉</note>; *Corpus Christi* on *May* 26, when it entered Gemini <note>original symbol: ♊</note>; *St. James* on *July* 25, when it entered Cancer <note>original symbol: ♋</note>; *St. Bartholomew* on *August* 24, when it entered Leo <note>original symbol: ♌</note>; and *Simon* and *Jude* on *October* 28, when it entered Scorpio <note>original symbol: ♏</note>. 

If there were any other remarkable days in the *Julian* Calendar, they placed the Saints upon them as well. For instance, *St. Barnabas* was placed on *June* 11, where the Roman poet *Ovid* seems to place the feast of the goddesses *Vesta* and *Fortuna*, and the goddess *Matuta* <note>In his work *Fasti*, Ovid cataloged the Roman festivals and their astronomical alignments</note>. They also placed *St. Philip* and *St. James* on the first of *May*, a day dedicated both to the *Good Goddess* <note>original: "Bona Dea"</note>, or *Great Mother* <note>original: "Magna Mater"</note>, and to the goddess *Flora*, which is still celebrated with her rites <note>A reference to May Day traditions involving flowers and greenery</note>. All of this shows that these days were fixed in the first *Christian* Calendars by mathematicians as they pleased, without any basis in tradition; and that the *Christians* afterwards simply adopted what they found in those Calendars.

Neither was there any certain tradition about the years of *Christ's* life. For the *Christians* who first began to inquire into these things, as

Clement of Alexandria <note>original: "Clemens Alexandrinus"</note>, Origen, Tertullian, Julius Africanus, Lactantius, Jerome, St. Augustine <note>original: "St. Austin"</note>, Sulpicius Severus, Prosper, and everyone else who places the death of Christ in the 15th or 16th year of the reign of Tiberius <note>Emperor of Rome from 14 to 37 AD</note>, believe that Christ preached for only one year, or at most two. Eventually, Eusebius <note>A 4th-century historian often called the "Father of Church History"</note> discovered four successive Passovers in the Gospel of John, and based on this, he promoted the opinion that Christ preached for three and a half years, and so died in the 19th year of Tiberius. 

Others later found that the opinion that he died during the Equinox on March 25 was more consistent with the timing of the Jewish Passover in the 17th and 20th years of Tiberius's reign; they have placed his death in one of those two years. There is no greater certainty regarding the opinions about the time of his birth either. The first Christians placed his baptism near the beginning of the 15th year of Tiberius. Counting thirty years backward from that point, they placed his birth in the 43rd Julian year, which was the 42nd year of the reign of Augustus and the 28th year after the victory at Actium <note>original: "Actiac victory"; the Battle of Actium in 31 BC, where Augustus secured his power over the Roman Empire</note>. 

This was the opinion that prevailed in the first centuries until Dionysius Exiguus <note>A 6th-century monk who invented the AD/BC dating system we use today</note> placed the baptism of Christ in the 16th year of Tiberius. By misinterpreting the text of Luke 3:23—as if Jesus was only just beginning to turn 30 years old when he was baptized—he invented the common <note>original: "vulgar"</note> era, in which Christ's birth is placed two years later than the earlier accounts. Therefore, as it relates to these things—

...regarding these things, there is no tradition worth considering; let us lay aside all of them and examine what evidence <note>original: "prejudices"; here meaning preliminary conclusions or evidence gathered before a final judgment</note> can be gathered from records of good standing.

The fifteenth year of Tiberius began August 28, in the year 4727 of the Julian Period. <margin>Year of the Julian Period 4727</margin> <note>The Julian Period is a chronological system used by historians to provide a continuous count of years regardless of calendar changes.</note> As soon as the winter was over and the weather became warm enough, we may estimate that John began to baptize; and that before the next winter, his fame spread abroad so that all the people came to his baptism, including Jesus among the rest. "Do you not say," he said, "there are still four months, and then comes the harvest? Behold, I say to you, lift up your eyes and look on the fields, for they are already white for harvest!"

<meta>This page continues the chronological analysis of the life of Jesus, specifically focusing on how the timing of his teachings and parables aligns with the seasons and surroundings in which he spoke.</meta>

<header>Observations upon Part I.</header>

meaning, that the people in the fields were ready for the Gospel, as his next words show <note>original: "shew"</note>. *John* there—

I observe that *Christ* and his forerunner *John*, in their parabolic <note>original: "parabolical"</note> discourses, were accustomed <note>original: "wont"</note> to allude to things present. The old Prophets, when they wanted to describe things emphatically, did not only draw parables from objects that presented themselves—such as from the tearing of a garment (*1 Samuel 15*), from the Sabbatical year (*Isaiah 37*), or from the vessels of a potter (*Jeremiah 18*), and so on—but also, when such fitting objects were missing, they supplied them through their own actions. These actions included tearing a garment (*1 Kings 11*), shooting arrows (*2 Kings 13*), baring their bodies <note>original: "making bare their body"; referring to Isaiah walking naked and barefoot as a sign</note> (*Isaiah 20*), and giving symbolic names to their sons (*Isaiah 8, Hosea 1*). Other examples include hiding a belt <note>original: "girdle"</note> in the bank of the *Euphrates* (*Jeremiah 13*), breaking a potter’s vessel (*Jeremiah 19*), putting on chains <note>original: "fetters"</note> and yokes (*Jeremiah 27*), binding a book to a stone and casting them both into the *Euphrates* (*Jeremiah 51*), besieging a painted city (*Ezekiel 4*), dividing hair into three parts (*Ezekiel 5*), making a chain (*Ezekiel 7*), and carrying out household goods like a trembling captive (*Ezekiel 12*), and so forth.

The Prophets loved to speak through such types <note>A "type" is a symbolic person, object, or event in the Old Testament seen as prefiguring something in the New Testament.</note>. And *Christ*, being endowed <note>original: "endued"</note> with a nobler prophetic spirit than the rest, also excelled in this kind of speaking. However, he did so without performing his own symbolic actions—as that was considered less serious <note>original: "grave"</note> and appropriate <note>original: "decent"</note>—but instead turned things that were already present into parables. 

On the occasion of the harvest approaching, he admonishes his disciples more than once about the spiritual harvest (*John 4:35, Matthew 9:37*). Seeing the lilies of the field, he instructs his disciples about flashy <note>original: "gay"</note> clothing (*Matthew 6:28*). In allusion to the current fruit season, he warns his disciples about knowing men by their fruits (*Matthew 7:16*). During the time of the *Passover*, when trees put forth leaves, he tells his disciples to learn a parable from the fig tree: *when its branch is yet tender and puts forth leaves, you know that summer is near, etc.* (*Matthew 24:32, Luke 21:29*). On the same day, alluding both to the season of the year and to his suffering <note>original: "passion"</note>, which was to occur two days later, he formed a parable about the time of fruits approaching and the murdering of the heir (*Matthew 21:33*). 

Alluding at the same time both to the money-changers whom he had newly driven out of the Temple and to his upcoming suffering, he made a parable of a nobleman going into a far country to receive a kingdom and return, delivering his goods to his servants; upon his return, he condemned the lazy servant because he did not give his money to the money-changers (*Matthew 25:14, Luke 19:12*). Being near the Temple where sheep were kept in pens <note>original: "folds"</note> to be sold for sacrifices, he spoke many things parabolically about sheep, the shepherd, and the door of the sheepfold; and it is revealed that he alluded to the sheepfolds

<meta>catchword: which</meta>

<summary>Newton explains that Jesus and the Hebrew Prophets used immediate physical surroundings and seasonal events as the basis for their parables and symbolic prophecies. He lists numerous biblical examples where objects like harvest crops, fig trees, and money-changers were used to deliver spiritual messages.</summary>

<header>Chapter II. *Prophecies of Daniel.*</header>

[John the Baptist] was therefore imprisoned around *November*, in the **17th year of** *Tiberius*; and *Christ* thereupon

[began to preach], speaking of those who were to be hired in the marketplace, and of such sheepfolds that a thief could not enter through the door, nor could the shepherd himself open them, but instead a gatekeeper <note>original: "porter"</note> opened for the shepherd (*John 10:1, 3*). While on the Mount of Olives (*Matthew 26:30; John 14:31*)—a place so fertile that it could not lack <note>original: "want"</note> vines—he spoke many things symbolically <note>original: "mystically"</note> about the Farmer <note>original: "Husbandman"</note>, the vine, and its branches (*John 15*). Meeting a blind man, he warned <note>original: "admonished"</note> of spiritual blindness (*John 9:39*). At the sight of little children, he described repeatedly <note>original: "once and again"</note> the innocence of the chosen <note>original: "elect"</note> (*Matthew 18:2; 19:13*). Knowing that Lazarus was dead and would be raised again, he spoke <note>original: "discoursed"</note> about the resurrection and eternal life (*John 11:25-26*). Hearing about the slaughter of some whom *Pilate* had killed, he warned of eternal death (*Luke 13:1*). To his fishermen, he spoke of being fishers of men (*Matthew 4:19*) and composed another parable about fish (*Matthew 13:47*). While by the Temple, he spoke of the temple of his body (*John 2:19*). At supper, he told a parable about the symbolic <note>original: "mystical"</note> supper to come in the kingdom of heaven (*Luke 14*). On the occasion of physical <note>original: "temporal"</note> food, he taught his disciples about spiritual food and about eating his flesh and drinking his blood symbolically (*John 6:27, 53*). When his disciples lacked bread, he told <note>original: "bad"</note> them to beware of the leaven <note>original: "leven"</note> of the Pharisees (*Matthew 16:6*). When asked to eat, he answered that he had other food <note>original: "meat"</note> (*John 4:31*). On the great day of the Feast of Tabernacles, when the *Jews*—as was their custom—brought a great amount of water from the river *Shiloah* into the Temple, *Christ* stood and cried out, saying: *If anyone is thirsty, let him come to me and drink. He who believes in me, out of his heart <note>original: "belly"</note> shall flow rivers of living water* (*John 7:37*). The next day, referring <note>original: "in allusion"</note> to the servants who were newly set free because of the Sabbatical year, he said: *If you continue in my word, the truth shall make you free.* The *Jews*, understanding this literally regarding the current release <note>original: "manumission"</note> of servants, answered: *We are Abraham’s descendants <note>original: "seed"</note> and were never in bondage to any man; how can you say, "You shall be made free"?* (*John 8*). They asserted their freedom with a two-part argument: first, because they were the descendants of *Abraham* and would therefore have been recently freed if they had ever been in bondage; and second, because they claimed they had never been in bondage at all. During the last Passover, when *Herod* led his army through *Judea* against *Aretas*, the King of *Arabia*—who was the aggressor and had the stronger military forces, as the outcome showed—*Christ*, referring to those current events, composed the parable of a weaker king leading his army against a stronger one who made war upon him (*Luke 14:31*). And I do not doubt that various <note>original: "divers"</note> other parables were created for other occasions, the history of which we do not possess.

Up to this point, *John* is detailed and clear in relating the actions of Jesus’s first year—events which the other Gospel writers <note>original: "Evangelists"</note> left out. The rest of his history from this time forward is related more fully by the other Gospel writers than by *John*, for he typically omits what they have already recorded.

From this time, therefore, *Jesus* taught in the synagogues of *Galilee* on the Sabbath days and was honored <note>original: "glorified"</note> by everyone. However, he passed through the middle of them and went his way, coming to dwell at *Capernaum* (*Luke 4*). By this time, we may calculate <note>original: "reckon"</note> that the second Passover was either already past or near at hand.

Thus, by comparing the *Gospels* of *Matthew* and *John*, we have the history of *Christ’s* actions in chronological <note>original: "continual"</note> order during five *Passovers*. *John* is more detailed <note>original: "distinct"</note> regarding the beginning and end of the ministry, while *Matthew* focuses more on the middle; what one omits, the other provides. 

The first *Passover* occurred between the baptism of *Christ* and the imprisonment of *John* the Baptist (*John* 2:13). The second was within four months after *John’s* imprisonment and the beginning of *Christ’s* preaching in *Galilee* (*John* 4:35). The fourth was the feast that was near when *Christ* performed the miracle of the five loaves (*Matthew* 14:13–21).

### Chapter III. Prophecies of Daniel.

...death, being afraid of *Herod* as well as of the Jewish leaders <note>original: "Jews"; Newton typically uses this to refer to the religious authorities in Jerusalem who opposed Jesus</note>, he traveled <note>original: "walked"</note> more privately this year than before. In this way, we find that the Gospels of *Matthew* and *John* tell all these things in their proper order, from the beginning of *John’s* preaching to the death of *Christ*. The years are distinguished from one another by such essential characteristics that they cannot be mistaken.

The second *Passover* is distinguished from the first by the intervening event <note>original: "interposition"</note> of *John’s* imprisonment.

The third is distinguished from the second by two characteristics: first, by the intervening event of the feast to which *Christ* traveled (*Matthew 8:19*, *Luke 9:57*); and second, by the length of time since the beginning of *Christ’s* preaching. The second Passover occurred at the very start of his preaching, but the third occurred so long after that, before it arrived, *Christ* said, "From the days of *John* the *Baptist* until now," and so on. He also rebuked the cities of *Galilee* for not repenting despite his preaching and the mighty works he had performed throughout all that time.

The fourth is distinguished from the third by the mission of the twelve Apostles, whom *Christ* sent to preach in the cities of *Judea* during the entire interval between them.

The fifth is distinguished from all the previous ones by the fact that the twelve had returned from their preaching mission, and...

<page-num>162</page-num>

<header>Observations upon the Part I.</header>

<margin>Josephus, *Antiquities of the Jews*, Book 3, Chapter 10.</margin>
Josephus, who was among the priests who had ministered in the temple, tells us that the *Passover* was kept on the 14th day of *Nisan*, <note>original: κατὰ σελήνην</note> according to the moon, when the sun was in *Aries*. This is also confirmed by two instances recorded by him, which totally disprove the hypothesis that the Jews used a flawed <note>original: "vicious"</note> cycle. For in that year in which *Jerusalem* was taken and destroyed, he says the *Passover* was on the 14th day of the month *Xanticus* <note>A Macedonian month name used by Josephus as an equivalent to the Hebrew month Nisan</note>, which according to *Josephus* is our *April*; and that five years before, it fell on the 8th day of the same month. These two instances agree with the actual course of the moon.

Therefore, by computing the new moons of the first month according to the movement of the moon and the rule *Jah* <note>The "Rule Jah" refers to a traditional Jewish calendrical calculation (postponement rule) used to determine the start of the year</note>, and counting 14 days from then, I find that the 14th day of this month:
* In the year of *Christ* 31, fell on Tuesday, *March* 27; 
* In the year 32, on Sunday, *April* 13; 
* In the year 33, on Friday, *April* 3;

* And in the year 36, on Saturday, *March* 31.

<header>Chap. II. *Prophecies of Daniel.*</header>

...two Sabbaths in a row <note>original: "together"</note> they would be prohibited from burying their dead and preparing fresh meat; for in that hot region, their meat would be likely to spoil <note>original: "corrupt"</note> within two days. To avoid these and similar inconveniences, the *Jews* postponed their months by one day whenever the first day of the month *Tishri*—or, what amounts to the same thing, the third day of the month *Nisan*—fell on a Sunday, Wednesday, or Friday. They called this rule *Adu* <note>original: אדו</note>, using the Hebrew letters *Aleph* (א), *Dalet* (ד), and *Vav* (ו), which represent the numbers 1, 4, and 6; these signify the 1st, 4th, and 6th days of the week, which we call Sunday, Wednesday, and Friday. Therefore, by applying this postponement rule to the months calculated above, the 14th day of the month *Nisan* would fall:
* In the year of *Christ* 31, on Wednesday, *March* 28; 
* In the year 32, on Monday, *April* 14; 
* In the year 33, on Friday, *April* 3; 
* In the year 34, on Friday, *April* 23; 
* In the year 35, on Wednesday, *April* 13; 
* and in the year 36, on Saturday, *March* 31.

By this computation, the year 32 is absolutely ruled out <note>original: "excluded"</note>, because the Passion <note>The suffering and death of Jesus Christ</note> cannot fall on a Friday without making it five days after the full moon, or two days before it; whereas it ought to occur on the day of the full moon, or the following day. For the same reason, the years 31 and 35 are ruled out, because in those years the Passion cannot fall on a Friday without making it three days after the full moon,

<margin>Josephus, *Antiquities of the Jews*, Book 3, Chapter 10.</margin>
Josephus, who was among the priests who had ministered in the temple, tells us that the Passover was kept on the 14th day of *Nisan*, <note>original: "κατὰ σελήνην"</note> according to the moon, when the sun was in the constellation of *Aries*. This is also confirmed by two instances recorded by him, which totally disprove the hypothesis that the *Jews* used a flawed <note>original: "vicious"</note> calendar cycle. For he says that in the year in which *Jerusalem* was taken and destroyed, the Passover fell on the 14th day of the month *Xanticus* (which, according to *Josephus*, is our *April*); he also notes that five years earlier, it fell on the 8th day of the same month. These two instances agree with the actual course of the moon.

Calculating, therefore, the new moons of the first month according to the course of the moon and the rule *Jah* <note>A Hebrew calculation rule referring to the timing of the new moon</note>, and then counting forward 14 days, I find that the 14th day of this month fell on the following dates:
* In the year of *Christ* 31, on Tuesday, *March* 27; 
* In the year 32, on Sunday, *April* 13; 
* In the year 33, on Friday, *April* 3; 
* In the year 34, on Wednesday, *March* 24—or rather, to avoid the Equinox which fell on the same day, and to allow for a better time for the harvest, on Thursday, *April* 22; 
* In the year 35, on Tuesday, *April* 12; 
* and in the year 36, on Saturday, *March* 31.

However, because the 15th and 21st days of *Nisan*, a day or two of *Pentecost*, and the 10th, 15th, and 22nd of *Tishri* <note>original: "Tisri"</note> were always sabbatical days (days of rest), it was inconvenient on

...or four days before it: errors so massive that they would be clearly visible in the sky to even the most untrained eye <note>original: "vulgar eye"</note>. The year 36 is supported by few, if any, scholars; both this year and the year 35 may be ruled out for the following reasons:

At the beginning of his reign, *Tiberius* appointed *Valerius Gratus* as Governor <note>original: "President"</note> of *Judea*. Meanwhile, after *Vitellius* had removed *Pilate* from office, he traveled to *Jerusalem* during the Passover to visit that province and others at the start of his term. There, he replaced *Caiaphas* as High Priest and appointed *Jonathan* <note>original: "Jonathas"</note>, the son of *Ananus* (or *Annas*, as he is called in the Bible).

Tiberius reigned for 22 years and 7 months, and he died on March 16 at the beginning of the year AD 37. That year, the feast of the Passover fell on the following April 20—which was 35 days after the death of Tiberius. This allows about 36 to 38 days for the news of the Emperor's death to travel from Rome to Vitellius in Jerusalem. Since this is a reasonable amount of time for such a message to arrive, it confirms that the festival Vitellius and Herod attended was indeed the Passover. It is noteworthy that the high priesthood had, by this time, become an annual office, and the Passover was the customary time for appointing a new high priest. For *Gratus*, the predecessor of *Pilate*, says *Josephus*, appointed *Ismael* as high priest after *Ananus*. A short time later—presumably a year—he removed him and substituted *Eleazar*; a year later he appointed *Simon*, and after another year *Caiaphas*; he then gave way to *Pilate*. Similarly, *Vitellius* at one Passover appointed *Jonathan* <note>original: "Jonathas"</note> as the successor...

# CHAPTER XII.

## Regarding the Prophecy of the Scripture of Truth.

The kingdoms represented by the second and third beasts (the Bear and the Leopard) are described again by *Daniel* in his final prophecy. This was written in the third year of *Cyrus’s* reign over *Babylon*, which was the same year he conquered *Persia*. This prophecy serves as a commentary on the earlier Vision of the Ram and the He-Goat.

<margin>Chapter 11:2, 3, 4.</margin>

Then a mighty king [*Alexander the Great*] shall arise, who shall rule with great power <note>original: "dominion"</note> and do according to his will. The *Babylonians* now began to count time by a new *Era* <note>calendar system</note> which they called the *Era of Philip*. ### Chapter 12: Prophecies of Daniel

Philip Aridaeus <note>Alexander the Great's half-brother</note> reigned under the administratorship <note>regency</note> of *Antipater*, and for over a year more under that of *Polyperchon*. In total, he reigned for six years and four months before he was killed, along with his Queen *Eurydice*, in *September* by the order of *Olympias*, the mother of *Alexander the Great*. 

The Greeks, disgusted by the cruelties of *Olympias*, revolted and joined *Cassander*, the son and successor of *Antipater*. *Cassander*, seeking total control over Greece, killed *Olympias*. Soon after, in the 432nd year of the Era of *Nabonassar* <note>316 BCE; the Era of Nabonassar was a chronological system used by ancient historians and astronomers</note>, he imprisoned the young king *Alexander* <note>Alexander IV, the young son of Alexander the Great and Roxana</note> and his mother *Roxana* in the castle of *Amphipolis*, under the guard of *Glaucias*. 

The following year, *Ptolemy*, *Cassander*, and *Lysimachus*—prompted by *Seleucus*—formed an alliance against *Antigonus*. After several wars, they made peace with him in the 438th year of the Era of *Nabonassar* <note>310 BCE</note> on these conditions: *Cassander* would command the forces of *Europe* until *Alexander* (the son of *Roxana*) reached adulthood; *Lysimachus* would govern *Thrace*; *Ptolemy* would govern *Egypt* and *Libya*; and *Antigonus* would govern all of *Asia*. 

*Seleucus* had taken possession of *Mesopotamia*, *Babylonia*, *Susiana*, and *Media* the previous year. About three years after the death of *Alexander the Great*, he had been appointed governor of *Babylon* by *Antipater*. He was later expelled by *Antigonus*, but he had now recovered and expanded his rule over a large part of the *East*. This expansion led to the start of a new calendar era, called the *Seleucid Era* <note>original: "Æra Seleucidarum"; this calendar system began in 312 BCE and was used for centuries throughout the Near East</note>.

# Chapter 12: Prophecies of Daniel

->**Observations upon the Part I.**<-

About twelve years after the battle between *Philopator* and *Antiochus*, *Philopator* died and left his kingdom to his young son *Ptolemy Epiphanes*, a child of five years old. At that point, *Antiochus the Great* <note>original: "Antiochus Magnus"</note> formed an alliance with *Philip*, king of *Macedon*, agreeing that they should each invade the territories of *Epiphanes* that bordered their own. This led to a fluctuating war between *Antiochus* and *Epiphanes*, as they took turns seizing *Phoenicia* and *Coelesyria* <note>original: "Cœlosyria"; a region in the Levant comprising the Beqaa Valley and surrounding areas, heavily contested during the Syrian Wars</note>. As a result, those regions suffered greatly under both sides. First *Antiochus* seized them; then a man named *Scopas*, being sent with the army of *Egypt*, recovered them from *Antiochus*. The next year, in the year of Nabonassar 550 <note>original: "An. Nabonass. 550"; the Era of Nabonassar was used by ancient astronomers. This year corresponds to approximately 198 BC</note>, *Antiochus* fought and defeated *Scopas* near the headwaters of the *Jordan* river, besieged him in *Sidon*, captured the city, and reclaimed *Syria* and *Phoenicia* from *Egypt*, with the *Jews* siding with him voluntarily. 

But about three years later, while preparing for a war against the *Romans*, he came to *Raphia* on the borders of *Egypt*. He made peace with *Epiphanes* and gave him his daughter *Cleopatra* <note>this was Cleopatra I Syra, the first of the famous Ptolemaic queens of that name; the marriage was intended to secure Egyptian neutrality</note> in marriage. The next autumn, he crossed the *Hellespont* <note>the Dardanelles strait, the boundary between Asia and Europe</note> to invade the cities of *Greece* that were under *Roman* protection, and he captured some of them. However, he was defeated by the *Romans* the following summer and forced to retreat with his army back into *Asia*. Before the end of that year, the fleet of *Antiochus* was defeated by the fleet of

<meta>This page continues Isaac Newton’s interpretation of the prophecies in the Book of Daniel, specifically transitioning from the actions of the Greek successors of Alexander the Great (the Kings of the North and South) to the rise of the Roman Empire as the next great power in biblical prophecy.</meta>

### Chapter 12. Prophecies of Daniel.

The **Romans** conquered the kingdom of **Macedon**, the fundamental kingdom of the Empire of the **Greeks**, and reduced it into a **Roman province**; and thereby began to put an end to the reign of **Daniel's** third **Beast** <note>In Daniel 7, the "third beast" is traditionally interpreted by scholars as the Greek Empire founded by Alexander the Great.</note>. This is expressed by **Daniel** in this way: *And after him arms, that is the Romans, shall stand up.* Just as מלך <note>original: "מלך" (melek); Newton is arguing that in Daniel 11:8, this can imply "after the king"</note> signifies "after the king," so too ממנו <note>original: "ממנו" (mim-mennu); usually translated as "from him" or "on his part," but Newton interprets it here as "after him"</note> may signify "after him." **Arms** are everywhere in this prophecy of **Daniel** used to represent the military power of a kingdom; and they "stand up" when they conquer and grow powerful.

Up to this point, **Daniel** described the actions of the **Kings** of the **North** and **South** <note>The Seleucid and Ptolemaic dynasties</note>; but upon the conquest of **Macedon** by the **Romans**, he stopped describing the actions of the **Greeks** and began to describe those of the **Romans** in Greece. They conquered **Macedon**, **Illyricum**, and **Epirus** in the year 580 of the Era of **Nabonassar** <note>168 BCE; Newton uses this ancient Babylonian era for chronological precision</note>. 

Thirty-five years later, by the last will and testament of **Attalus**, the last King of **Pergamum**, they inherited that rich and flourishing kingdom—that is, all of **Asia** westward of Mount **Taurus**. Sixty-nine years after that, they conquered the kingdom of **Syria** and reduced it into a province; and thirty-four years after that, they did the same to **Egypt**. By all these steps, the **Roman arms** stood up over the **Greeks**. # Observations upon the Prophecies of Daniel, Part I.

[...took] away the daily sacrifice, and then placed the **abomination of desolation** <note>A biblical term from Daniel 11:31 and 12:11, which Jesus refers to in the Gospels as a sign of the end times or the destruction of Jerusalem.</note>. (Anno Christi)</note>, the Romans placed this abomination by building a Temple to **Jupiter Capitolinus** <note>The chief deity of the Roman state religion</note> where the Temple of God in **Jerusalem** had stood.

Thereupon the **Jews**, under the leadership of **Bar Kokhba** <note>original: "Barchochab"; the leader of the Jewish rebellion against the Roman Empire (132–136 AD)</note>, rose up in arms against the **Romans**. In that war, they had 50 cities demolished, 985 of their best towns destroyed, and 580,000 men slain by the sword. At the end of the war, in **AD 136**, they were banished from **Judea** upon pain of death; from then on, the land remained desolate of its old inhabitants.

In the beginning of the **Jewish** war during **Nero's** reign, the Apostles fled out of **Judea** with their flocks; some went beyond the **Jordan** to **Pella** and other places, while others went into **Egypt**, **Syria**, **Mesopotamia**, **Asia Minor**, and elsewhere. **Peter** and **John** came into **Asia**, and **Peter** went from there by way of **Corinth** to **Rome**. However, **John**, staying in **Asia**, was banished by the **Romans** to the island of **Patmos**, as he was seen as the head of a faction of the **Jews** whose nation was at war with the **Romans**. By this dispersion of the **Jewish Christians**, the **Christian** religion—which was already spreading westward as far as **Rome**—spread fast into all the **Roman Empire** and suffered many persecutions under it until the...
