### CHAPTER XIII.

-> *Concerning the king who acted according to his own will, exalted himself above every god, honored "Mahuzzims," and did not regard the desire of women.* <-
<note>Newton is quoting Daniel 11:36–38. He interprets this "king" as the Roman Church/Empire in its later, corrupt state.</note>

In the first ages of the Christian religion, the Christians of every city were governed by a Council of Presbyters <term>Presbyters: Elders or senior members of the church who exercised leadership</term>, and the president of the council was the Bishop of the city. The Bishop and Presbyters of one city did not interfere with the affairs of another city, except by sending letters or messages of advice. Nor did the Bishops of various cities meet together in a Council before the time of the Emperor *Commodus* <note>Reigned 180–192 AD. Newton notes that early church organization was localized until the late 2nd century</note>; for they could not meet together without the permission of the *Roman* governors of the provinces. But in the days of that Emperor, they began to meet in Provincial Councils by the permission of the governors. These meetings occurred first in *Asia* <note>referring to the Roman province of Asia, in modern-day Turkey</note> in opposition to the *Cataphrygian* heresy <note>original: "Cataphrygian Heresy"; also known as Montanism, a movement that emphasized new prophetic revelations</note>, and soon after in other places and for other reasons. The Bishop of the chief city, or Metropolis, of the *Roman* province was usually appointed as the president of the Council.

Council;
<meta>catchword: Council;</meta>

<summary>Isaac Newton explains the early organization of the Christian church, noting that it was initially governed by local councils of elders until provincial councils began to form under the Emperor Commodus.</summary>

Council; and from this came the authority of Metropolitan Bishops <note>Bishops of provincial capitals who held authority over other bishops in their province</note> above that of other Bishops within the same province. Hence also it was that the Bishop of *Rome* in *Cyprian’s* days <note>St. Cyprian of Carthage, mid-3rd century AD</note> called himself the "Bishop of Bishops." As soon as the Empire became Christian, the Roman Emperors began to call general Councils out of all the provinces of the Empire; and by prescribing to them what points they should consider, and influencing them by their interest and power, they set up whatever party they pleased. By this means the *Greek* Empire—upon the division of the *Roman* Empire into the *Greek* and *Latin* Empires—became the ***King who, in matters of religion, did according to his will; and, in legislature, exalted and magnified himself above every God***: and at length, by the seventh general Council <note>The Second Council of Nicaea, 787 AD, which restored the use and veneration of icons</note>, established the worship of images and the souls of dead men, here called ***Mahuzzims*** <note>Newton interprets the Hebrew term "Mahuzzim" (meaning "fortresses" or "protectors") as referring to the saints and martyrs, whom the church began to invoke as celestial guardians</note>.

<margin>Book 4. chapters 28, 29.</margin>
The same King placed holiness in abstinence from marriage. *Eusebius* in his *Ecclesiastical History* tells us that *Musanus* wrote a tract against those who fell away to the heresy of the Encratites <note>From the Greek 'enkrateia' (self-control); an ascetic sect that rejected marriage and meat</note>, which was then newly risen and had introduced destructive errors; and that *Tatian*, the disciple of *Justin*, was the author of it. He also notes that *Irenaeus*, in his first book against heresies, teaches this, writing of *Tatian* and his heresy in these words: *Stemming from Saturninus and Marcion...* <note>original: "A Saturnino & Marcione profecti..." Newton is quoting Irenaeus's description of the origins of the Encratite heresy.</note>

### Observations upon the Part I.

> ...who are called "the Continent" <note>original Latin: *Continentes*; a name for the Encratites who practiced strict self-denial</note>, taught that marriage should not be contracted. In doing so, they rejected that original work of God and silently accused the God who created male and female for the procreation of the human race. They also introduced abstinence from eating what they call "animals" <note>meaning meat</note>, showing themselves ungrateful to God, who created everything. They even deny the salvation of the first man <note>Adam</note>. This was recently devised among them, with a man named Tatian being the very first author of this impiety. While he was a student of Justin <note>Justin Martyr, a prominent 2nd-century Christian apologist</note> and lived with him, he produced nothing of this kind. However, after Justin’s martyrdom, Tatian broke away from the Church. Puffed up and arrogant in his role as a teacher—as if he were superior to everyone else—he concocted a new form of doctrine. He invented "invisible Aeons" <note>In Gnostic theology, Aeons are various emanations or beings that mediate between God and the world</note> just as Valentinus did. He also asserted, along with Saturninus and Marcion, that marriage is nothing more than corruption and debauchery <note>original Latin: *stuprum*</note>. Furthermore, he devised new arguments to undermine the belief in Adam’s salvation. This is what Irenaeus wrote concerning the heresy of the Encratites <note>The name comes from the Greek word for "self-control" or "disciplined"</note> which flourished at that time.

This concludes the account from *Eusebius*. But although the followers of *Tatian* were at first condemned as heretics under the name of *Encratites* or *Continentes*, their principles could not yet be completely discredited. For *Montanus* <note>A 2nd-century figure who claimed to receive new prophecies and advocated for strict moral rigor</note> refined these ideas and made only second marriages unlawful; he also introduced frequent fastings and annual fasting days, including the observance of

<meta>The text continues on the next page with the word "Lent."</meta>

<page-num>197</page-num>

# Chapter 13. Prophecies of Daniel.

...Lent, and the practice of eating only dried meats. The *Apostolici* <note>A group that aimed to live like the apostles by renouncing property and marriage</note>, about the middle of the third century, condemned marriage and were a branch of the disciples of *Tatian*. The *Hierocitae* <note>Followers of Hieracas, who taught that marriage was only for those under the Old Testament and forbidden for Christians</note> in *Egypt*, during the late third century, also condemned marriage. *Paul the Hermit* fled into the wilderness to escape the persecution of *Decius* and lived a solitary life there until the reign of *Constantine the Great*, but he gathered no disciples. *Antony* did the same during the persecution of *Diocletian*, or shortly before, and he did gather disciples; many others soon followed his example.

Up to this point, the principles of the *Encratites* had been rejected by the Churches. But now, after being refined by the monks and applied not to all people but only to those who would voluntarily choose a monastic life, these ideas began to be admired. They began to flood first the *Greek* Church and then the *Latin* Church like a torrent.

<margin>In the *Life of Constantine* <note>original Latin: *In vita Constantini*</note>, Book 4, Chapter 28.</margin>
*Eusebius* tells us that *Constantine the Great* held those men in the highest regard who dedicated themselves entirely to "divine philosophy" <note>In this context, "divine philosophy" refers to the ascetic and contemplative life of the monks</note>. He almost worshipped the most holy company of virgins who were perpetually devoted to God, being certain that the God to whom he had consecrated himself lived within their minds. During his time and the time of his sons, this commitment to a single life was promoted in *Egypt* by *Antony* and in *Syria* by *Hilarion*, and it spread so quickly,

<summary>This page tracks the evolution of Christian asceticism from a rejected heresy to an admired monastic tradition supported by Emperor Constantine, highlighting figures like Antony and Hilarion.</summary>

--- Page 217 ---
<meta>This page continues the historical analysis of how ascetic sects, once considered heretical, eventually influenced mainstream Christian practice and theology in the fourth century.</meta>

<page-num>200</page-num>

<header>Observations upon the Part I.</header>

...according to his will, that *he should not regard the desire of women.*

Thus, the sect of the *Encratites* <note>original: *Encratites*; from the Greek word for "self-control," this group practiced extreme asceticism, including the rejection of marriage and certain foods</note>, established by the *Gnostics* and spread by *Tatian* and *Montanus* near the end of the second century, overspread the *Eastern* churches in the fourth century. This sect was condemned by the churches of the second and third centuries, but its ideas were later refined by its followers. Before the end of the fourth century, these views also began to spread through the *Western* churches. 

From this point on, the Christian churches—maintaining an outward appearance of godliness but lacking its true spiritual power—fell into the hands of the *Encratites*. The pagans <note>original: *Heathens*</note>, who converted to Christianity in large numbers during the fourth century, more easily accepted this version of Christianity. It shared more similarities with their old superstitions than did the faith of the sincere Christians. These earlier Christians had illuminated the universal church <note>original: *Church Catholic*</note> during the first three centuries using the light of the seven churches of *Asia* <note>A reference to the seven churches mentioned in the biblical Book of Revelation</note>, rather than the light of the monasteries.

The *Cataphrygians* <note>Another name for the Montanists, a charismatic and ascetic movement originating in Phrygia</note> also introduced several other superstitions, such as the doctrine of ghosts <note>original: *Ghosts*; referring here to the spirits of the dead</note> and their punishment in Purgatory. They promoted prayers and offerings <note>original: *oblations*</note> to lessen that punishment, as *Tertullian* teaches in his books *On the Soul* <note>original Latin: *De Anima*</note> and *On Monogamy* <note>original Latin: *De Monogamia*</note>. They also used the sign of the cross as a protective charm. *Tertullian* writes about this in his book *On the Soldier's Crown* <note>original Latin: *de Corona militis*</note>: *At every forward step...* <note>original Latin: *Ad omnem progressum atque promotum...*</note>

<summary>This page describes how ascetic sects like the Encratites and Cataphrygians influenced the early Church, leading to the adoption of monasticism, the doctrine of Purgatory, and the use of the sign of the cross as a charm.</summary>
<keywords>Encratites, Gnostics, Tatian, Montanus, Eastern Churches, Western Churches, Cataphrygians, Purgatory, Tertullian, asceticism, monasticism</keywords>

--- Page 218 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the rise of asceticism and ritualism, specifically how the "mystery of iniquity" began with early sects like the Cataphrygians and Gnostics before influencing the wider church.</meta>

<page-num>201</page-num>

<header>Chapter 13. Prophecies of Daniel.</header>

...[at every] step and movement, at every entry and exit, when putting on clothes and shoes, at the baths, at the tables, at the lighting of lamps, at the beds, at the seats—whatever the occasion—we wear away our foreheads with the sign of the cross. <note>original: *sum atque promotum, ad omnem aditum & exitum, ad vestitum, ad calceatum, ad lavacra, ad mensas, ad lumina, ad cubilia, ad sedilia, quacunque nos conversatio exercet, frontem crucis signaculo terimus.* This is a quote from the early Christian author Tertullian, describing the frequent ritual use of the sign of the cross in daily life.</note> The Apostle Paul refers to all these superstitions when he says: *Now the Spirit expressly says that in the latter times some shall depart from the faith, giving heed to seducing spirits and doctrines of demons* <note>original: *Dæmons*; here the author equates these with the spirits and ghosts of the dead</note>—the ghosts worshipped by the pagans—*speaking lies in hypocrisy* about their apparitions, the miracles performed by them, their relics, and the sign of the cross; *having consciences seared with a hot iron, forbidding to marry, and commanding to abstain from certain foods* <note>original: *meats*; in this context, it refers to any kind of food prohibited by religious law</note>, etc. (1 Timothy 4:1–3). These principles and practices were passed down to later generations from the Cataphrygians <note>An early ascetic sect also known as Montanists, originating in Phrygia</note>. *For the mystery of iniquity was already at work* in the days of the Apostles among the Gnostics; it continued to work very strongly in their descendants, the Tatianists and Cataphrygians, and was destined to work until *that man of sin should be revealed.* His *coming is after the working of Satan, with all power and signs and lying wonders, and all the deceptiveness of unrighteousness*; disguised by an outward form of Christian godliness, but lacking the true power of it (2 Thessalonians 2:7–10).

## CHAPTER XIV.

### Of the Mahuzzims, honored by the King who acts according to his own will.

In scripture, we are told of some who *trust in God* and others who *trust in idols*, and that *God is our refuge, our strength, our defense*. In this sense, God is the "rock" of his people, and false gods are called the *rock* of those who *trust in them* (Deuteronomy 32:4, 15, 18, 30, 31, 37). In the same sense, the gods of the King who *shall do according to his will* are called *Mahuzzims* <note>original: "Mahuzzims"; a Hebrew term often translated as "fortresses" or "strongholds," which the author interprets here as guardian saints or protectors.</note>—meaning munitions, fortresses, protectors, guardians, or defenders. <margin>Chapter 11:38, 39.</margin> *In his estate*, says Daniel, *shall he honor Mahuzzims; even with a God whom his fathers knew not, shall he honor them with gold and silver, and with precious stones, and things of value. Thus shall he do in the most strongholds* or temples; *and he shall cause them to rule over many, and divide the land among them for a possession.* This came to pass by degrees in the following manner.

Shortly after that persecution—around the year 314—the Council of Laodicea in Phrygia, which met to restore the lapsed discipline of the Church, issued the following canons:

Therefore, let those who visit them be cursed <term>anathema</term> <note>original: "anathema"; a formal curse by a church authority, excommunicating a person or denouncing a doctrine.</note>.

The Council of Paphlagonia <note>Also known as the Council of Gangra.</note>, held in the year 324, established this canon: *If anyone, out of arrogance, expresses hatred for the congregations of the martyrs, the liturgies performed there, or the commemorations of the martyrs, let him be cursed.* <note>original: "anathema"</note>

During Diocletian's persecution, the faithful used to pray in the cemeteries or burial places of the dead to avoid the danger of persecution and because of the lack of churches, which had all been torn down. After the persecution was over, they continued that practice in honor of the martyrs until new churches could be built. Through habit, they came to see this as beneficial for devotion and for restoring the health of those who were sick.

It also appears that in these burial places they commemorated the martyrs every year on days dedicated to them. They considered all these practices pious and religious, and they cursed <term>anathematized</term> <note>original: "anathematized"; to formally denounce or excommunicate.</note> as arrogant those men who opposed them or who prayed in the *martyries* <note>Shrines or churches built over the graves of martyrs.</note> of the heretics. They also lit torches for the martyrs during the daytime, just as the pagans <note>original: "heathens"</note> did for their gods; this custom became very common in the *West* before the end of the fourth century. 

They sprinkled the worshippers of the martyrs with holy water, as the pagans did for the worshippers of their gods, and went on pilgrimages to see *Jerusalem* and other holy places, as if those locations conferred sanctity on the visitors. From the custom of praying in the cemeteries and martyries came the practice of moving <term>translating</term> <note>original: "translating"; the formal ceremony of moving a saint's body or relics from one location to another.</note> the bodies of the saints and martyrs into the newly built churches. During **Diocletian’s** persecution, Christians used to pray in the **cemeteries** or burying-places of the dead. This was done to avoid the danger of the persecution and because their churches had all been torn down. After the persecution was over, they continued that practice in honor of the martyrs until new churches could be built. Through habit, they came to consider it beneficial for devotion and for restoring the health of those who were sick. 

It also appears that in these burying-places, they commemorated the martyrs every year on days dedicated to them. They accounted all these practices as pious and religious, and they **anathematized** <term>original: "anathematized"; to formally curse or excommunicate someone from the Church</term> those men as arrogant who opposed them, or who prayed in the **shrines** <term>original: "Martyries"; churches or shrines built over the tomb of a martyr</term> of the heretics. 

They also lit torches for the martyrs during the day, just as the heathens did for their gods; this custom became very common in the **West** before the end of the fourth century. They sprinkled the worshipers of the martyrs with holy water, as the heathens did for the worshipers of their gods, and went on pilgrimages to see **Jerusalem** and other holy places, as if those places conferred sanctity on the visitors. 

From the custom of praying in the cemeteries and shrines came the practice of **translating** <note>In this context, "translating" refers to the ritual movement of a saint's physical remains from one location to a new church or shrine.</note> the bodies of the saints and martyrs into such churches as were newly built. The Emperor **Constantius** <note>Constantius II, son of Constantine the Great, reigned 337–361 AD.</note> began this practice around the year 359, by ordering the bodies of **Andrew** the

All of this gave the Emperor *Julian* <note>Julian "the Apostate" (r. 361–363), the last pagan Roman emperor, who attempted to reverse the Christianization of the Empire.</note> an opportunity, as *Cyril* records, to accuse the *Christians* in this manner: *Who can express enough disgust for your practice of adding many new dead men to that ancient dead man, Jesus? You have filled all places with tombs and monuments, even though you are nowhere commanded to bow down before tombs or to show them such formal respect.* <note>original: "respect them officiously"; here, "officiously" means with dutiful or ceremonial attention.</note> 

A little later he says: *Since Jesus said that tombs are full of filthiness, how can you call upon God while standing over them?* In another place, he says that if *Christians* had followed the teachings of the *Hebrews*, they would have worshiped one God instead of many, and they would not worship a man—or rather, many wretched <note>original: "unhappy"</note> men. He also claimed that they worshiped the wood of the cross, marking its image on their foreheads and in front of their houses.

Martyrs were buried in them, and annual festivals were held in their honor, with sacrifices offered to God in their name. The next step toward the invocation of saints <note>The practice of praying to saints to ask for their intercession with God.</note> was attributing a power of working miracles to their dead bodies, bones, and other relics, by means of their departed souls <note>original: "separate souls"; souls believed to exist consciously apart from the body after death.</note>. These souls were believed to know what we do or say, and to be able to do us good or harm, and to perform those miracles. 

This was the exact concept the pagans <note>original: "heathens"</note> held regarding the departed souls of their ancient kings and heroes, whom they worshiped under the names of *Saturn*, *Rhea*, *Jupiter*, *Juno*, *Mars*, *Venus*, *Bacchus*, *Ceres*, *Osiris*, *Isis*, *Apollo*, *Diana*, and the rest of their gods. Because these gods were described as male and female, husband and wife, son and daughter, brother and sister, they are revealed to be nothing more than ancient men and women. 

Now, just as the first step toward the invocation of saints was initiated by the persecution of *Decius* <note>Emperor Decius (r. 249–251 AD), whose empire-wide persecution of Christians led to a surge in the veneration of those who died for the faith.</note>, and the second step by the persecution of *Diocletian* <note>Emperor Diocletian (r. 284–305 AD), who led the "Great Persecution," the last and most severe persecution of Christians in the Roman Empire.</note>, so this third step seems to have been caused by the actions of *Constantius* and *Julian the Apostate*. When *Julian* began to restore the worship of the pagan gods and to speak ill of the saints and martyrs, the *Christians* of *Syria* and *Egypt* seem to have made a great noise about the miracles performed by the relics of the *Christian* saints and martyrs, in opposition to the powers...

The holy blood of the blessed martyrs is received everywhere, while in their presence demons howl, while diseases are driven away, while miraculous works are seen: bodies raised up without ropes, and the clothes of women suspended by their feet do not fall down over their faces <note>A common claim in hagiography that the clothes of female martyrs or possessed women remained miraculously modest even when they were hung upside down.</note>, spirits are burned without fires, and demons confess without the questioner's faith increasing." <note>original: *Sanctus ubique beatorum martyrum sanguis exceptus est, dum in his Dæmones mugiunt, dum ægritudines depelluntur, dum miraculorum opera cernuntur, elevari sine laqueis corpora, & suspensis pede fœminis vestes non defluere in faciem, uri sine ignibus spiritus, confiteri sine interrogantis incremento fidei.*</note>

# Observations upon the Prophecies of Daniel, Part I

The same miraculous powers were attributed by *Julian* <note>Emperor Julian "the Apostate" (r. 361–363 AD), who attempted to revive Roman paganism.</note> and the pagans <note>original: "heathens"</note> to their idols. For *Sozomen* and *Rufinus* <note>Fifth-century Christian historians.</note> tell us that when Julian opened the pagan temples and consulted the Oracle of *Apollo of Daphne* <note>original: "Apollo Daphnæus"</note> in the suburbs of *Antioch*, he pressed for an answer with many sacrifices. The Oracle at length told him that the bones of the Martyr *Babylas*, which were buried there, hindered him from speaking. By this answer, we may understand that some *Christian* had managed to get into the place where the pagan priests used to speak through a pipe while delivering their oracles. 

Before this, *Hilary* <note>Saint Hilary of Poitiers (c. 310–367 AD), a prominent theologian.</note>, in his book against *Constantius* <note>Emperor Constantius II (r. 337–361 AD).</note> written in the last year of that Emperor’s life, makes the following mention of what was then happening in the *East*, where he was stationed: 

"You persecute without making martyrs. We owe more to the cruelty of your predecessors—Nero, Decius, and Maximian <note>Earlier Roman Emperors who violently persecuted Christians.</note>. For through them we conquered the Devil. Everywhere the holy blood of the blessed martyrs has been received, while in their presence demons roar, diseases are driven away, and miraculous works are seen: bodies are raised up without ropes, and when women are suspended by their feet, their garments do not fall down to expose their faces; spirits are burned without physical fires, and they confess their nature without any prompting from the faith of the questioner." <note>original: "Sine martyrio persequeris. Plus crudelitati vestræ Nero, Deci, Maximiane, debemus. Diabolum enim per vos vicimus. Sanctus ubique beatorum martyrum sanguis exceptus est, dum in his Dæmones mugiunt, dum ægritudines depelluntur, dum miraculorum opera cernuntur, elevari sine laqueis corpora, & dispensis pede fœminis vestes non defluere in faciem, uri sine ignibus spiritus, confiteri sine interrogantis incremento fidei."</note>

And *Gregory Nazianzen* <note>A 4th-century Archbishop of Constantinople and theologian.</note>, in his first oration against the Emperor *Julian* then reigning, writes thus: 

"You did not fear the martyrs, to whom illustrious honors and festivals are dedicated, by whom demons are driven out and diseases are cured; of whom..." <note>original: "Martyres non extimuisti quibus præclari honores & festa constituta, à quibus Dæmones propelluntur & morbi curantur; quo-"</note>

# Observations upon the Part I.

...with the multitude flowing in. For indeed, the temples of the holy martyrs display traces and signs of the future judgment, since the demons are truly beaten with whips, and men are tormented and set free. <margin>Letter 27 to Eustochium.</margin>

"Paula saw Samaria: there the prophets Elisha and Obadiah are buried, as well as John the Baptist, where she trembled, overwhelmed by many miracles. For she saw demons roaring with various torments and howling before the tombs of the saints: men barking with the voices of dogs, roaring like lions, hissing like serpents, and bellowing like bulls in the manner of wolves. Others spun their heads and touched the ground with the top of their heads behind their backs; and though women were suspended by their feet, their clothes did not fall down over their faces." <note>original Latin: "Paula vidit Samariam: ibi siti sunt Elisæus & Abdias prophetæ, & Joannes Baptista, ubi multis intremuit consternata miraculis. Nam cernebat variis dæmones rugire cruciatibus, & ante sepulchra sanctorum ululare, homines more luporum vocibus latrare canum, fremere leonum, sibilare serpentum, mugire taurorum, alios rotare caput & post tergum terram vertice tangere, suspensisque pede fœminis vestes non defluere in faciem."</note> This was about the year 384 AD; and Chrysostom <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD), the Archbishop of Constantinople known for his powerful preaching.</note> in his oration on the Egyptian martyrs, seems to identify Egypt as the leader <note>original: "ringleader"</note> in these matters, saying:

<margin>Edition of Fronto Ducaeus, Volume 1.</margin>

"Blessed be God, since martyrs come forth from Egypt—from that Egypt that fought against God and was most insane, and from where impious mouths and blasphemous tongues originated. Martyrs are held in Egypt; not in Egypt only, nor in the bordering and neighboring regions, but EVERYWHERE IN THE WORLD." <note>original Latin: "Benedictus Deus quandoquidem ex Ægypto prodeunt martyres, ex Ægypto illa cum Deo pugnante ac insanissima, & unde impia ora, unde linguæ blasphemæ; ex Ægypto martyres habentur; non in Ægypto tantum, nec in finitima vicinaque regione, sed UBIQUE TERRARUM."</note>

# Chapter 14. Prophecies of Daniel.

RARE. <note>original Latin: "RARUM"</note> And just as in a time of the greatest abundance of grain, when the inhabitants of cities see that the harvest is greater than what the residents require for their own use, they export the surplus to foreign cities. By doing this, they show both their kindness and generosity, and they ensure that, through their own abundance, they can easily obtain the things they lack from others in return. The Egyptians did the same regarding the "athletes of religion" <note>original: "religionis athletas"—a common early Christian metaphor for martyrs who struggled for their faith like athletes in a stadium.</note>. When they saw a great abundance of them by God's kindness among themselves, they did not confine this great gift of God within their own city. Instead, they poured out these treasures of good things into **ALL PARTS OF THE EARTH**. They did this to show their love for their brothers, to honor the common Lord of all, to gain glory for their city among all people, and to declare it to be the **METROPOLIS** of the whole **WORLD**. <note>original Latin: "totiusque terrarum ORBIS esse METROPOLIN declararent"</note>

---

For the bodies of those saints fortify our city more securely than any indestructible <note>original: "adamantino"—referring to adamant, a legendary hard stone or diamond-like material.</note> and impregnable wall. Like high cliffs jutting out on all sides, they do not only repel the attacks of visible enemies we can see with our eyes, but they also overthrow and scatter the snares of invisible demons and all the deceits of the devil.

---

Nor is this possession <note>The possession of the saints' relics.</note> useful to us only against the plots of men or the deceits of demons. If our common Lord should be angry with us because of the multitude of our sins, by placing these bodies before Him as a defense, we will immediately be able to render <note>The page ends here; the catchword "reddere" translates to "to render" or "to make."</note> Him favorable...

...restore it to the city. <note>original Latin: "reddere civitati"</note> This oration was written at *Antioch*, while *Alexandria* was still the metropolis of the *East*—that is, before the year 381, when *Constantinople* became the metropolis. It was a work of some years for the *Egyptians* to have distributed the miracle-working relics of their martyrs over all the world, as they had done before that year. *Egypt* abounded most with the relics of saints and martyrs; the *Egyptians* even kept them embalmed upon beds in their private houses. *Alexandria* was eminent above all other cities for dispersing them, so as to acquire glory among all men and manifest itself to be the *metropolis* of the world. *Antioch* followed the example of *Egypt* in dispersing the relics of the forty martyrs <note>The Forty Martyrs of Sebaste, a group of Roman soldiers who became famous martyrs in the 4th century.</note>; and the examples of *Egypt* and *Syria* were soon followed by the rest of the world.

This oration is not yet published, but *Gerard Vossius* <note>A noted 17th-century Dutch scholar and theologian.</note> saw it in manuscript in the library of Cardinal *Ascanius* in *Italy*, as he says in his commentary upon the oration of *Ephrem the Syrian* <note>original Latin: "Ephræm Syrus"</note> on the same forty martyrs. ### Chapter 14: Prophecies of Daniel

...earth, and thereby acquired glory for their city, and established it in these matters as the metropolis <term>A "metropolis" here refers to the chief city of a region or the leading center of a particular movement.</term> of the whole world, as we have observed from the writings of *Chrysostom* <note>St. John Chrysostom (c. 347–407), an influential Archbishop of Constantinople known for his eloquent preaching.</note>. It may be concluded that before *Alexandria* received the relics of the Forty Martyrs from *Antioch*, it had already begun to send out the relics of its own martyrs to all parts of the world, setting the first example for other cities. This practice, therefore, began in *Egypt* some years before the death of *Athanasius* <note>Athanasius of Alexandria (c. 296–373), a prominent bishop and major figure in early Christian theology.</note>. It began when the miracle-working bones of *John the Baptist* were carried into *Egypt* and hidden in the wall of a church, so that they might be beneficial to future generations. The practice was restrained during the reign of *Julian the Apostate* <note>The Roman Emperor Julian (reigned 361–363), who attempted to suppress Christianity and revive traditional Roman paganism.</note>, but then it spread from *Egypt* throughout the entire Empire—*Alexandria* being the "metropolis of the whole world," according to *Chrysostom*, for promoting this sort of devotion, and *Antioch* and other cities soon following its example.

In spreading these superstitions, the leaders <note>original: "ring-leaders"</note> were the monks, and *Antony* <note>Saint Anthony the Great (c. 251–356), a famous Egyptian monk considered the father of Christian monasticism.</note> was at the head of them. For at the end of the *Life of Antony*, *Athanasius* relates that these were his dying words to his disciples who then attended him:

> "Take care," said *Antony*, "to adhere to *Christ* in the first place, and then to the saints, so that after death they may receive you as friends and acquaintances into the eternal dwellings <note>original: "everlasting Tabernacles"; a biblical reference to heaven from Luke 16:9.</note>. Think upon these things, perceive..."

...these things; and if you have any regard for me, remember me as a father." This instruction, given to the monks by *Antony* at his death in 356 AD <note>original: "A. C. 356"; Anno Christi, or "in the year of Christ."</note>, could not help but inspire their entire community with devotion toward the Saints, viewing it as the direct way to be received by them into eternal dwellings <note>original: "Tabernacles"; a biblical reference to heavenly homes.</note> after death. From this arose the great public attention <note>original: "noise"</note> regarding the miracles performed by the relics <note>original: "reliquies"</note> of the Saints during the reign of *Constantius* <note>Constantius II, Roman Emperor from 337–361.</note>. This also led to the distribution of miracle-working relics throughout the entire Empire; *Alexandria* set the example and was more renowned for this practice than any other city. 

Consequently, in the days of *Julian* <note>Julian the Apostate, who attempted to revive Roman paganism.</note> in 362 AD, *Athanasius*—guided by a prophetic spirit, as *Ruffinus* <note>Tyrannius Rufinus (c. 345–411), a historian and monk.</note> tells us—hid the bones of *John the Baptist* from the pagans <note>original: "Heathens"</note>. He did not bury them in the ground to be forgotten, but placed them within the hollow wall of a church in the presence of witnesses, so that they might be beneficial to future generations. 

From this also arose the practice of the invocation of the Saints—praying to them to perform such miracles, to assist men in their devotions, and to mediate with God. For *Athanasius*, even from his youth, looked upon the deceased Saints and Martyrs as mediators of our prayers. In his letter <note>original: "Epistle"</note> to *Marcellinus*, written during the reign of *Constantine the Great*, he says that the words of the *Psalms* are not to be rearranged or changed in any way, but are to be recited and sung without any artifice, exactly as they are writ-

While Egypt abounded with monks more than any other country, the veneration of the Saints began earlier and spread faster there than in other places. Palladius <note>A historian of early monasticism and Bishop of Helenopolis (c. 363–420 AD).</note>, traveling to Egypt in the year 388 to visit the monasteries and the tombs of Apollonius and other Martyrs of Thebais <note>A region in ancient Egypt named after its capital, Thebes.</note> who had suffered under Maximinus <note>Maximinus II, a Roman Emperor who oversaw the final Great Persecution of Christians.</note>, says of them: *“For all these [martyrs], the Christians built a single shrine, where many miracles are now performed. Such was the grace of this man [Apollonius] that he was heard immediately regarding the things for which he prayed, as the Savior honored him in this way. We also saw him when we prayed at the site of their martyrdom, along with those who were martyred with him; and worshipping God, we paid our respects to their bodies.”* <note>original: "Iis omnibus Christiani fecerunt ædem unam, ubi nunc multæ virtutes peraguntur. Tanta autem fuit viri gratia, ut de iis quæ esset precatus statim exaudiretur, eum sic honorante servatore : quem etiam nos in martyrio precati vidimus, cum iis qui cum ipso fuerunt martyrio affecti ; & Deum adorantes, eorum corpora salutavimus."</note>

Eunapius <note>A Greek sophist and historian (c. 347–414 AD) who remained a dedicated pagan and was critical of Christianity.</note> also—a pagan, yet a reliable witness of the events of his own time—describes how the soldiers handed over the temples of Egypt to the monks, which happened in the year 389. He rails in an insulting manner against the Martyrs, as they were taking the place of the old gods of Egypt: *“Those same soldiers also established monks at Canopus <note>An ancient Egyptian coastal town near Alexandria.</note>, so that in place of the gods who...”* <note>original: "Illi ipsi, milites, Monachos Canobi quoque collocârunt, ut pro Diis qui..."</note>

*...[those who] are perceived by the mind, they worshiped slaves—and wicked ones at that—with divine honors, the minds of men being bound to these rituals and ceremonies. For they displayed the pickled and salted heads of those who, because of a multitude of crimes, had been executed by judges with the ultimate sentence, as if they were Gods. They bent their knees to them, they received them into the number of the Gods, defiling themselves with dust and filth at their graves. Therefore they were called Martyrs, and were considered the ministers, ambassadors, and arbiters of prayers with the Gods; even though they had been unfaithful slaves severely beaten with whips, who carry the scars of their crimes and the traces of their wickedness on their bodies; such are the Gods the earth produces.* <note>original: "animo cernuntur, servos & quidem flagitiosos divinis honoribus percolerent, hominum mentibus ad cultum ceremoniasque obligatis. This passage is by the pagan historian Eunapius, who mockingly describes Christian relic worship in Egypt.</note> Through these instances, we can understand that the invocation of Saints was by this time well-established in Egypt, and that it was already generally accepted and practiced there by the common people.

...assistants <note>original: "coadjutors"</note> of our prayers, most powerful ambassadors to God, etc. By all of this, it is clear that before the year 378, the speeches and sermons regarding the Saints went far beyond simple poetic language. The common people in the East were already generally corrupted by the Monks with the practice of saint-worship.

Gregory Nazianzen <note>A 4th-century Archbishop of Constantinople and one of the "Cappadocian Fathers" central to early Christian theology.</note>, a Monk, wrote in his sixth speech <note>original: "Oration"</note> in 373 A.D. <note>original: "A. C." (Anno Christi), meaning "the year of Christ," equivalent to A.D.</note>, when he was newly appointed Bishop of *Sasima*: *Let us purify ourselves for the Martyrs, or rather for the God of the Martyrs;* and a little later he calls the Martyrs *mediators for achieving heaven or a divine state.* In the same year, at the end of his speech about *Athanasius* <note>Saint Athanasius of Alexandria (c. 296–373), a staunch defender of Trinitarianism against the Arian heresy.</note>, who had recently died, he addressed him with this prayer:

> *Look down upon us favorably, and lead these people as perfect worshippers of the perfect Trinity—which is contemplated and worshiped as the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit. If there is peace, protect me and feed my flock alongside me; but if there is war, bring me home and place me next to yourself and those like you, however great my request may be.*

And at the end of the funeral speech for *Basil* <note>Saint Basil the Great (330–379), Bishop of Caesarea and a close friend of Gregory Nazianzen.</note>, written in 378 A.D., he addresses him in this way:

> *But you, O divine and sacred Head, look down upon us from heaven; and by your prayers either take away that thorn in the flesh <note>A reference to the Apostle Paul’s "thorn in the flesh" mentioned in 2 Corinthians 12:7.</note> which is given...*

### Chap. 14. Prophecies of Daniel.

...given to us by God for our spiritual growth <note>original: "exercise"</note>, or obtain for us the strength to bear it with courage and direct our entire lives toward what is most fitting for us. When we leave this life, receive us there into your heavenly dwellings <note>original: "Tabernacles"</note>, so that by living together and beholding the holy and blessed Trinity more purely and perfectly—of which we now have only an imperfect view—we may reach the fulfillment of our desires and receive the reward for the struggles <note>original: "wars"</note> we have endured or suffered."

In his sermon <note>original: "Oration"</note> regarding ***Cyprian***—not the famous Bishop of *Carthage*, but a Greek martyr—he calls upon him in the same way. He also recounts how a devout young woman named ***Justina*** was protected by calling upon the **Virgin *Mary***, and how miracles were performed through the remains <note>original: "ashes"</note> of ***Cyprian***.

***Gregory of Nyssa*** <note>original: "Gregory Nyssen," a 4th-century theologian and younger brother of Basil the Great.</note>, another prominent monk and bishop, describes in his account of the life of ***Ephrem the Syrian*** <note>original: "Ephræm Syrus," a prolific Syriac-language hymnwriter and theologian.</note> how a certain man returning from a distant country was in great danger because all the roads were blocked by the armies of barbarian nations. However, after calling out to ***Ephrem*** by name and saying, "*Holy Ephrem, help me*," he escaped the danger, cast aside his fear of death, and returned home safely beyond all expectation. At the conclusion of this sermon, ***Gregory*** calls upon ***Ephrem*** in the following manner: 

> *But you, O Ephrem, who now serve at the divine altar and offer sacrifice to the Prince of Life and to the most holy Trinity...*

Observations upon the Part I.

"...raise its head against us, so that heresies may not spring up like thorns in the vineyard, and that weeds <note>original: "tares"</note> which have grown up may not choke the wheat. Let no rock lacking the richness <note>original: "fatness"</note> of true dew stand against us and make the fruitful power of the Word lose its root. But through the power of your prayers and those of your companions—O admirable man and eminent among the Martyrs—may the community <note>original: "common-wealth"</note> of Christians become a field of ripened grain." The same **Gregory of Nyssa** <note>original: "Gregory Nyssen"</note>, in his sermon on the death of **Meletius, Bishop of Antioch**, preached at **Constantinople** that same year, 381 AD <note>original: "A. C. 381"</note>, before the **Bishops of all the East** assembled in the second general Council, spoke this way about **Meletius**: "*The Bridegroom,* he says, *is not taken from us. <margin>In the year 381, Section 41.</margin>
And since **Gregory** preached this before the **Council of Constantinople**, you may conclude from that, says **Baronius** <note>Cesare Baronius (1538–1607), an influential Catholic Cardinal and church historian.</note>, that he was declaring what the entire Council—and therefore the whole Church in those regions—believed: namely, that the saints in heaven offer prayers for us before God.

### Chapter 14: Prophecies of Daniel

**Ephrem the Syrian** <note>original: "Ephræm Syrus," a prolific Syriac-language hymnwriter and theologian (c. 306–373 AD).</note>, another eminent monk who was a contemporary of **Basil** and died in the same year, concludes his *Encomium* <note>a formal speech or piece of writing that highly praises someone</note> or oration about **Basil** (who had then recently died) by calling upon him in this way:

> *Intercede for me, a very miserable man, and recall me by your intercessions, O father. You who are strong, pray for me who am weak; you who are diligent, for me who am negligent; you who are cheerful, for me who am heavy-hearted <note>original: "heavy"</note>; you who are wise, for me who am foolish. You who have stored up a treasure of all virtues, be a guide to me who am empty of every good work.*

In the beginning of his praise of the **Forty Martyrs** <note>A group of Roman soldiers in Cappadocia who became famous martyrs for their Christian faith in 320 AD.</note>, written at the same time, he calls upon them like this:

> *Help me, therefore, O you Saints, with your intercession; and O you beloved, with your holy prayers; so that Christ, by His grace, may direct my tongue to speak, etc.*

And afterwards, mentioning the mother of one of these forty martyrs, he concludes the oration with this prayer:

> *I entreat you, O holy, faithful, and blessed woman, pray for me to the Saints, saying: "Intercede, you who triumph in Christ, for the most lowly <note>original: "little"</note> and miserable Ephrem, so that he may find mercy and be saved by the grace of Christ."*

Again, in his second sermon or oration on the praises of the holy martyrs of Christ, he addresses them this way:

> *We entreat you, most holy martyrs, to intercede with the Lord—*

*Constantinople* was free from these superstitions until *Gregory Nazianzus* <note>original: "Gregory Nazianzen"</note> arrived there in *379 AD* <note>original: "A. C. 379"</note>; but in a few years, it was also inflamed with them.

This practice of sending relics <note>original: "reliques"</note> from place to place to work miracles, and thereby inflaming the devotion of nations toward the dead Saints and their remains, and establishing the religion of invoking their souls, lasted only until the middle of the reign of the Emperor *Theodosius* the Great; for he then prohibited it by the following law <note>original: "Edict"</note>.

Mart."</note> at Constantinople, in the consulship of Honorius..." <note>original: "Humatum corpus, nemo ad alterum locum transferat; nemo Martyrem distrahat, nemo mercetur: Habeant verò in potestate, si quolibet in loco sanctorum est aliquis conditus, pro ejus veneratione, quod Martyrium vocandum sit, addant quod voluerint fabricarum." This law from 386 AD was intended to stop the commercial trade and dismemberment of saints' bodies.</note>

This practice of sending relics from place to place to perform miracles—thereby inflaming the devotion of the nations toward the dead Saints and their relics and establishing the religion of invoking their souls—lasted only until the middle of the reign of the Emperor *Theodosius the Great*. He then prohibited it by the following Edict: 

*No one shall transfer a buried body to another place; no one shall divide a Martyr; no one shall trade them. However, if a saint is buried in any place, people shall have the power to add whatever buildings they wish for the sake of his veneration, in what is called a Martyrium <note>A church or shrine built over the tomb of a martyr; original Latin: "Humatum corpus, nemo ad alterum locum transferat; nemo Martyrem distrahat, nemo mercetur: Habeant verò in potestate, si quolibet in loco sanctorum est aliquis conditus, pro ejus veneratione, quod Martyrium vocandum sit, addant quod voluerint fabricarum."</note>. Given on the fourth day before the Kalends of March [February 26th] at Constantinople, during the reign of Ho-*

<header>Observations upon the Part I.</header>

<margin>Homily 28. During the consulship of the boy Honorius and Euodius, 386 AD <note>original: "norio nob. puero & Euodio Coss. A. C. 386."</note></margin>
After this, they filled the fields and highways with altars erected to Martyrs, which they claimed to discover through dreams and revelations. This led to the creation of the fourteenth Canon of the fifth Council of Carthage in 398 AD. 

<margin>Augustine on Psalm 44.</margin> 
*It was also decided that altars which are set up everywhere in fields or on roads as monuments to Martyrs—but in which no body or relics of Martyrs are proven to be buried—should, if possible, be overturned by the Bishops who preside over those places.* 

<margin>Augustine on Psalm 98.</margin> 
*However, if this is not allowed due to public unrest, the common people should still be warned not to visit those places, so that those who think rightly are not held captive there by any superstition.* 

<margin>Sermon 1 on St. Stephen.</margin> 
*Absolutely no memorial of a Martyr should be accepted as genuine unless the body or some certain relics are there, or if a very reliable tradition identifies it as the site of their home, property, or place of suffering.* 

<margin>Sermon 31 on the Saints.</margin> 
*As for those altars set up everywhere through dreams and the empty, so-called revelations of various people, they should be rejected entirely.* <note>original Latin: "Item placuit, ut altaria, quæ passim... omnimodè reprobentur."</note> 

<margin>Augustine, *The City of God*, book 22, chapter 8.</margin>
These altars were intended for invoking <term>invoking</term><note>The practice of praying to a saint to ask for their intercession with God.</note> the Saints or Martyrs buried (or claimed to be buried) under them. First, they filled the churches in all places with the relics or supposed relics of the Martyrs to facilitate invoking them within the church buildings; then, they filled the fields and highways with altars for invoking 

<meta>This page ends mid-sentence; the next page continues the discussion of the spread of these altars.</meta>

<summary>This page describes the late 4th-century trend of erecting unauthorized altars to martyrs in public spaces based on private "revelations" and the subsequent efforts by the Council of Carthage and St. Augustine to regulate these practices.</summary>

# Chapter 14: Prophecies of Daniel

[...altars to Martyrs were found] everywhere. This new religion was established by monks throughout the *Greek Empire* before the military expedition of the Emperor *Theodosius* against *Eugenius*, and I believe it was even before his previously mentioned edict in 386 AD. <note>original: "A. C. 386." Anno Christi, or Year of Christ.</note>

The same practice of worshiping <term>Mahuzzims</term><note>A Hebrew term from Daniel 11:38 traditionally translated as "strongholds" or "fortresses." Newton interprets these "forces" as guardian saints or protective spirits.</note> quickly spread into the *Western Empire* as well. However, in this prophecy, *Daniel* primarily describes the events occurring among the nations included within the body of his third Beast <note>In Daniel's vision, the third beast (a leopard) is generally interpreted as the Greek or Macedonian Empire.</note>.

# Observations upon the Prophecies of Daniel, Part I.

> ...[It was decreed that] although it might not be pleasing [to the people] for them <note>The "them" refers to the unauthorized altars and shrines mentioned in the context of the Council's decree.</note> to be removed, the common people should still be warned to visit those places in such a way that those who are truly wise are not kept there under the bond of any superstition. Absolutely no memorial of martyrs should be accepted as genuine unless the body or some certain relics are present, or where the site of a residence, property, or martyrdom has been passed down by a most reliable tradition. For those altars which are established everywhere based on dreams and the empty, supposed revelations of various men must be completely rejected. <note>original: "placuerit non fieri, plebi tamen admoneantur... altaria, omnino reprobentur." The Council was attempting to regulate the explosion of "false" martyr shrines that Newton identifies as the fulfillment of Daniel's prophecy regarding the worship of "Mahuzzims" or protectors.</note>

# Observations upon Part II.

<margin>ᵃ *The Proof of the Gospel*, Book 3 <note>original: "Dem. Evang. l. 3."</note></margin>
[John] might himself at that time have made a new publication of it, from which *Irenæus* might imagine it was then but newly written. *Eusebius*, in his *Chronicle* and *Ecclesiastical History*, follows *Irenæus*; but afterwards, in his *Proof of the Gospel* ᵃ, he connects the banishment of *John* to *Patmos* with the deaths of *Peter* and *Paul* <note>Peter and Paul were martyred during the reign of Nero, suggesting an earlier date for John's banishment than the reign of Domitian.</note>. *Tertullian* and *Pseudo-Prochorus* do the same ᵇ, as well as the first author (whoever he was) of that very ancient fable: that *John* was put by *Nero* into a vessel of hot oil, and coming out unhurt, was banished by him to *Patmos*. 
<margin>ᵇ See Pamelius in his notes on Tertullian’s *On Prescription against Heretics*, note 215; and Jerome, Book 1, *Against Jovinianus*, chapter 14, Erasmus edition. <note>original: "Vid. Pamelium in notis ad Tertull. de Præscriptionibus... & Hieron. l. 1. contra Jovinianum..."</note></margin>
Although this story is no more than a fiction, it was founded on a tradition of the early Church that *John* was banished to *Patmos* in the days of *Nero*. *Epiphanius* represents the *Gospel of John* as being written in the time of *Domitian*, and the *Apocalypse* <term>The Book of Revelation</term> even before that of *Nero*. ᶜ *Arethas*, in the beginning of his Commentary, quotes the opinion of *Irenæus* from *Eusebius*, but does not follow it; for he afterwards affirms the *Apocalypse* was written before the destruction of *Jerusalem* <note>Jerusalem was destroyed by the Romans in 70 AD.</note>, and that former commentators had interpreted the sixth seal as referring to that destruction.
<margin>ᶜ Arethas, chapters 18, 19.</margin>

<meta>This page continues the argument for an earlier dating of the Book of Revelation, using a biographical anecdote about the Apostle John to show that his return from exile must have occurred during the reign of Nero rather than Domitian.</meta>

<page-num>237</page-num>
<header>Chap. I. Apocalypse of St. John.</header>

*The Revelation* was given to *John* the *Evangelist* by God on the *Island of Patmos*, where he had been banished by *Nero Caesar*. This is confirmed by a story told by <margin><sup>d</sup> *Ecclesiastical History*, book 3, chapter 23 <note>original: "Hist. Eccl. l. 3. c. 23."</note></margin> <sup>d</sup> *Eusebius*, citing *Clement of Alexandria* and other ancient authors, concerning a youth whom *John*—some time after his return from *Patmos*—entrusted to the care of the *Bishop* of a certain city. *The Bishop* educated, instructed, and eventually baptized him; but then, becoming less vigilant in his care, the young man fell into bad company. He began, step by step, first to live wildly and become corrupt, then to assault and rob those he met at night. At last, he grew so desperate that his companions formed a band of highwaymen and made him their captain. <margin><sup>e</sup> *Chrysostom*, *To the fallen Theodore* <note>original: "Chrysoſt. ad Theodorum lapſum."</note></margin> And, says <sup>e</sup> *Chrysostom*, he remained their captain for a long time. Eventually, *John* returned to that city and, hearing what had happened, rode out to find the thief. When the young man fled out of reverence for his old master, *John* rode after him, called him back, and restored him to the Church. This story spans many years and requires that *John* should have returned from *Patmos* at the death of *Nero* rather than at the death of *Domitian*. This is because there were only two and a half years between the death of *Domitian* and the death of *John*; <margin><sup>f</sup> Jerome, *Commentary on Galatians*, book 3, chapter 6 <note>original: "Hieron. in Epiſt. ad Gal. l. 3. c. 6."</note></margin> and in his old age, *John* was <sup>f</sup> so physically weak that he had to be carried to Church, finally dying when he was over 90 years old.

<summary>The author uses a famous anecdote about the Apostle John redeeming a young bandit captain to argue that John's exile ended during Nero's reign, as the timeline of the story is too long to fit into the brief window between Domitian's death and John's own passing.</summary>
<keywords>Apostle John, Patmos, Nero, Domitian, Eusebius, Clement of Alexandria, Chrysostom, Jerome, Church history, dating of Revelation</keywords>

<header>Observations upon the Part II.</header>

[John was] old, and therefore could not be supposed capable of riding after the thief at that time <note>Referring to the later date of Domitian's reign</note>.

This opinion is further supported by the allusions in the *Apocalypse* to the Temple, the Altar, and the Holy City as if they were still standing; and to the *Gentiles*, who were soon after to trample the Holy City and the outer court under foot. It is also confirmed by the style of the *Apocalypse* itself, which is fuller of *Hebraisms* <term>Hebraisms: linguistic features or idioms from Hebrew or Aramaic appearing in another language, in this case, Greek</term> than his *Gospel*. From this, it may be inferred that it was written when *John* had recently arrived from *Judea*, where he had been accustomed to the *Syriac* tongue <note>The regional Aramaic dialect</note>; and that he did not write his *Gospel* until he had spent a long time interacting with the *Asiatic Greeks*, by which point he had moved away from most of those *Hebraisms*.

It is also confirmed by the many false *Apocalypses*—such as those attributed to *Peter*, *Paul*, *Thomas*, *Stephen*, *Elijah* <note>original: "Elias"</note>, and *Cerinthus*—which were written in imitation of the true one. Just as the many false *Gospels*, false *Acts*, and false *Epistles* were prompted by the existence of true ones, the writing of many false *Apocalypses* and the practice of attributing them to *Apostles* and *Prophets* suggests that there was a true *Apostolic* version in great demand among the first *Christians*. Therefore, this true one may well be supposed to have been written early, so that there would be enough time within the *Apostolic* age for so many false ones to be written afterward and falsely attributed to *Peter*, *Paul*, *Thomas*, and others who had died before *John*. *Caius*,

Gaius, who was a contemporary of *Tertullian*, <margin>g</margin> tells us that *Cerinthus* wrote his *Revelations* as if he were a great Apostle and claimed the visions were shown to him by angels. Cerinthus asserted there would be a *millennium* <note>original: "millennium"; a thousand-year period</note> of physical pleasures in *Jerusalem* after the resurrection; thus, his *Apocalypse* was clearly written to imitate *John's*. And yet Cerinthus lived so early that <margin>h</margin> he opposed the Apostles at *Jerusalem* in or before the first year of the Emperor *Claudius*—which was twenty-six years before the death of *Nero*—and <margin>i</margin> he died before *John*.

These reasons may be enough to determine the date; and yet there is one more reason that thoughtful people might find convincing, though others might not. I will propose it and leave it to everyone’s own judgment. The *Apocalypse* seems to be referenced in the Epistles of *Peter* and the Epistle to the *Hebrews*, and therefore must have been written before them. I believe such references in the Epistle to the *Hebrews* include the discussions about the High Priest in the heavenly Tabernacle, who is both Priest and King, just as *Melchizedek* <note>original: "Melchisedec"</note> was. They also include the passages regarding the *word of God* with the *sharp two-edged sword*, the *sabbath rest* <note>original: "σαββατισμὸς" (sabbatismos)</note> or *thousand-year rest*, the earth whose end is to be burned (presumably by the lake of fire), the judgment and fiery indignation which shall consume the enemies, and the heavenly City which has foundations, whose builder and maker is

He suggests that the Apostles Peter and John fled Judea for Asia Minor at the start of the Roman-Jewish war (c. 66 AD), and that the Epistles of Peter, the Epistle to the Hebrews, and the Book of Revelation were all written shortly thereafter to address the Jewish Christians displaced by the conflict.</meta>

Jews by birth; and therefore, to prevent insurrections, they secured their leaders and banished *John* to *Patmos*. It also seems probable to me that the *Apocalypse* <note>the Book of Revelation</note> was composed there, and that soon after, the Epistle to the *Hebrews* and the Epistles of *Peter* were written to these churches, referring to this prophecy as something that concerned them particularly. For it appears from these Epistles that they were written during times of general affliction and tribulation under the pagans <note>original: "heathens"</note>, and consequently when the Empire made war upon the *Jews*; for until then, the pagans were at peace with the *Christian Jews* as well as with the rest of the nation. The Epistle to the *Hebrews*, since it mentions *Timothy* as being related to those *Hebrews*, must have been written to them after their flight into *Asia* <note>the Roman province in modern-day Turkey</note>, where *Timothy* was Bishop; and consequently, after the war began, since the *Hebrews* in *Judea* were strangers to *Timothy*. *Peter* also seems to call *Rome* "*Babylon*," both regarding the war made upon *Judea* and the approaching captivity—similar to the captivity under old *Babylon*—and regarding the use of that name in the *Apocalypse*. In writing to the "strangers" scattered throughout *Pontus*, *Galatia*, *Cappadocia*, *Asia*, and *Bithynia*, he seems to suggest <note>original: "intimate"</note> that they were the refugees <note>original: "strangers"</note> newly scattered by the *Roman* wars; for those were the only "strangers" there who were under his care.

This account of events aligns best with history when it is properly corrected. For ⁿ Justin and ᵒ Irenaeus say that Simon Magus <note>a sorcerer mentioned in the Book of Acts</note> came to Rome during the reign of Claudius and performed magic tricks there. The writer known as Pseudo-Clement adds that Simon attempted to fly there, but broke his neck through the prayers of Peter. Because of this, ᵖ Eusebius—or rather Jerome, his translator and editor—recorded that Peter came to Rome in the second year of Claudius. 

However, ᵠ Cyril (Bishop of Jerusalem), Philastrius, Sulpitius, Prosper, Maximus of Turin, and Hegesippus the Younger all place this victory of Peter over Simon Magus during the reign of Nero. In fact, the older tradition was that Peter arrived in Rome during the days of this Emperor <note>Nero</note>, as can be seen in ʳ Lactantius. ˢ John Chrysostom tells us that the Apostles remained in Judea for a long time, and that they only went to the Gentiles after being driven out by the Jews. 

This dispersion occurred in the first year of the Jewish War <note>c. 66 AD</note>, when Josephus tells us the Jews began to be rebellious and violent in all places. Everyone agrees that the Apostles were scattered into several regions at the same time. ᵗ Origen has specified the timing, telling us that at the beginning of the Jewish War, the Apostles and disciples of our Lord were scattered into all nations: Thomas went into Parthia <note>modern-day Iran</note>, Andrew into Scythia <note>modern-day Central Eurasia</note>, John into Asia <note>the Roman province in modern-day Turkey</note>, and Peter went first into Asia, where he preached to the refugees <note>original: "dispersion"</note>, and 

thence <note>catchword for the following page</note>

...thence into Italy. <margin>v Eusebius, *Ecclesiastical History*, book 2, chapter 25.</margin> *Dionysius of Corinth* <note>original: "Dionysius Corinthius," a second-century bishop</note> says that *Peter* went from *Asia* by way of *Corinth* to *Rome*, and all ancient writers agree that *Peter* and *Paul* were martyred there at the end of *Nero*'s reign. *Mark* went with *Timothy* to *Rome* (2 *Timothy* 4:11; *Colossians* 4:10). *Silvanus* <note>original: "Sylvanus," a leader in the early church and companion of Paul and Peter</note> was *Paul*'s assistant; and from the companions of *Peter* mentioned in his first Epistle, we may know that he wrote from *Rome*. The ancients generally agree that in this Epistle he used the name *Babylon* to mean *Rome*. His second Epistle was written to the same "dispersed strangers" as the first (2 *Peter* 3:1), and in it he says that *Paul* had written about the same things to them, and also in his other Epistles (verses 15, 16). Now, as there is no Epistle from *Paul* to these strangers besides the one to the *Hebrews*, so in this Epistle (chapter 10, verses 11–12), we find at length all those things which *Peter* had been speaking of and refers to here. This specifically includes *the passing away of the old heavens and earth, and the establishing of an immovable inheritance*, along with an exhortation to grace, because to the wicked, God is *a consuming fire* (Hebrews 12:25, 26, 28, 29).

Having determined the time of writing the *Apocalypse* <note>the Book of Revelation</note>, I do not need to say much about its truthfulness, since it was in such high demand during the early ages that many people endeavored to imitate it by forging other *Apocalypses* under the names of the Apostles.

<summary>Newton argues that the Apostle Peter wrote his epistles from Rome (using "Babylon" as a code name) and synchronizes Peter's writings with Paul's Epistle to the Hebrews to date the Book of Revelation. He notes that the Apocalypse was so highly regarded in the early church that it inspired numerous contemporary forgeries.</summary>

I do not find that *Christ* was called the "Word of God" <note>original: "the word of God"; this refers to the Greek term *Logos*</note> in any book of the New Testament written before the *Apocalypse*; and therefore I am of the opinion that the language was taken from this prophecy, as were also many other phrases in this Gospel. [Prophecy should] not be understood before the last age of the world; and therefore it adds to the credibility of the prophecy that it is not yet understood. The mistake of interpreters has been to predict specific times and events using this prophecy, as if God intended to make them prophets. Through this recklessness, they have not only embarrassed themselves but have also brought the prophecy itself into contempt. God’s intent was quite different. He gave this and the prophecies of the Old Testament not to satisfy human curiosity by enabling people to foreknow things, but so that after they were fulfilled, they might be interpreted by the events themselves. In this way, His own providence <note>the protective care and sovereign guidance of God over the world</note>—rather than the skill of the interpreter—would then be demonstrated to the world. ## CHAPTER 2

### On the relationship between the *Apocalypse* <note>original: "Apocalypse"; the Book of Revelation</note> of *John*, the *Book of the Law of Moses*, and the *worship of God in the Temple*.

The *Apocalypse* of *John* is written in the same style and language as the Prophecies of *Daniel*, and it has the same relationship to them that they have to each other, so that all of them together form a single complete prophecy. Similarly, it consists of two parts: an introductory prophecy and its interpretation.

The prophecy is divided into seven successive parts by the opening of the seven seals of the book that *Daniel* was commanded to seal up; because of this, it is called the *Apocalypse* or *Revelation of Jesus Christ*. The period of the seventh seal is subdivided into eight successive parts: the silence in heaven for half an hour and the successive sounding of seven trumpets. The seventh trumpet signals the battle of the great day of God Almighty, through which *the kingdoms of this world become the kingdoms of the*

<summary>Newton argues that the Book of Revelation and the Book of Daniel constitute a single prophetic system. He describes the structure of Revelation as a series of seven seals and seven trumpets that culminate in the final battle and the establishment of God's kingdom.</summary>

<keywords>Isaac Newton, Apocalypse of John, Book of Daniel, seven seals, seven trumpets, biblical prophecy, Revelation of Jesus Christ</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Isaac Newton's analysis of the Apocalypse (the Book of Revelation). Newton transitions from the structure of the prophecy to its interpretation, framing the book’s visions within the context of Jewish Temple worship and the liturgical calendar, specifically the feasts of the seventh month.</meta>

<page-num>255</page-num>
### Chapter 2. Apocalypse of St. John.

...of the Lord and of his Christ, and those who destroyed the earth are themselves destroyed.

The Interpretation begins with the words, *And the temple of God was opened in heaven, and there was seen in his temple the Ark of his Testament* <note>original: "Ark of his Testament"; the Ark of the Covenant</note>: and it continues to the end of the Prophecy. The Temple is the scene of the visions, and the visions in the Temple relate to the feast of the seventh month: for the feasts of the *Jews* were typical <note>original: "typical"; in this context, meaning they served as "types" or prophetic symbols foreshadowing future events</note> of things to come. The Passover related to the first coming of *Christ*, and the feasts of the seventh month to his second coming. Because his first coming had already occurred before this Prophecy was given, the feasts of the seventh month are the only ones alluded to here.

On the first day of that month, in the morning, the High Priest dressed <note>original: "dressed"; tended to, cleaned, and prepared</note> the lamps. In allusion to this, this Prophecy begins with a vision of one like the *Son of Man* in the High Priest’s garments, appearing, as it were, in the midst of the seven golden candlesticks—or positioned across from the center of them—tending the lamps, which appeared like a rod <note>original: "rod"; a staff or cluster</note> of seven stars in his right hand. This "dressing" of the lamps was performed by sending seven Epistles <note>original: "Epistles"; letters</note> to the Angels or Bishops of the seven Churches of *Asia*, which in the primitive times illuminated the Temple or the Universal Church <note>original: "Church Catholick"; the whole or universal Christian church, rather than the specific Roman Catholic denomination</note>. These Epistles contain ad-

warnings <note>original: "monitions"</note> against the approaching Apostasy, and therefore relate to the times when the Apostasy began to work strongly, and before it prevailed. It began to work in the Apostles' days, and was to continue working until the *man of sin should be revealed* <note>original: "man of sin"; a figure described in 2 Thessalonians 2:3, traditionally interpreted as the Antichrist</note>. It began to work among the disciples of *Simon*, *Menander*, *Carpocrates*, *Cerinthus*, and similar sorts of men who had absorbed the metaphysical philosophy of the *Gentiles* and *Cabalistic Jews* <note>original: "Cabalistical Jews"; followers of Jewish mystical traditions</note>, and were for that reason called *Gnostics*. *John* calls them *Antichrists*, saying that in his days there were many *Antichrists*.

But because these men were condemned by the Apostles and their immediate disciples, they put the Churches in no danger during the opening of the first four seals. The visions at the opening of these seals relate only to the civil affairs of the pagan *Roman* Empire. During that time, the Apostolic traditions prevailed and preserved the Church in its purity; therefore, the affairs of the Church do not begin to be considered in this Prophecy before the opening of the fifth seal. She began then to decline and to need warnings; she is therefore admonished by these letters <note>original: "Epistles"</note> until the Apostasy prevailed and took hold, which occurred at the opening of the seventh seal. The warnings in these seven letters, therefore, relate to the state of the Church in the times of the fifth and sixth seals. At the opening of the fifth seal,

> ...power, and riches, and wisdom, and strength, and honor, and glory, and blessing. And every creature which is in heaven, and on the earth, and under the earth, and such as are in the sea, and all that are in them, I heard saying: "Blessing, honor, glory, and power, be unto him who sits upon the throne, and unto the Lamb forever and ever." And the four living creatures <note>original: "Beasts"</note> said, "Amen." And the twenty-four elders fell down and worshiped him who lives forever and ever.

This was the worship of the early <note>original: "primitive"</note> Christians.

It was the custom for the High Priest, seven days before the fast of the seventh month <note>The fast of the seventh month refers to Yom Kippur, the Day of Atonement</note>, to stay constantly in the Temple and study the book of the Law so that he might be perfect in his knowledge of it for the Day of Atonement <note>original: "expiation"</note>. On that day, the service—which was various and intricate—was to be performed entirely by him. Part of this service involved reading the Law to the people. 

To encourage his studying, the Sanhedrim <note>The supreme judicial council of ancient Israel</note> appointed certain priests to be with him during those seven days in one of his chambers in the Temple. There, they would discuss the Law with him, read it to him, and remind him to read and study it himself. This opening and reading of the Law during those seven days is referenced <note>original: "alluded unto"</note> in the Lamb’s opening of the seals. We are to understand that those seven...

...seven days begin in the evening before each day, because the Jews began their day in the evening; it is also understood that the solemn ceremony of the fast begins on the morning of the seventh day.

The seventh seal was therefore opened on the Day of Atonement <note>original: "expiation"</note>, and then *there was silence in heaven for about half an hour.* <note>Revelation 8:1</note> *And an angel*—representing the High Priest—*stood at the altar, holding a golden censer; and he was given much incense to offer with the prayers of all the saints on the golden altar that was before the throne.* <note>Revelation 8:3</note> 

On ordinary days, the custom was for one of the priests to take fire from the great altar <note>The Great Altar refers to the Altar of Burnt Offering, located in the outer court of the Temple</note> using a silver censer. But on this specific day, the High Priest himself took fire from the great altar using a golden censer. After he came down from the great altar, he received incense from one of the priests who brought it to him and carried it to the golden altar <note>The Golden Altar refers to the Altar of Incense, located inside the Holy Place</note>. While he offered the incense, the people outside prayed in silence; this corresponds to the "silence in heaven for half an hour" mentioned in the prophecy. 

Once the High Priest had placed the incense on the altar, he carried a burning censer of it in his hand into the Most Holy Place <note>The Holy of Holies, the innermost chamber of the Temple containing the Ark of the Covenant</note>. *And the smoke of the incense, together with the prayers of the saints, rose up before God from the angel’s hand.* <note>Revelation 8:4</note>

Furthermore, the seven Angels poured out their vials of wrath, which represent the drink-offerings associated with those sacrifices.

*...the four corners of the earth, holding the four winds of the earth, so that the wind should not blow on the earth, nor on the sea, nor on any tree. And I saw another Angel ascending from the East, having the seal of the living God; and he cried with a loud voice to the four Angels, to whom it was given to harm the earth and the sea, saying, "Do not harm the earth, nor the sea, nor the trees, until we have sealed the servants of our God in their foreheads."*

<margin>Buxtorf in *The Jewish Synagogue* <note>original Latin: "Synagoga Judaica"</note>, chapters 18, 21.</margin>
This sealing refers to a tradition of the *Jews* that on the Day of Atonement <note>original: "expiation"</note>, all the people of *Israel* are sealed into the books of life and death. For the *Jews* explain in their *Talmud* that at the beginning of every new year—the first day of the month of *Tishrei* <note>original: "Tifri"</note>, the seventh month of the sacred year—three books are opened for judgment. These are: the book of life, in which the names of those who are perfectly righteous <note>original: "just"</note> are written; the book of death, in which the names of those who are atheists or very wicked are written; and a third book for those whose judgment is suspended until the Day of Atonement, and whose names are not written in the book of life or death before that day. They call the first ten days of this month the "Days of Repentance" <note>original: "penitential days"</note>; throughout these days, they fast and pray extensively and are very devout, so that on the tenth day their sins may be forgiven <note>original: "remitted"</note> and their names may be written in the book of life.

The same meaning is signified by the two goats, upon whose foreheads the High Priest yearly, on the Day of Atonement, lays two lots <note>A reference to the ritual in Leviticus 16 where lots are cast to decide the fate of two goats</note> inscribed with the words *'For God'* and *'For Azazel'* <note>Azazel is a Hebrew term often associated with the "scapegoat" or a desert demon</note>. The lot "For Azazel," which was sent into the wilderness, represents those who receive the mark and name of the Beast and go into the wilderness with the Great Whore.
